Matrix-weighted estimates beyond Calderón–Zygmund theory
Spyridon Kakaroumpas and Thu Hien Nguyen and Dimitris Vardakis

Abstract
. We investigate matrix-weighted bounds for the sublinear non-kernel operators considered by F. Bernicot, D. Frey, and S. Petermichl. We extend their result to sublinear operators acting upon vector-valued functions. First, we dominate these operators by bilinear convex body sparse forms, adapting a recent general principle due to T. Hytönen. Then we use this domination to derive matrix-weighted bounds, adapting arguments of F. Nazarov, S. Petermichl, S. Treil, and A. Volberg. Our requirements on the weight are formulated in terms of two-exponent matrix Muckenhoupt conditions, which surprisingly exhibit a rich structure that is absent in the scalar case. Consequently, we deduce that our matrix-weighted bounds improve the ones that were recently obtained by A. Laukkarinen. The methods we use are flexible, which allows us to complement our results with a limited range extrapolation theorem for matrix weights, extending the results of P. Auscher and J. M. Martell, as well as M. Bownik and D. Cruz-Uribe.

Additionally, the notation
u\lesssim v
means that there exists a constant
C>0
such that
u\leq Cv
. This constant
C
is just an absolute constant or one depending on parameters that are specified each time or that are understood by the context. Finally, the notation
u\simeq v
means
u\lesssim v
and
v\lesssim u
.

1.
Introduction

A
scalar weight

w
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a locally integrable function
w:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with
w(x)>0
for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. For any weight
w
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
1<p<\infty
we denote
L^{p}(w):=L^{p}(w(x)\,\mathrm{d}x)
. The question of establishing bounds of the form

\lVert Tf\rVert_{L^{p}(w)}\leq C\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(w)},

for
1<p<\infty
and classical operators
T
like Calderón–Zygmund operators and the Hardy–Littlewood maximal function (where
C
is some constant depending on
p
, the weight
w
, the parameters of the ambient space and the operator but
not
the function
f
) has a long history. The field began with the seminal paper of Helson–Szegö
[
HS60
]
on stationary stochastic processes. Subsequent works
[
Muc72
,
MW76
,
HMW73
,
CF74
]
showed that the correct condition on the weight for such bounds to hold is

[w]_{A_{p}}:=\sup_{Q}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}w(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\right)\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}w(x)^{-1/(p-1)}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{p-1}<\infty,

where the supremum is taken over all cubes
Q\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
(with faces parallel to the coordinate hyperplanes). The exact dependence of the constant
C
on
[w]_{A_{p}}
turned out to be significantly harder and was much later completely described
[
Wit00
,
Pet07
,
Pet07a
,
Hyt12
]
. A desire to give a simple proof of the exact dependence led to the invention of the method of
sparse domination
by L. Lerner
[
Ler13
]
which was soon further developed and refined
[
Ler16
,
Lac17
]
and also applied to rough singular integral operators
[
Con+17
,
DHL21
]
. Of particular importance for the present article are the results by F. Bernicot, D. Frey, and S. Petermichl
[
BFP16
]
, which give a bilinear sparse domination principle for very general non-kernel operators under mild regularity conditions. This, in turn, was used to derive sharp weighted bounds for such operators and doubling measure metric spaces in general. Here, we state the two main results from
[
BFP16
]
, and refer the reader to
Section

2
below for the notation and precise definitions.

Theorem A

(
[
BFP16
, Theorem 5.7]
)
.

Let
(M,\mu)
be a doubling measure metric space. Let
1\leq p_{0}<2<q_{0}\leq\infty
, and take
p\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
. Also, let
T:L^{p}(M,\mu)\to L^{p}(M,\mu)
be a bounded sublinear operator satisfying the assumptions in
Section

2
with respect to
p_{0}
and
q_{0}
. Let
{D}
be a dyadic system in
M
as in Subsection
2.2
. Let also
0<\varepsilon<1
. Then, there exists a constant
C=C(T,\mu,p_{0},q_{0},\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu)
and
g\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu)
that are supported on
\lambda Q_{0}
for some cube
Q_{0}\in\nolinebreak{D}
and sufficiently large (depending on
{D}
)
\lambda\geq 2
, there exists an
\varepsilon
-sparse collection
{S}\subseteq{D}
(depending on
T,p_{0},q_{0},f
and
g
) with

\left|\int_{Q_{0}}Tf\cdot g\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right|\leq C\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\textmu(P)\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\lambda P}|f|^{p_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{1/p_{0}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\lambda P}|g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{1/q_{0}^{\prime}}.

Theorem B

(
[
BFP16
, Theorem 6.4]
)
.

Let
(M,\mu)
be a doubling measure metric space. Let
1\leq p_{0}<q_{0}\leq\infty
and
p\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
. Let
{D}
be a dyadic system in
M
as in Subsection
2.2
and let
\lambda\geq 2
. For
\varepsilon\in\nolinebreak(0,1)
, let
{S}\subseteq{D}
be an
\varepsilon
-sparse collection. Let
w
be a weight on
M
. Then, there exists a constant
C=C(\mu,p,p_{0},q_{0},\varepsilon)
such that the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\lambda P}|f|^{p_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{p_{0}}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\lambda P}|g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}^{\prime}}}\leq C([w]_{A_{p/p_{0}}}[w]_{\RH_{(q_{0}/p)^{\prime}}})^{\alpha}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{w}(M,\mu)}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{w^{\prime}}(M,\mu)},

for all
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{w}(M,\mu)
and
g\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}_{w^{\prime}}(M,\mu)
, where
w^{\prime}:=w^{-1/(p-1)}
and

(1.1)

\displaystyle\alpha:=\max\left\{\frac{1}{p-p_{0}},\frac{q_{0}-1}{q_{0}-p}\right\}.

A major tool for extending given or already proven
L^{p_{0}}
-weighted bounds for some fixed
p_{0}
to
L^{p}
-weighted bounds for all
p
in some appropriate range is the method of
extrapolation
, introduced by José L. Rubio de Francia
[
Rub84
]
. Several works followed further developing this method, until J. Duoandikoextea
[
Duo11
]
proved a sharp version of it. We refer to the book
[
CMP10
]
for a history of the subject, as well as detailed proofs of various forms of extrapolation and related results. The original Rubio de Francia Extrapolation assumes an already known
L^{p_{0}}(w)
-bound, for all
A_{p_{0}}
weights
w
, for some fixed
1<p_{0}<\infty
, and yields
L^{p}(w)
-bounds, for all
A_{p}
weights
w
, for all
1<p<\infty
. Of particular importance for the present article is a form of
limited range extrapolation
established by P. Auscher and J. M. Martell
[
AM07
, Theorem 4.9]
(we refer to
Section

6
for the notation):

Theorem C

(
[
AM07
, Theorem 4.9]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a collection of pairs
(f,g)
of measurable functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that neither
f
nor
g
is a.e. equal to
0
. Let
0<p_{0}<q_{0}\leq\infty
. Assume that there exists
p
with
p_{0}\leq p\leq q_{0}
, and
p<\infty
if
q_{0}=\infty
, such that for all
(f,g)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}}
and for all weights
w\in\nolinebreak A_{p/p_{0}}\cap RH_{(q_{0}/p)^{\prime}}
with
\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(w)}<\infty
,

\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(w)}\leq C\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p}(w)}

holds
for some constant
C
depending on
n,p,p_{0},q_{0}
and
w
.

Then, for all
p_{0}<q<q_{0}
, for all
(f,g)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}}
and for all weights
w\in\nolinebreak A_{q/p_{0}}\cap RH_{(q_{0}/q)^{\prime}}
with
\lVert f\rVert_{L^{q}(w)}<\infty
,

\lVert f\rVert_{L^{q}(w)}\leq C\lVert g\rVert_{L^{q}(w)}

holds
for some constant
C
depending on
n,p,q,p_{0},q_{0}
and
w
.

Questions regarding multivariate stationary stochastic processes lead naturally to the investigation of the boundedness of operators on
matrix-weighted
Lebesgue spaces. More precisely, a
d\times d

matrix weight

W
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a locally integrable function
W:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to M_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
taking values in the set of
d\times d
complex matrices such that
W(x)
is a positive definite matrix for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Suppose
T
is a
linear
operator, which acts on a space of scalar-valued functions and goes into a space of scalar-valued functions. In that case, we can consider (abusing notation) the operator
T
acting on a space of
d
-dimensional vector-valued functions by
T(f):=(Tf_{1},\ldots,Tf_{d})
. If we denote
\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(W)}:=\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/p}f(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/p}
for
1<p<\infty
, then the question of matrix-weighted boundedness reads as

\lVert Tf\rVert_{L^{p}(W)}\leq C\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(W)},

where
T
is the vector-valued extension of a linear operator. The correct condition on the weight
W
for the above bound to hold was established by F. Nazarov, S. Treil and A. Volberg over several works
[
NT96
,
TV97
,
Vol97
]
and is the following:

[W]_{A_{p}}:=\sup_{Q}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/p}W(y)^{-1/p}|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{p/p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty,

where the supremum is taken over all cubes
Q\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
(with faces parallel to the coordinate hyperplanes). The class of such weights class was further studied by M. Bownik
[
Bow01
]
, while M. Christ and M. Golderg
[
CG01
,
Gol03
]
studied a Hardy–Littlewood type maximal function for vector-valued functions in the presence of matrix weights.

The question of quantifying matrix-weighted bounds is much harder and deeper. A major breakthrough was achieved by F. Nazarov, S. Petermichl, S. Treil and A. Volberg
[
Naz+17
]
; they proved a
pointwise convex body sparse domination
of Calderón–Zygmund operators and established the bound

\lVert Tf\rVert_{L^{2}(W)}\leq C[W]_{A_{2}}^{3/2}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}(W)},

where the constant
C
does not depend on the weight. These bounds were a bit later generalized to any exponent
1<p<\infty

[
CIM18
]
. Very recently, K. Domelevo, S. Petermichl, S. Treil, and A. Volberg
[
Dom+24a
]
proved that when
T
is the Hilbert transform on
{\mathbb{R}}
, the exponent
3/2
in the above estimate cannot be improved.

Matrix-weight bounds for rough singular integral operators were established in
[
DHL21
]
, while
[
MR22
]
established a
bilinear convex body sparse domination
for such operators. More recently, a quite general principle for deriving a bilinear convex body sparse domination from
scalar
sparse domination was provided by Hytönen
[
Hyt24
]
. Matrix-weighted bounds for such bilinear convex body sparse forms were already present in special cases in
[
DHL21
,
MR22
]
and were obtained in full generality very recently by Laukkarinen
[
Lau23
]
.

An analog of the Rubio de Francia extrapolation for matrix-weighted estimates seemed out of reach for many years, until the breakthrough by M. Bownik and D. Cruz-Uribe
[
BC22
]
, which developed a whole theory of
convex body valued
operators. See further
[
Cru23
]
for an exposition of the results in
[
BC22
]
.

1.1.
Main results

The main goal of this paper is to prove matrix-weighted bounds for the sublinear operators considered in
[
BFP16
]
. Our main result is the following:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
(M,\mu)
be a doubling measure metric space.
1\leq p_{0}<2<q_{0}\leq\infty
and take
p\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
. Let
\mathbf{T}:L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})\to L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(M,\mu)
be a sublinear operator compatible with a bounded sublinear operator
T:L^{p}(M,\mu)\to L^{p}(M,\mu)
satisfying the assumptions in
Section

2
with respect to
p_{0}
and
q_{0}
(see Definitions
4.1
and
4.2
). Set

t:=\frac{p}{p_{0}},\quad s:=\frac{q_{0}}{p},\quad a:=\frac{t^{\prime}p}{t},\quad b:=s^{\prime}p.

Let
W
be a
d\times d
matrix weight on
M
such that

[W^{s^{\prime}}]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}}:=\sup_{B}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B}|W(x)^{1/p}W(x)^{-1/p}|^{a}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\right)^{b/a^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\infty,

where the supremum ranges over all open balls
B\subseteq M
. Then, there exists a constant
K>0
depending only on
T,\mu,p_{0},q_{0},p
and
d
such that

\lVert\mathbf{T}f\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}},W}(M,\mu)}\leq K\cdot([W^{s^{\prime}}]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}})^{\alpha}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)},

for all compactly supported functions
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
, where
W^{\prime}:=W^{-1/(p-1)}
and

\alpha:=\frac{1}{s^{\prime}(p-p_{0})}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}.

We prove
Theorem

1.1
using a duality argument (explained in detail in
4.1.1
) in two steps. First, in
Section

4
we extend
Theorem

A
and prove a bilinear convex body sparse domination for the operators in question:

Theorem 1.2
.

1\leq p_{0}<2<q_{0}\leq\infty
, and fix
p\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
. Let
\mathbf{T}:L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})\to L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(M,\mu)
be a sublinear operator compatible with a bounded sublinear operator
T:L^{p}(M,\mu)\to L^{p}(M,\mu)
satisfying the assumptions in
Section

2
(see Definitions
4.1
and
4.2
). Let
{D}
be a dyadic system in
M
as in
Section

2.2
. Let also
0<\varepsilon<1
. Then, there exists a constant
C=C(T,\mu,p_{0},q_{0},\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
and
g\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
, whose coordinate functions are all supported on
\lambda Q_{0}
for some cube
Q_{0}\in\nolinebreak{D}
and sufficiently large (depending on
{D}
)
\lambda\geq 2
, there exists an
\varepsilon
-sparse collection
{S}\subseteq{D}
(depending on
T,p_{0},q_{0},d,f
and
g
) with

\int_{Q_{0}}|\mathbf{T}f\cdot g|\,\mathrm{d}\mu\leq Cd^{3/2}\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\textmu(P)\Big|\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda P;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\cdot\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(\lambda P;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\Big|.

Then, in
Section

5
we prove matrix-weighted bounds for bilinear convex body sparse forms of the type appearing in
Theorem

1.2
:

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
1\leq p_{0}<q_{0}\leq\infty
and
p\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
. Let
{D}
be a dyadic system in
M
as in Subsection
2.2
and let
\lambda\geq 2
. Let
0<\varepsilon<1
and let
{S}\subseteq{D}
be an
\varepsilon
-sparse collection. Set

t:=\frac{p}{p_{0}},\quad s:=\frac{q_{0}}{p},\quad a:=\frac{p}{t-1},\quad b:=s^{\prime}p.

Let
W
be a
d\times d
matrix weight on
M
such that
[W^{s^{\prime}}]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}}<\infty
. Then, there exists a constant
C=(\mu,d,p_{0},q_{0},p)
such that for all
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)
and all
g\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{\prime}}(M,\mu)
the following estimate holds:

\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)\big|\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda P;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\cdot\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(\lambda P;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\big|\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon}([W^{s^{\prime}}]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}})^{\alpha}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{\prime}}(M,\mu)},

where
W^{\prime}:=W^{-1/(p-1)}
and

\alpha:=\frac{1}{s^{\prime}(p-p_{0})}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}.

In
Section

6
, we look more closely at conditions on the weights like the ones imposed in
Theorem

1.3
in the case of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. In contrast to the scalar case
[
JN91
,
AM07
,
BFP16
]
, the relation between these different conditions in the matrix case turns out to be more complicated.

On the one hand, some of the inclusions that were true in the scalar case continue to hold for matrix weights (we refer to
Section

6
for precise definitions):

Proposition 1.4
.

Let
1<t,s<\infty
. Let
W
be a
d\times d
matrix weight on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then

\max([W]_{A_{t}},[W]_{RH_{t,s}})^{s}\lesssim_{d,t,s}[W^{s}]_{A_{t,st}}\lesssim_{d,t,s}([W]_{A_{t}}[W]_{RH_{t,s}})^{s}.

On the other hand, several two-exponent Muckenhoupt classes, which in the scalar case just coincide, turn out to be quite different for matrix weights:

Proposition 1.5
.

Let
1<p_{i}\leq q_{i}<\infty
,
i=1,2
with
p_{1}\leq p_{2}
and
\frac{q_{1}}{p_{1}^{\prime}}=\frac{q_{2}}{p_{2}^{\prime}}
. Then, one has that

[W]_{A_{p_{1},q_{1}}}\leq[W]_{A_{p_{2},q_{2}}}

for all
d\times d
matrix weights
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. In the case when
p_{1}<p_{2}
, one can find a
2\times 2
matrix weight
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}
with
[W]_{A_{p_{1},q_{1}}}<\infty
and
[W]_{A_{p_{2},q_{2}}}=\infty
.

Our results are slightly stronger; see
Proposition

6.8
and
Proposition

6.9
. It is worth mentioning that our analysis in
Section

6
also covers several endpoint cases.

We note that
Proposition

1.4
and
Proposition

1.5
show that our matrix-weighted bounds in
Theorem

1.3
improve the ones obtained recently in
[
Lau23
]
; we explain this in
Section

6.4
.

Finally, in
Section

7
we prove the following limited range extrapolation theorem for matrix weights, where we refer to the notation and precise definitions:

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a collection of pairs
(F,G)
of measurable
{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
valued functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that neither
F
nor
G
is a.e. equal to
\{0\}
. Let
0<p_{0}<q_{0}\leq\infty
. Assume that for some fixed
p\in\nolinebreak[p_{0},q_{0}]
, there exists an increasing function
K_{p}
such that one of the following holds:

(1)

If
p<q_{0}
, then

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\leq K_{p}([W^{s(p)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(p)}})\lVert G\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)},\quad\text{for all }(F,G)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}}\text{ with }\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}<\infty,

for all
d\times d
matrix weights
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with
[W^{s(p)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(p)}}<\infty
.

(2)

If
p=q_{0}<\infty
, then

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\leq K_{p}([W^{t(p)^{\prime}/t(p)}]_{A_{\infty}})\lVert G\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)},\quad\text{for all }(F,G)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}}\text{ with }\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}<\infty,

for all
d\times d
matrix weights
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with
[W^{t(p)^{\prime}/t(p)}]_{A_{\infty}}<\infty
.

(3)

If
p=q_{0}=\infty
, then

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\leq K_{p}([W^{1/p_{0}}]_{A_{\infty}})\lVert G\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)},\quad\text{for all }(F,G)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}}\text{ with }\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}<\infty,

for all
d\times d
matrix weights
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with
[W^{1/p_{0}}]_{A_{\infty}}<\infty
.

Then, for all
q\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
,

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\leq K_{q}([W^{s(q)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(q)}})\lVert G\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)},\quad\text{for all }(F,G)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}}\text{ with }\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}<\infty,

for all
d\times d
matrix weights
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with
[W^{s(q)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(q)}}<\infty
, where

K_{q}([W^{s(q)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(q)}})=c_{1}K_{p}(c_{2}[W^{s(q)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(q)}}^{\alpha(p,q)}),

the exponent
\alpha(p,q)
is given by (
7.1
), and the constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
depend only on
n,d,p_{0},q_{0},p
and
q
.

2.
Set up and context of the problem

We repeat some notions from
[
BFP16
]
for clarity and the reader’s convenience.

Consider a space
M
, and an exponent
p\in\nolinebreak[1,\infty)
. Take also a subset
E\subseteq M
, and a Borel measure
m
on
E
. Then,
L^{p}(E,m)
is the Lebesgue space of (
m
-a.e. equivalence classes of) measurable functions
f:E\to{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
\int_{E}|f(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}m(x)<\infty
; this space is equipped with the norm

\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(E,m)}=\left(\int_{E}|f|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}m\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

For
p=\infty
and
m(E)\not=0
,
L^{\infty}(E,m)
is the Lebesgue space of (
m
-a.e. equivalence classes of) measurable functions
f:E\to{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak E}|f(x)|<\infty
; this space is equipped with the norm

\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(E,m)}=\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak E}|f(x)|.

Also,
L^{p}_{\textnormal{loc}}(M,m)
(for
1\leq p<\infty
) is the space consisting of all (
m
-a.e. equivalence classes of) measurable functions
f:M\to{\mathbb{C}}
with
\int_{E}|f(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}m(x)<\infty
for any bounded Borel subset
E
of
M
.

For the present text,
p_{0}
,
q_{0}
,
p
, and
q
stand for exponents. Also,
p^{\prime}
(and similarly for all other exponents that might appear) denotes the Hölder conjugate exponent of
p
, that is
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
. We also set
1^{\prime}:=\infty
and
\infty^{\prime}:=1
.

2.1.
Ambient doubling space

Let
(M,d,\mu)
be a locally compact separable metric space equipped with a non-zero Borel measure
\mu
which is finite on compact sets. We mostly refer to this space as
(M,\mu)
.

For all
x\in\nolinebreak M
and all
r>0
, we denote by
B(x,r)\subseteq M
the open ball for the metric
d
with centre
x
and radius
r
. Whenever
B
is an open ball in
M
we might write
r(B)
(instead of simply
r
) for its radius. If
B
is an open ball and
\lambda>0
, we denote by
\lambda B
the ball concentric with
B
of radius
\lambda r
. In the sequel, by “ball” we always mean
open
ball unless otherwise specified.

We assume that
(M,d,\mu)
satisfies the
volume-doubling property
, that is there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mu(B(x,2r))\leq C\mu(B(x,r))\qquad\text{for all\penalty\ }x\in\nolinebreak M\text{ and\penalty\ }r>0.

We denote by
C_{\mu}
the smallest such constant
C
. Obviously,
C_{\mu}\geq 1
. An immediate consequence of the doubling property is that non-empty open sets have a non-zero
\mu
-measure.

From this assumption, we also deduce

(2.1)

\mu(B(x,r))\leq C_{\mu}\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{\nu}\mu(B(x,s))\qquad\text{for all\penalty\ }x\in\nolinebreak M\text{ and\penalty\ }r\geq s>0,

where
\nu:=\log_{2}(C_{\mu})\geq 0
, since
B(x,r)\subseteq B(x,2^{n+1}s)
for
n:=\lfloor\log_{2}(r/s)\rfloor
. As a consequence, it also holds that

\mu(B(x,r))\leq C_{\mu}\left(\frac{d(x,y)+r}{s}\right)^{\nu}\mu(B(y,s)),\qquad\text{for all\penalty\ }x,y\in\nolinebreak M\text{ and\penalty\ }r\geq s>0.

because
B(x,r)\subseteq B(y,d(x,y)+r)
. From the properties above, one can see that balls with a non-empty intersection and comparable radii have comparable measures. In particular,
C_{\mu}>1
, so
\nu>0
, unless
M
consists of a single point, a case we exclude from our considerations. In the rest of the text, we ignore the dependence on the implied constant
C_{\mu}
, unless specified otherwise.

Another important implication of the volume-doubling properties is that any ball of radius
r
can be covered by at most a fixed number (which depends only on the measure
\mu
) of balls with radius
\frac{r}{2}
. This is a consequence of the Vitali covering lemma.

2.2.
Dyadic systems

Here, we set up the basis of the “dyadic analysis” which is very important later on. For more details, one can refer to
[
HK12
]
where the following construction first appeared. A nice exposition can also be found in
[
Per19
]
. We note that our setting has slightly stronger assumptions on the ambient space
(M,d,\mu)
than the ones appearing in
[
HK12
]
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
(M,d,\textmu)
be as in
Section

2.1
. Suppose the constants
0<c_{0}\leq C_{0}<\infty
and
\delta\in\nolinebreak(0,1)
satisfy
12C_{0}\delta\leq c_{0}
. Then, for each
k\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}
there exists a countable set of indices
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
, and for each
i\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
there exists an open set
Q_{i}^{k}\subseteq M
, with the following properties:

(1)

(nestedness)
If
l\geq k
, then either
Q^{l}_{i}\subseteq Q^{k}_{j}
or
Q^{l}_{i}\cap Q^{k}_{j}=\emptyset
(for all
i\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}_{l}
and all
j\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
).

(2)

(covering)
For all
k\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}
, there exists a zero-measure set
N_{k}
(i.e.
\mu(N_{k})=0
) such that

M=\bigsqcup_{i\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}_{k}}Q^{k}_{i}\sqcup N_{k}.

(3)

(inner/outer balls)
For every
k\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}
and
i\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
there exist points
z^{k}_{i}\in\nolinebreak M
such that

B(z^{k}_{i},{\textstyle\frac{c_{0}}{3}}\delta^{k})\subseteq Q^{k}_{i}\subseteq B(z^{k}_{i},2C_{0}\delta^{k}).

(4)

If
l\geq k
and
Q^{l}_{i}\subseteq Q^{k}_{j}
(for
i\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}_{l}
and
j\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
), then
B(z^{l}_{i},2C_{0}\delta^{l})\subseteq B(z^{k}_{j},2C_{0}\delta^{k})
.

Remark 2.2
.

Fix
k\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}
. The points
z^{k}_{j}
mentioned in the above
Theorem

2.1
satisfy two rather important properties:

(2.2)

\displaystyle d(z^{k}_{i},z^{k}_{j})\geq c_{0}\delta^{k}\qquad\text{whenever\penalty\ }i\not=j\text{ for }i,j\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}_{k}

and also

(2.3)

\displaystyle\min_{i\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}_{k}}d(x,z^{k}_{i})<C_{0}\delta^{k}\qquad\text{for all\penalty\ }x\in\nolinebreak M.

The sets
Q^{k}_{i}
are called
dyadic sets
or
dyadic cubes
, and sometimes —when there is no risk of confusion— we call them simply
cubes
. For a fixed
k\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}
, the family
{D}_{k}:=\bigcup_{i\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}_{k}}\{Q^{k}_{i}\}
is called a
generation
(of dyadic sets), and the collection
{D}:=\bigcup_{k\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}}{D}_{k}
of all such families is called a
dyadic system
subject to the parameters
c_{0}
,
C_{0}
, and
\delta
.

The nestedness property implies that there is no partial overlap between generations, and as a consequence, each cube has unique “ancestors” in earlier (i.e. for smaller
k
) generations. In other words, if
Q\in\nolinebreak{D}_{k}
, then there is a unique cube
\hat{Q}\in\nolinebreak{D}_{k-1}
such that
Q\subseteq\hat{Q}
; we call
\hat{Q}
the
father
or
parent
of
Q
, and
Q
the
child
of
\hat{Q}
.
The doubling properties of the measure imply that each cube uniformly has finitely many children.

Moreover, any collection of dyadic sets
{\mathcal{G}}\subseteq{D}
is called a
dyadic collection
or a
dyadic family
.
If
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}
are two non-empty dyadic collections, we say that
{\mathcal{G}}

covers
(or is a
covering
of)
{\mathcal{F}}
whenever
\bigcup_{Q\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}}}Q\subseteq\bigcup_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}P
and no dyadic set in
{\mathcal{G}}
is strictly contained in any dyadic set in
{\mathcal{F}}
. This definition is a variant by Hytönen (see
[
Hyt24
, Proposition 4.6]
) of
[
Naz+17
, Definition 3.1]
.

We denote by
{D}(Q)
the collection of all cubes
Q^{\prime}\in\nolinebreak{D}
contained in
Q
, that is

\textstyle{D}(Q)=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q^{\prime}\in\nolinebreak{D}\\
Q^{\prime}\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}\{Q^{\prime}\}.

Also, if
{S}\subseteq{D}
is any dyadic collection, then by
\ch_{S}(Q)
we denote the
{S}
-children
of
Q\in\nolinebreak{D}
, that is the maximal cubes in
{S}
strictly contained inside
Q
.

Lastly, we need a certain form of “annuli”. For each
Q\in\nolinebreak{{D}}
, there is a
k\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}
for which
Q\in\nolinebreak{D}_{k}
. Set
\ell(Q):=2C_{0}\delta^{k}
.
Theorem

2.1
, then, tells us that

2\ell(Q^{\prime})=2\cdot 2C_{0}\delta^{k+1}\leq\frac{c_{0}}{3}\delta^{k}<\diam{Q}\leq 2\cdot 2C_{0}\delta^{k}=2\ell(Q)

for any
Q^{\prime}\in\nolinebreak{D}_{k+1}
. Next, for any
\lambda>1
, we define the
\lambda
-neighbourhood
of
Q
by

(2.4)

\lambda Q:=\{x\in\nolinebreak M\,:\,d(x,Q)\leq(\lambda-1)\ell(Q)\}=\{x\in\nolinebreak M\,:\,d(x,Q)\leq 2C_{0}(\lambda-1)\delta^{k}\}.

The annuli
S_{k}(Q)
“centred” around
Q
are the sets

(2.5)

S_{0}(Q):=2Q\quad\text{and}\quad S_{k}(Q):=2^{k+1}Q\setminus 2^{k}Q,\text{ for\penalty\ }k\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}.

Later, during the proof of
Theorem

1.2
, we will see that
\lambda
has to be at least
1+\frac{2}{\delta}
. However, a more precise bound would be
\lambda\geq 2^{\lceil\log_{2}(1+\frac{2}{\delta})-1\rceil}
. Whenever
\delta=\frac{2}{2^{m}-1}
for some
m\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 2}
, we get that
\lambda\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\delta}
. This gives a clearer picture of the geometry and the constants that appear later on than the setup in
[
BFP16
]
, where they define
\ell(Q)=\delta^{k}
for the integer
k
which satisfies
\delta^{k+1}\leq\diam Q<\delta^{k}
. In turn, the factor
\lambda
is more precise than simply “
5
” in Bernicot’s et al. Theorem 5.7 and onwards. One can retrieve
5
for
\lambda=1+\frac{2}{\delta}
and
\delta=\frac{1}{2}
.

The following short lemma is not hard to prove. Nevertheless, we include a proof here for the sake of completeness.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
P\in\nolinebreak{D}
be a dyadic set.

(i)

There exists an open ball
B\subseteq M
such that
P\subseteq B
and
\mu(B)\leq c\mu(P)
, where the constant
c>0
depends only on the doubling constant of
\mu
and the parameters of
{D}
.

(ii)

Let
\lambda>1
. Then, there exists an open balls
B,B^{\prime}\subseteq M
such that
B^{\prime}\subseteq P\subseteq\lambda P\subseteq B
and

\mu(B)\leq C_{\mu}\big(\frac{\lambda}{\lambda-1}\big)^{\nu}\mu(\lambda P)\quad\text{and}\quad\mu(\lambda P)\leq C_{\mu}\left(\frac{6C_{0}}{c_{0}}\right)^{\nu}\lambda^{\nu}\mu(B^{\prime}).

In particular,
\mu(\lambda P)\leq c_{\lambda}\mu(P)
for some
c_{\lambda}>0
. Also, if
\lambda\geq 2
, then
\mu(B)\leq C_{\mu}^{2}\mu(\lambda P)
.

Proof.

By the properties of
{D}
, we have that there exist
x\in\nolinebreak M
and
k\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

B\Big(x,\frac{c_{0}}{3}\delta^{k}\Big)\subseteq P\subseteq B(x,2C_{0}\delta^{k}).

(i)

Set
B:=B(x,2C_{0}\delta^{k})
. Then,
P\subseteq B
and we can directly estimate

\mu(P)\geq\mu\Big(B\Big(x,\frac{c_{0}}{3}\delta^{k}\Big)\Big)\geq\frac{1}{C_{\mu}}\left(\frac{c_{0}}{6C_{0}}\right)^{\nu}\mu(B).

(ii)

It is clear that when
\lambda>1

B(x,\frac{c_{0}}{3}\delta^{k})\subseteq P\subseteq B(x,2C_{0}(\lambda-1)\delta^{k})\subseteq\lambda P\subseteq B(x,2C_{0}\delta^{k}+2C_{0}(\lambda-1)\delta^{k}).

Set
B:=B(x,2C_{0}\lambda\delta^{k})
. Then, we have
\lambda P\subseteq B
and

\mu(\lambda P)\geq\mu(B(x,2C_{0}(\lambda-1)\delta^{k}))\geq\frac{1}{C_{\mu}}\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda}\right)^{\nu}\mu(B).

For
\lambda\geq 2
, notice that
\frac{\lambda}{\lambda-1}\leq 2
and recall that
\nu=\log_{2}(C_{\mu})
.

Finally, set
B^{\prime}:=B(x,\frac{c_{0}}{3}\delta^{k})
. Then, we have that
B^{\prime}\subseteq P
and

\mu(\lambda P)\leq\mu(B(x,2C_{0}\lambda\delta^{k}))\leq C_{\mu}\left(\frac{2C_{0}\lambda\delta^{k}}{\frac{c_{0}}{3}\delta^{k}}\right)^{\nu}\mu(B(x,\frac{c_{0}}{3}\delta^{k}))=C_{\mu}\left(\frac{6C_{0}}{c_{0}}\right)^{\nu}\lambda^{\nu}\mu(B^{\prime}).

∎

The maximal operators
{\mathcal{M}}

We denote by
\mathcal{M}
the
uncentered Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator

\mathcal{M}f(x):=\sup_{B\text{ ball}}\langle|f|\rangle_{B}\mathbbm{1}_{B}(x),\quad x\in\nolinebreak M

acting on functions of
L^{1}_{\textnormal{loc}}(M,\mu)
.
For an exponent
p>1
, we denote by
\mathcal{M}_{p}
the
p
-maximal operator,
\mathcal{M}_{p}(f):=[\mathcal{M}(|f|^{p})]^{\frac{1}{p}}
for functions
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{\textnormal{loc}}(M,\mu)
. We recall the well-known bound

(2.6)

\lVert\mathcal{M}\rVert_{L^{q}(M,\mu)\to L^{q}(M,\mu)}\leq(C_{\mu}3^{\nu})^{1/q}q^{\prime},\quad 1<q<\infty.

See, for example,
[
Gra14
, Exercises 1.3.3 and 2.1.1]
for a sketch of a proof in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that works without any changes in any doubling measure metric space.

We also denote by
{\mathcal{M}}^{D}
the corresponding
dyadic maximal operator
by

\mathcal{M}^{D}f(x):=\sup_{Q\in\nolinebreak{D}}\langle|f|\rangle_{Q}\mathbbm{1}_{Q}(x),\quad x\in\nolinebreak M

acting on
L^{1}_{\textnormal{loc}}(M,\mathrm{d}\mu)
, as well as
{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{D}(f):={\mathcal{M}}^{D}(|f|^{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}
. It turns out that for
\mu
-almost all
y\in\nolinebreak M

{\mathcal{M}}^{D}f(y)\lesssim{\mathcal{M}}f(y)\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathcal{M}}^{D}_{p}f(y)\lesssim{\mathcal{M}}_{p}f(y)

where the implicit constants depend only on the doubling measure
\mu
and the exponent
p
. Naturally, all the properties of
{\mathcal{M}}
are inherited by its dyadic version
{\mathcal{M}}^{D}
.

Next, for
Q\in\nolinebreak{D}
and for
1\leq p<2
we define the operator
{\mathcal{M}}_{Q,p}^{*}
as follows: when
x\in\nolinebreak Q

(2.7)

{\mathcal{M}}^{*}_{Q,p}f(x):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\nolinebreak{D}(Q)\\
P\ni x\end{subarray}}\inf_{y\in\nolinebreak Q}{\mathcal{M}}_{p}f(y),

and zero when
x\not\in\nolinebreak Q
. It turns out that
{\mathcal{M}}^{*}_{Q,p}f(x)
is of weak type
(p,p)
and of strong type
(q,q)
for all
q\in\nolinebreak(p,\infty]
(see
[
BFP16
, Section 5A]
).

2.3.
Sparse collections

Here, we introduce the notion of “sparseness”. It must be noted that one can find many different definitions of what a “sparse collection” is in the general bibliography. See, for example,
[
Naz+17
]
for an exposition of the alternative definitions in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. All such definitions are, in fact, equivalent: one can look into
[
Hän18
]
to see the proof of such equivalences (see also
[
LN19
]
for the special case of dyadic systems). Our setting is the following:

Definition 2.4

(Sparse Collection)
.

Let
{S}
be a collection of Borel subsets of
M
of positive and finite
\mu
measure, and consider a number
\varepsilon\in\nolinebreak(0,1)
. The collection
{S}
is called
\varepsilon
-sparse
when for all
Q\in\nolinebreak{S}
there exist pairwise disjoint measurable sets
F_{Q}\subseteq Q
such that

\mu(F_{Q})\geq\varepsilon\mu(Q)\qquad\text{for all\penalty\ }Q\in\nolinebreak{S}.

Later in the text, we work with sparse collections
{S}\subseteq{D}
whose elements are dyadic sets.

2.4.
The operators

In this section, we introduce the main operators of our study along with some important properties.

The unbounded operator
L

For
\theta\in\nolinebreak[0,\pi/2)
, a linear operator
L
with dense domain
{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(L)
in
L^{2}(M,\mu)
is called
\theta
-accretive
if the spectrum
\sigma(L)
of
L
is contained in the closed sector
S_{\theta+}:=\{\zeta\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}:|\arg\zeta|\leq\theta\}\cup\{0\}
, and
Lg\cdot g\in\nolinebreak S_{\theta+}
for all
g\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(L)
.

We assume the existence of an unbounded operator
L
on
L^{2}(M,\mu)
which satisfies the following properties: For any
\theta\in\nolinebreak[0,\pi/2)
,
L
is an injective
\theta
-accretive operator with dense domain
{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(L)
in
L^{2}(M,\mu)
. Also, suppose there exist two exponents
p_{0}
and
q_{0}
with
1\leq p_{0}<2<q_{0}\leq\infty
such that for all balls
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
of radius
\sqrt{t}
(
t>0
)

(2.8)

\lVert e^{-tL}\rVert_{L^{p_{0}}(B_{1})\to L^{q_{0}}(B_{2})}\lesssim|B_{1}|^{-1/p_{0}}|B_{2}|^{1/q_{0}}e^{-cd(B_{1},B_{2})^{2}/t},

where
d(B_{1},B_{2})
is the usual distance between
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
;
d(B_{1},B_{2}):=\inf\{d(x,y)\,:\,{x\in\nolinebreak B_{1},\ y\in\nolinebreak B_{2}}\}
.

From these assumptions,
L
is what is called a
maximal accretive operator
on
L^{2}(M,\mu)
, which implies that
L
has a bounded
H^{\infty}
functional calculus on
L^{2}(M,\mu)
. Note that if
L
is unbounded,
e^{-tL}
cannot be represented in the form of a power series. Since
\theta<\frac{\pi}{2}
,
-L
is a generator of the analytic semigroup
(e^{-tL})_{t>0}
in
L^{2}(M,\mu)
, see
[
ADM96
,
Kat66
]
. Therefore, this semigroup satisfies the so-called
L^{p_{0}}
-
L^{q_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates
, a generalization of the
L^{2}
-
L^{2}
Davies–Gaffney estimates. This kind of off-diagonal estimate is useful in the case when pointwise heat kernel bounds fail.

The sublinear operator
T

Throughout this text, we consider an operator
T
which satisfies the following properties:

(T1)

T
is a well-defined bounded sublinear operator on
L^{2}(M,\mu)
. Here, by “sublinearity” of
T
we understand the following conditions:

\displaystyle|T(f+g)(x)|\leq|Tf(x)|+|Tg(x)|,

\displaystyle|Tf(x)-Tg(x)|\leq|T(f-g)(x)|,

and

\displaystyle|T(af)(x)|=|a|\cdot|Tf(x)|,

for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak M
, for all
f,g\in\nolinebreak L^{2}(M,\mu)
, and for all
a\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}
.
The second condition ensures that if
T
is bounded on the Lebesgue space
L^{p}(M,\mu)
as an operator
T:L^{p}(M,\mu)\to L^{p}(M,\mu)
for some
1<p<\infty
, then
T
is actually
continuous
as a map
T:L^{p}(M,\mu)\to L^{p}(M,\mu)
.

(T2)

There exists
N_{0}\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all integers
N\geq N_{0}
and all balls
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
of radius
\sqrt{t}

\lVert T(tL)^{N}e^{-tL}\rVert_{L^{p_{0}}(B_{1},\mu)\to L^{q_{0}}(B_{2},\mu)}\lesssim|B_{1}|^{-1/p_{0}}|B_{2}|^{1/q_{0}}\left(1+\frac{d(B_{1},,B_{2})^{2}}{t}\right)^{-\frac{\nu+1}{2}}.

(T3)

There exists an exponent
p_{1}\in\nolinebreak[p_{0},2)
such that for all
x\in\nolinebreak M
and
r>0

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B(x,r)}|Te^{-r^{2}L}f|^{q_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}\lesssim\underset{y\in\nolinebreak B(x,r)}{\inf}{\mathcal{M}}_{p_{1}}(Tf)(y)+\underset{y\in\nolinebreak B(x,r)}{\inf}{\mathcal{M}}_{p_{1}}f(y).

The assumptions (T2) and (T3) serve as a substitute for the notion of Calderón–Zygmund operators, see for instance
[
Aus07
]
and
[
Aus+04
]
. Namely, condition (T2) provides the cancellation property for the operator
T
which corresponds to the cancellation of the considered semigroup; this is known as
L^{p_{0}}
-
L^{q_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates
. Condition (T3) is a Cotlar-type inequality, which allows one to get off-diagonal estimates for the low-frequency part of the operator
T
.

In the sequel, we fix an integer
N>\max\left\{\frac{3}{2}\nu+1,N_{0}\right\}
, where
\nu=\log_{2}(C_{\mu})
, and
N_{0}
comes from the assumptions on
T
. Unless specified otherwise, we don’t emphasize the dependence on the implicit constant
N
.

The approximation operators
P_{t}
and
Q_{t}

Further, we introduce two classes of elementary operators;

•

the family
(P_{t})_{t>0}
, which corresponds to an approximation of the identity at scale
\sqrt{t}
, and commutes with the heat semigroup, and

•

the family
(Q_{t})_{t>0}
, which satisfies some extra cancellation condition with respect to
L
.

Definition 2.5
.

Set
c_{N}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{N}e^{-s}\,\mathrm{d}s/s
. For
t>0
, we define

\displaystyle Q_{t}^{(N)}:=c_{N}^{-1}(tL)^{N}e^{-tL}

and

\displaystyle P_{t}^{(N)}:=\int_{1}^{\infty}Q_{st}^{(N)}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}=\Phi_{N}(tL),

where
\Phi_{N}(x):=c_{N}^{-1}\int_{x}^{+\infty}s^{N}e^{-s}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}
for
x\geq 0
.

The operators above are derived from the semigroup
(e^{-tL})_{t>0}
and substitute the Littlewood–Paley operators which are more typical for our circle of questions. We refer to
[
Hof+11
,
AM07a
]
for a detailed discussion of their properties. We mention only some of them below.

Remark 2.6
.

Let
p\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
.

(i)

We have
Q_{t}^{(N)}=(-1)^{N}c_{N}^{-1}t^{N}\partial_{t}^{N}e^{-tL}
and
P_{t}^{(N)}=\psi(tL)e^{-tL}
, where
\psi
is a polynomial of degree
N-1
with
\psi(0)=1
. Moreover, the following identity holds:

t\partial_{t}P_{t}^{(N)}=tL{\Phi^{\prime}_{N}}(tL)=-Q_{t}^{(N)}.

(ii)

By the
L^{p}
-analyticity of the semigroup and (
2.8
), we know that for every
t>0
the operators
P_{t}^{(N)}
and
Q_{t}^{(N)}
satisfy off-diagonal estimates (as in assumption (T2) on
T
) at scale
\sqrt{t}
. For the proof of this result, see, for example,
[
Hof+11
, Proposition 3.1]
.

(iii)

In view of (ii), the operators
P_{t}^{(N)}
and
Q_{t}^{(N)}
are
L^{p}(M,\mu)
-bounded
uniformly in
t>0
, see
[
AM07a
, Theorem 2.3]
.

Proposition 2.7

(Calderón Reproducing Formulae)
.

Let
p\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
. For every
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu)
, the following limits hold in the
L^{p}(M,\mu)
sense:

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0+}P_{t}^{(N)}f=f;

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}P_{t}^{(N)}f=0;

and

\displaystyle f=\int_{0}^{+\infty}Q_{t}^{(N)}f\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}.

In particular, one can write

(2.9)

P_{t}^{(N)}=\id-\int_{0}^{t}Q_{s}^{(N)}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s},

and as a consequence, we have

(2.10)

\id=\int_{0}^{\infty}Q_{t}^{(N)}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}.

Since
T
is sublinear, for any
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu)

eq.

2.10
gives that

(2.11)

|T(f)|\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}|TQ_{t}^{(N)}(f)|\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\quad\mu\text{-a.e. on }M.

The maximal operators associated to
T

Next, we introduce a certain maximal operator related to
T
which comes into play later.

Definition 2.8
.

For
x\in\nolinebreak M
, the
maximal operator
T^{\#}
of
T
, acting on functions
f\in\nolinebreak L_{\textnormal{loc}}^{q_{0}}(M,\mu)
, is given by

T^{\#}f(x):=\sup_{B\ni x}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|T\int_{r(B)^{2}}^{\infty}Q_{t}^{(N)}f\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right|^{q_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}},

where the supremum is taken over all balls
B\subseteq M
containing
x
.

Since
T
is
L^{2}
-bounded, the Calderón reproducing formulae (
Proposition

2.7
), imply the following properties for
T^{\#}
:

(i)

For every function
f\in\nolinebreak L^{2}(M,\mu)
,
|Tf|\leq T^{\#}f

\mu
-almost everywhere on
M
.

(ii)

The sublinear operator
T^{\#}
is of weak type
(p_{0},p_{0})
and is bounded in
L^{p}(M,\mu)
for every
p\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},2]
.

Details and proofs of these properties can be found in
[
BFP16
, Section 4]
.

One can also consider a localized dyadic version of
T^{\#}
. For this, start with a cube
Q\in\nolinebreak{D}
. For
x\in\nolinebreak Q
, we define the operator
T^{\#}_{Q}
as follows:

(2.12)

T^{\#}_{Q}f(x):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\nolinebreak{D}(Q)\\
P\ni x\end{subarray}}\Biggl(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{P}\Bigg|T\int_{\ell(P)^{2}}^{\infty}{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\Bigg|^{q_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Biggr)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}\qquad\text{when\penalty\ }x\in\nolinebreak Q,

and zero when
x\not\in\nolinebreak Q
. It turns out that for
\mu
-almost all
y\in\nolinebreak M
we have that
T^{\#}_{Q}f(y)\lesssim T^{\#}f(y)
, where the implicit constant depends only on the doubling measure
\mu
and the exponent
q_{0}
.

Naturally,
|Tf|\leq T^{\#}_{Q}f

\mu
-almost everywhere for every
f\in\nolinebreak L^{2}(M,\mu)
. All the remaining important properties of
T^{\#}_{Q}
which we need, are inherited from
T^{\#}
. In particular,
T^{\#}_{Q}
is of weak type
(p_{0},p_{0})
and bounded in
L^{p}(M,\mu)
for every
p\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},2]
(see
[
BFP16
, Section 4B]
).

2.5.
Matrix weights

Let
W:M\to M_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
be a measurable function with
W(x)\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{P}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak M
, and
0<p<\infty
. For any measurable function
f:M\to{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
, we denote

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)}=\left(\int_{M}|W(x)^{1/p}f(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Naturally, we identify all functions for which
\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)}=0
into an equivalence class. Under this identification, this quantity defines a norm when
p\in\nolinebreak[1,\infty)
, and therefore, for such
p
, we obtain the normed complex Banach space
(L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)})
consisting of all (
\mu
-a.e. equivalence classes of) measurable functions
f:M\to{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
with
\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)}<\infty
. We note that the dual
(L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu))^{*}
of
L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)
can be naturally identified with
L^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{\prime}}(M,\mu)
under the usual
L^{2}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
-pairing, where
W^{\prime}:=W^{-1/(p-1)}
.

In general, “matrix weighted” spaces as above might behave rather pathologically. However, the class of functions
W
considered in this paper will prevent such issues.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
W:M\to M_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
be a measurable function with
W(x)\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{P}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak M
. We say that
W
is a
d\times d
matrix weight on
M
if
\int_{E}|W(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\infty
for any bounded Borel subset
E
of
M
.

Let
W
be a
d\times d
matix weight on
M
. Then, it is not hard to see that the class of continuous compactly supported functions on
M
is actually dense in
L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)
, for any
1\leq p<\infty
. This observation allows us below to prove estimates only for functions in an appropriate “nice class” and then make use of “standard” approximation arguments to extend these estimates to more general functions.

3.
Preliminaries from convex-set-valued analysis

In this section, we review some notions and facts related to measurable convex-set-valued maps. This places the sparse domination of
Section

4
, as well as the limited range extrapolation of
Section

7
on firm ground.

Our exposition follows closely
[
AF09
]
and
[
BC22
]
. We omit most proofs and instead refer the reader to these two references.

3.1.
Operations with subsets of Euclidean spaces

As we deal with functions whose values are convex subsets of complex Euclidean spaces, we introduce some notation for the elementary operations between such sets.

Let
d
and
n
be positive integers, and let
A
,
B
, and
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
be non-empty subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
. Also, consider arbitrary
\lambda\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}
,
D\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
,
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
and
C\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{M}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
; we denote by
x^{1},\ldots,x^{d}
the coordinates of
x
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
. Then, we have the following definitions:

(a)

The
Minkowski sum
of
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
:

(3.1)

A_{1}+\ldots+A_{n}:=\{a_{1}+\ldots+a_{n}\,:\,a_{i}\in\nolinebreak A_{i}\text{ for all\penalty\ }i=1,\ldots,n\}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{d};

(b)

the
Minkowski (dot) product
of
A
and
B
:

(3.2)

A\cdot B:=\{a\cdot b\,:\,a\in\nolinebreak A,\penalty\ b\in\nolinebreak B\}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}},

where for any
x,y\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
the operation
x\cdot y
stands for the usual Hermitian product of
x,y
, that is
x\cdot y:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x^{i}\overline{y^{i}}
;

(c)

A\cdot x:=A\cdot\{x\}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{d};

(d)

\lambda A:=\{\lambda a\,:\,a\in\nolinebreak A\}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{d};

(e)

DA:=\{da\,:\,d\in\nolinebreak D,\ a\in\nolinebreak A\}=\textstyle\bigcup_{d\in\nolinebreak D}dA\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{d};

(f)

CA:=\{Ca\,:\,a\in\nolinebreak A\}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{d};

(g)

|A|:=\sup\{|a|\,:\,a\in\nolinebreak A\}\in\nolinebreak[0,\infty].

3.2.
Convex subsets of Euclidean spaces

Before talking about maps taking convex sets as values, we first review a few facts about convex sets themselves.

Let
d
be a positive integer and consider the Euclidean space
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
. A subset
K
of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
is said to be
convex
if for all
x,y\in\nolinebreak K
and
t\in\nolinebreak[0,1]
it holds that
tx+(1-t)y\in\nolinebreak K
. Any non-empty intersection of convex subsets is again convex. For any
A\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
, the
convex hull

\mathrm{conv}(A)
of
A
is the smallest convex subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
containing
A
. If
(K_{n})^{\infty}_{n=1}
is an increasing sequence of convex subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
, then
\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{n}
is again a convex subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
.

The convex sets we are interested in exhibit some “symmetry”. Namely, we say that a subset
K
of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
is
complex-symmetric around
0
(or sometimes in the current text simply
symmetric
) if for any
x\in\nolinebreak K
and any
\lambda\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}
with
|\lambda|=1
we have
\lambda x\in\nolinebreak K
. We denote by
{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
the set of all non-empty, convex, closed, and complex-symmetric subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
. Moreover, we denote by
{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
the set of all non-empty, convex, closed, bounded and complex-symmetric subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
. The elements of
{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
are called
convex bodies (around
0
)
. Observe that if
K
is a non-empty, convex, closed, and complex-symmetric subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
, then
0\in\nolinebreak K
, and
\lambda x\in\nolinebreak K
for any
x\in\nolinebreak K
and any
\lambda\in\nolinebreak\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
.

A particular type of convex bodies is so important that it deserves special terminology. We say that a subset
K
of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
is a
complex ellipsoid around
0
, (or sometimes in the current text simply
ellipsoid
) if there exists an invertible matrix
A\in\nolinebreak M_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
with
K=A\overline{\mathbf{B}}
, where
\overline{\mathbf{B}}
is the standard (Euclidean) closed unit ball in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
:
\overline{\mathbf{B}}:=\{x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}\,:\,|x|\leq 1\}
. In this case, the polar decomposition of
A^{*}
yields
K=C\overline{\mathbf{B}}
, where
C=(AA^{*})^{1/2}\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{P}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
.

In many theoretical and practical applications, one can “replace” a convex body with a non-empty interior with an ellipsoid. More precisely, if
K
is a convex body in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
with
0\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{Int}(K)
, then there exists a unique ellipsoid
{\mathcal{E}}_{K}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
satisfying the following two properties:

(1)

{\mathcal{E}}_{K}\subseteq K

(2)

{\mathcal{E}}_{K}
has maximum volume among all ellipsoids
{\mathcal{E}}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
with
{\mathcal{E}}\subseteq K
.

This unique ellipsoid
{\mathcal{E}}_{K}
is called the
John-ellipsoid of
K
. It is well-known that

{\mathcal{E}}_{K}\subseteq K\subseteq\sqrt{d}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{K}.

For proofs and more details on John ellipsoids, we refer to
[
Gol03
,
Naz+17
,
BC22
,
Dom+24
]
.

The following lemma follows immediately from the definitions, the fact that continuous maps preserve compactness, and the observation that if
C\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
is non-empty, compact, and
\lambda C\subseteq C
for all
\lambda\in\nolinebreak\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
, then either
C=\{0\}
or there exists
r>0
with
C=\overline{D}(0,r)
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
K,L\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
. Then,
K+L\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
and
|K+L|\leq|K|+|L|
. Moreover, either
K\cdot L=\{0\}
or there exists
r>0
with
r\leq|K||L|
and such that
K\cdot L=\overline{D}(0,r)
.

3.3.
Measurable convex-set-valued maps

We come to the central notion of this section, the measurability of convex-set-valued maps. Our definitions follow
[
AF09
, Chapter 8]
.

Recall that a
Polish space
is a complete separable metric space. In the rest of this subsection,
X
stands for a fixed Polish space, and a
(\Omega,{\mathcal{A}})
for fixed measurable space. We denote by
{\mathcal{K}}(X)
the set of all closed subsets of
X
and by
{\mathcal{K}}^{*}(X)
the set of all non-empty closed subsets of
X
.

Definition 3.2
.

A map
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}(X)
is said to be
weakly
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable
(or simply
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable
in the current text) if for any open subset
U
of
X
we have

F^{-1}(U):=\{\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega\,:\,F(\omega)\cap U\neq\emptyset\}\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}.

The following lemma will allow us not to worry later about the measurability of maps arising, for example, through pointwise operations between measurable convex body valued maps (such as the Minkowski sum (
3.1
) or the Minkowski dot product) or through pointwise multiplication of measurable convex body valued maps with measurable matrix-valued functions.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
Y,Z
be Polish spaces, and let
F
and
G
be
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable maps
F,G:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}^{*}(X)
, which take compact sets as values. If the map
\varphi:X\times Y\to Z
is continuous, then the map
H:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}^{*}(Z)
given by

H(\omega):=\varphi(F(\omega)\times G(\omega))\qquad\text{for\penalty\ }\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega

is
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable.

Proof.

First of all, observe that
H
indeed takes closed subsets of
Z
as values. Let now
W
be any open subset of
Z
. Then we have

H^{-1}(W)=\{\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega\,:\,\varphi(F(\omega)\times G(\omega))\cap W\neq\emptyset\}=\{\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega\,:\,(F(\omega)\times G(\omega))\cap\varphi^{-1}(W)\neq\emptyset\}.

Observe that
\varphi^{-1}(W)
is an open subset of
X\times Y
, therefore, there exist sequences
(U_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
(V_{n})^{\infty}_{n=1}
of open subsets of
X
and
Y
respectively such that

\varphi^{-1}(W)=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}(U_{n}\times V_{n}).

Thus, we have

H^{-1}(W)=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}(F^{-1}(U_{n})\cap G^{-1}(V_{n}))\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}

concluding the proof.
∎

The following useful alternative characterization of measurability for closed-set-valued maps follows immediately from
Definition

3.2
coupled with the separability of the metric space
X
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}^{*}(X)
be a map. The following statements are equivalent:

(i)

The map
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}^{*}(X)
is
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable.

(ii)

For any
x\in\nolinebreak X
, the function

\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto\dist(x,F(\omega))\in\nolinebreak[0,\infty)

is
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable.

Consequently, we do not have to worry about measurability issues when “comparing” the values of measurable closed-set-valued maps pointwise.

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
F,G:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}^{*}(X)
be
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable maps. Then, we have

\{\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega\,:\,F(\omega)\subseteq G(\omega)\}\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}.

Proof.

Let
(x_{n})^{\infty}_{n=1}
be a sequence of points of
X
with

X=\mathrm{clos}(\{x_{n}\,:\,n=1,2,\ldots\}).

We claim that

(3.3)

\{\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega\,:\,F(\omega)\subseteq G(\omega)\}=\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\{\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega\,:\,\dist(x_{n},F(\omega))\geq\dist(x_{n},G(\omega))\}.

Given
Lemma

3.4
, this is enough to conclude the required result.

The inclusion
\subseteq
in (
3.3
) is clear. For the reverse inclusion
\supseteq
, let
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
such that

\dist(x_{n},F(\omega))\geq\dist(x_{n},G(\omega))\qquad\text{for all\penalty\ }n=1,2,\ldots

Let
x\in\nolinebreak F(\omega)
be arbitrary. Then, one can find a sequence
(y_{m})^{\infty}_{m=1}
of points in
\{x_{n}\,:\,n=1,2,\ldots\}
with
y_{m}\to x
in
X
as
m\to\infty
. In other words,
\lim_{m\to\infty}d(y_{m},x)=0
, where
d
is the metric of
X
. Since
x\in\nolinebreak F(\omega)
, then
\lim_{m\to\infty}\dist(y_{m},F(\omega))=0
. By assumption
\dist(y_{m},G(\omega))\leq\dist(y_{m},F(\omega))
, for all
m=1,2,\ldots
and therefore
\lim_{m\to\infty}\dist(y_{m},G(\omega))=0
. It immediately follows that
\dist(x,G(\omega))=0
. Since
G(\omega)
is a closed subset of
X
,
x\in\nolinebreak G(\omega)
concluding the proof.
∎

To develop a measurability theory for set-valued functions, we need to be able to study them through usual “single-valued” functions that
represent
them.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}^{*}(X)
be a map. A
selection map for
F
is a map
f:\Omega\to X
such that
f(\omega)\in\nolinebreak F(\omega)
for any
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
.

A far-reaching characterization of the measurability of closed-set-valued maps in terms of selection maps was established by C. Castaing and it is also known as the
Castaing representation theorem
.

Theorem 3.7

(
[
AF09
, Theorem 8.3.1]
)
.

Let
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}^{*}(X)
be a map. The following statements are equivalent:

(i)

The map
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}^{*}(X)
is
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable.

(ii)

There exists a sequence
f_{n}:\Omega\to X
(
n=1,2,\ldots
) of
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable selection maps for
F
such that

F(\omega)=\mathrm{clos}(\{f_{n}(\omega)\,:\,n=1,2,\ldots\}),\quad\forall\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega.

Let now
d
be any positive integer. We are particularly interested in
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable maps
F:\Omega\mapsto{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
. For technical reasons, we need the following lemma:

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
be an
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable map.

(1)

The function
|F|:\Omega\to[0,\infty]
, where
|F|(\omega):=|F(\omega)|
for
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
, is
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable.

(2)

Let
\mu
be a measure on
(\Omega,{\mathcal{A}})
such that
F(\omega)
is bounded for
\mu
-a.e.
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
. Then there exists an
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable map
\tilde{F}:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
with
\tilde{F}(\omega)=F(\omega)
for
\mu
-a.e.
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
.

Proof.

(1)

It suffices to observe that

\{\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega\,:\,|F(\omega)|>r\}=F^{-1}(\{v\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}\,:\,|v|>r\})\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}\quad\forall r\in\nolinebreak(0,\infty).

(2)

Set

A:=\{\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega\,:\,F(\omega)\text{ is unbounded}\}.

By part (1), we have that
A\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}
. Thus, by assumption, it holds that
\mu(A)=0
. Consider the map

\tilde{F}:=\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega\setminus A}F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d}).

It is clear that
\tilde{F}
takes values in
{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
, is
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable, and
\tilde{F}(\omega)=F(\omega)
for
\mu
-a.e.
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
.

∎

3.4.
Lebesgue spaces of convex-set-valued maps

In this subsection, we briefly consider Lebesgue spaces of convex body valued functions, which furnish natural domains and/or target spaces for the operators considered in this paper.

First, fix a positive integer
d
as well as a
\sigma
-finite measure space
(\Omega,\mu)
.

For a
\mu
-measurable map
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
, we define

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(\Omega,\mu)}:=\left(\int_{\Omega}|F(\omega)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)\right)^{1/p}\qquad\text{for all\penalty\ }0<p<\infty.

Observe that if
\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(\Omega,\mu)}<\infty
for some
0<p<\infty
, then
F(\omega)
is bounded for
\mu
-a.e.
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
. Naturally, we identify all
\mu
-measurable maps
F,G:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
for which
F(\omega)=G(\omega)
for
\mu
-a.e.
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
, and for each
p\in\nolinebreak(0,\infty)
we define
L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(\Omega,\mu)
to be the set of all (equivalence classes of)
\mu
-measurable maps
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
which satisfy
\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(\Omega,\mu)}<\infty
. As usual, our notation does not distinguish between equivalence classes and representatives.

In the case when
p>1
,
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{L^{p}_{\mathcal{K}}(\Omega,\mu)}
satisfies all the necessary properties (triangle inequality, absolute homogeneity, and positive definiteness) of a norm. Nevertheless, this does
not
define a norm as the space
L^{p}_{\mathcal{K}}(\Omega,\mu)
is not a vector space; there is no additive inverse when the addition is defined through the Minkowski sum. However, this does not create a problem for our work here.

Note that in view of
Lemma

3.8
whenever
F\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(\Omega,\mu)
we can assume without loss of generality that
F(\omega)
is bounded —i.e.
F(\omega)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
— for all
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
.

It is important to mention that a consequence of
[
BC22
, Lemma 3.9]
is that the computation of a Lebesgue “norm” of a convex body valued function reduces to the computation of the same Lebesgue “norm” of an appropriately chosen selection function. For future reference, we record a slightly more general form of
[
BC22
, Lemma 3.9]
:

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
W:\Omega\to\mathrm{P}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
be a
\mu
-measurable function, and
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
a
\mu
-measurable map. Then, there exists a
\mu
-measurable selection function
h:\Omega\to{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
for
F
such that
|W(\omega)h(\omega)|=|W(\omega)F(\omega)|
for
\mu
-a.e.
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
.

In particular, there exists a
\mu
-measurable selection function
h:\Omega\to{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
for
F
such that
|h(\omega)|=|F(\omega)|
for
\mu
-a.e.
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
.

Proof.

Let
\bar{\mu}
be the completion of
\mu
. By
[
BC22
, Lemma 3.9]
coupled with
Lemma

3.3
, there exists a
\bar{\mu}
-measurable selection function
g:\Omega\to{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
for the
\mu
-measurable map
WF:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
with
|W(\omega)F(\omega)|=|g(\omega)|
for all
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
. Then, there exists a
\mu
-measurable function
\tilde{g}:\Omega\to{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
with
\tilde{g}(\omega)=g(\omega)
for
\mu
-a.e.
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
. Let
A
be a
\mu
-measurable subset of
\Omega
such that
\mu(\Omega\setminus A)=0
and
\tilde{g}(\omega)=g(\omega)
for all
\omega\in\nolinebreak A
. Consider the function
h:=\mathbbm{1}_{A}W^{-1}\tilde{g}:\Omega\to{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
.
It is clear that
h:\Omega\to{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
is a
\mu
-measurable selection function for
F
with
|W(\omega)h(\omega)|=|W(\omega)F(\omega)|
for all
\omega\in\nolinebreak A
thus concluding the proof.
∎

Since we are interested in weighted spaces, we also need to establish the weighted versions of the above definitions.
Let
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
and
W:\Omega\to\mathrm{M}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
be
\mu
-measurable maps.
Then, for any
0<p<\infty
, we define the quantity

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}},W}(\Omega,\mu)}:=\left(\int_{\Omega}|W(\omega)^{1/p}F(\omega)|^{1/p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)\right)^{1/p},

and the space
L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}},W}(\Omega,\mu)
of all (equivalence classes of)
\mu
-measurable maps
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
such that
\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}},W}(\Omega,\mu)}<\infty
.
The equivalence classes are defined as above.

The quantity
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}},W}(\Omega,\mu)}
again satisfies all the properties of a norm when
p>1
, but
L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}},W}(\Omega,\mu)
is not a vector space.
Also, just like above, whenever
F\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}},W}(\Omega,\mu)
we can assume without loss of generality that
F(\omega)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
for every
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
.

4.
Bilinear convex body sparse domination

In their paper
[
BFP16
, Theorem 5.7]
, Bernicot, Frey, and Petermichl prove a sparse dual estimate for sublinear operators
T
, which satisfy the assumptions of
Section

2
and act on functions from
L^{p}(M,\mu)
. Here, refining a recent result by Hytönen
[
Hyt24
]
, we extend their result to sublinear operators
\mathbf{T}
which now act upon vector-valued functions
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
and yield as output convex body valued functions
\mathbf{T}f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(M,\mu)
. More specifically, we prove what one can call a
bilinear convex body sparse domination
for sublinear operators.

The number
d
stands for a positive integer. Also,
\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
denotes the closed unit disk in
{\mathbb{C}}
, and
\overline{\mathbf{B}}
the (Euclidean) closed unit ball of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
.

4.1.
Convex body sublinear operators

We start by introducing the class of operators, whose sparse bounds are of interest to us. We need two fundamental definitions.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
1<p<\infty
. A map
\mathbf{T}:L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})\to L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(M,\mu)
is said to be
sublinear
if for all
f,g\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
and for all
a\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}
it holds that

\mathbf{T}(f+g)(x)\subseteq\mathbf{T}f(x)+\mathbf{T}g(x)

and

\mathbf{T}(af)(x)=a\mathbf{T}f(x)=|a|\mathbf{T}f(x)

for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak M
.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
1<p<\infty
. Consider a map
\mathbf{T}:L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})\to L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(M,\mu)
and a map
T:L^{p}(M,\mu)\to L^{p}(M,\mu)
. We say that
\mathbf{T}
is
compatible
with
T
if for any
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu)
and for any
v\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
we have

(4.1)

\mathbf{T}(fv)(x)=Tf(x)\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}v=\{\lambda Tf(x)v\,:\,\lambda\in\nolinebreak\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\}

for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak M
.

In the current text, we are interested in sublinear maps
\mathbf{T}:L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})\to L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(M,\mu)
that are compatible with those operators
T:L^{p}(M,\mu)\to L^{p}(M,\mu)
which satisfy the assumptions of
Section

2
.

4.1.1.
Estimating the operators

Let
1<p<\infty
and let
\mathbf{T}:L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})\to L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(M,\mu)
be a map.
Let
W
be a
d\times d
matrix weight on
M
, such that the function
W^{-1/(p-1)}
is also locally integrable. Suppose we want to show that there exists a finite positive constant
C
, such that

\lVert\mathbf{T}f\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}},W}(M,\mu)}\leq C\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)}

for any compactly supported function
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
. By
Lemma

3.9
, it is enough to prove that for any
\mu
-measurable selection function
h:M\to{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
for
\mathbf{T}f
we have

\lVert h\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)}\leq C\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)}.

Thus, in view of the Riesz representation theorem and by a standard density argument we only have to show that for any
compactly supported
function
g\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
one has

\left|\int_{M}h(x)\cdot g(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right|\leq C\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{\prime}}(M,\mu)},

where
W^{\prime}:=W^{-1/(p-1)}
.

Take
\lambda\geq 1+\frac{2}{\delta}
, where
\delta
is the parameter of
{D}
as in Subsection
2.2
. (The choice of this particular constant
\lambda
is explained in the proof of
Theorem

1.2
below.)
Looking back at the construction of the dyadic sets in Subsection
2.2
(also see
[
HK12
]
), thanks to the doubling properties of
\mu
, we see that there are cubes
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{K}\in\nolinebreak{D}
, where
K
is a large enough positive integer depending only on
{D}
and
\mu
, such that all dilates
\lambda Q_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,K
contain the support of
f
and
g
, and in addition the union
\bigcup_{i=1}^{K}Q_{i}
also contains the support of
f
and
g
(up to a set of zero
\mu
-measure).
Therefore, we see that it suffices to show that whenever
Q_{0}
is a dyadic set in
{D}
such that
\lambda Q_{0}
contains the support of both
f
and
g
, then we have

\int_{Q_{0}}|h(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq C\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{\prime}}(M,\mu)}.

Observe that it is always true that

\int_{Q_{0}}|h(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\int_{Q_{0}}|\mathbf{T}f(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Thus, all we need to show is that

\int_{Q_{0}}|\mathbf{T}f(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq C\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{\prime}}(M,\mu)}.

4.1.2.
Extensions of scalar linear operators

Let us observe that the above setup already covers the case of the usual extensions of
linear
operators on vector-valued functions. Indeed, if
T:L^{p}(M,\mu)\to L^{p}(M,\mu)
is a linear operator, then
\vec{T}:L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})\to L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
is defined as

(4.2)

\vec{T}f:=(Tf_{1},\ldots,Tf_{d})\qquad\text{for all\penalty\ }f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d}).

Now, consider the operator
\mathbf{T}:L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})\to L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(M,\mu)
which is given by

\mathbf{T}f(x):=\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\,\vec{T}f(x)\quad\text{ for }x\in\nolinebreak M\text{ and }f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d}).

It is easy to see that
\mathbf{T}
is sublinear and compatible with
T
.

Moreover,

\lVert\mathbf{T}f\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}},W}(M,\mu)}=\lVert\vec{T}f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)}

for all
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
and for any
d\times d
matrix weight
W
on
M
. Thus, if there exists a finite positive constant
C
such that

\lVert\mathbf{T}f\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}},W}(M,\mu)}\leq C\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)},

for every compactly supported function
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)
, then we deduce

\lVert\vec{T}f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)}\leq C\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)},

for every compactly supported function
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)
. If in addition the original linear operator
T:L^{p}(M,\mu)\to L^{p}(M,\mu)
is bounded, then since
\vec{T}
is itself linear, a standard density argument yields that we can find a bounded linear extension
\widetilde{\vec{T}}:L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)\to L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)
of
\vec{T}
(that is, agreeing with
\vec{T}
on
L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})\cap L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)
) with
\lVert\widetilde{\vec{T}}\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)}\leq C
.

Finally, note that
eq.

4.2
is the standard way to extend a linear operator
T
acting on scalar-valued functions to a linear operator
\vec{T}
acting on vector-valued functions. Typically, —as is the case for the current text as well— the arrow in
\vec{T}
is omitted and “
T
” is used to refer to both
T:L^{p}(M,\mu)\to L^{p}(M,\mu)
or
\vec{T}:L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})\to L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
depending on the function space the operator is acting on.

4.2.
Bootstrapping scalar sparse dual bounds

Bilinear convex body bounds for the operators of interest are achieved through an inductive process. In this subsection, we treat the inductive step of this procedure. We assume that the “base” operator which acts on scalar-valued functions admits a sparse domination principle that relies on an inductive step of an appropriate form. Then, we adapt
[
Hyt24
, Proposition 4.6]
.

4.2.1.
Notation

We begin by introducing some notation and conventions. Let
X,Y
be vector spaces over
{\mathbb{C}}
.

If
x
is an element in
X^{d}
, then we denote by
x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}
its components (i.e.
x_{i}\in\nolinebreak X
for all
i=1,\dots,d
). Moreover, if
v
is a vector in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
, then we denote by
v^{1},\ldots,v^{d}
its coordinates. Also for
v,w\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
, the operation
v\cdot w
stands for the usual Hermitian product of
v
and
w
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
:

v\cdot w=\sum_{i=1}^{d}v^{i}\overline{w^{i}}.

If
T:X\to Y
is a linear map, then —as explained earlier— we extend
T
to a linear map
\vec{T}:X^{d}\to Y^{d}
by setting

\vec{T}(x):=(T(x_{1}),\ldots,T(x_{d}))\qquad\text{for\penalty\ }x\in\nolinebreak X^{d}.

Abusing the notation, we denote
\vec{T}
simply by
T
.

For
x\in\nolinebreak X^{d}
and
u\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
, we follow
[
Hyt24
]
and set

v\bullet x:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}v^{i}x_{i}.

In the case when
X={\mathbb{C}}
,

(4.3)

v\bullet x=\sum_{i=1}^{d}v^{i}x^{i}=v\cdot\bar{x}\qquad\forall v,x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d},

where
\bar{x}=(\overline{x^{1}},\ldots,\overline{x^{d}})
.

For
A\in\nolinebreak M_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
and
x\in\nolinebreak X^{d}
we set

A\bullet x:=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}A_{1j}x_{j},\ldots,\sum_{j=1}^{d}A_{dj}x_{j}\right)\in\nolinebreak X^{d}.

Notice that if
X={\mathbb{C}}
, then we simply have
A\bullet x=Ax
for all
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
and
A\in\nolinebreak M_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
.

4.2.2.
Convex body “averages”

Here, we describe the “building blocks” of our sparse domination principle.

Let
X
be a normed space over
{\mathbb{C}}
, and
X^{*}
its dual space. For all
x\in\nolinebreak X^{d}
, we denote

\llangle x\rrangle_{X}:=\{x^{*}(x):\penalty\ x^{*}\in\nolinebreak X^{*},\ \lVert x^{*}\rVert_{X^{*}}\leq 1\}.

It is clear that
\llangle x\rrangle_{X}
is a complex-symmetric subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
. It is proved in
[
Hyt24
, Lemma 2.3]
that
\llangle x\rrangle_{X}
is a convex body in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
for all
x\in\nolinebreak X^{d}
; the proof is given there only for real normed spaces, but it works verbatim for complex ones. In our application, the role of the Banach space
X
is taken over by the Lebesgue spaces
L^{p}(Q,\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu(Q)})
, where
1\leq p<\infty
and
Q\subseteq M
is some Borel set (with
0<\mu(Q)<\infty
), equipped with the normalized measure
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu(Q)}
; for brevity we write
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.70834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.85004pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.3542pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.14171pt}}\!L^{p}(Q):=L^{p}(Q,\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu(Q)})
. In this case,
X^{d}
can be identified with the normalized Lebesgue space
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.70834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.85004pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.3542pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.14171pt}}\!L^{p}(Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d}):=L^{p}(Q,\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu(Q)};{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
. Naturally, for
f=(f_{1},\dots,f_{d})\in\nolinebreak\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.70834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.85004pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.3542pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.14171pt}}\!L^{p}(Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
we have

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}\varphi f\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\Bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}\varphi f_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu,\dots,\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}\varphi f_{d}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigg)\quad\text{ for any }\varphi\in\nolinebreak\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.70834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.85004pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.3542pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.14171pt}}\!L^{p^{\prime}}(Q),

and under the usual identification of
(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.70834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.85004pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.3542pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.14171pt}}\!L^{p}(Q))^{*}
with
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.70834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.85004pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.3542pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.14171pt}}\!L^{p^{\prime}}(Q)
we have

\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p}(Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}=\Biggl\{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}\varphi f\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,:\,\varphi\in\nolinebreak\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.70834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.85004pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.3542pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.14171pt}}\!L^{p^{\prime}}(Q),\penalty\ \lVert\varphi\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p^{\prime}}(Q)}\leq 1\Biggr\}.

4.2.3.
The bootstrapping principle

Here, we state and prove our bootstrapping principle.
Recall that a (non-empty) family
{\mathcal{G}}\subseteq{D}
covers the (non-empty) family
{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq{D}
when
\bigcup_{Q\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}}}Q\subseteq\bigcup_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}P
, and no dyadic set in
{\mathcal{G}}
is strictly contained in any dyadic set in
{\mathcal{F}}
.

Our goal is to prove the following.

Proposition 4.3
.

Fix a dyadic set
Q\in\nolinebreak{D}
and a number
\lambda>1
. Also, take
\varepsilon\in\nolinebreak(0,1)
and
1\leq r_{1},r_{2}<\infty
with
r_{1}<r_{2}^{\prime}
. For
p\in\nolinebreak[r_{1},r_{2}^{\prime}]
, let
\mathbf{T}:L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})\to L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(M,\mu)
be a sublinear map that is compatible with a map
T:L^{p}(M,\mu)\to L^{p}(M,\mu)
. Assume that there exist linear maps
\Lambda_{P}:L^{p}(M,\mu)\to L^{p}(M,\mu)
indexed by dyadic sets
P\in\nolinebreak{D}(Q)
and constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that the following holds:

For all
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu)
supported on
\lambda Q
, there exists a family
{\mathcal{F}}
of pairwise disjoint dyadic sets in
{D}(Q)
with
\sum_{B\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}}}\mu(B)\leq\varepsilon\mu(Q)
such that for any family
{\mathcal{G}}
of pairwise disjoint dyadic sets in
{D}(Q)
which covers
{\mathcal{F}}
the following estimates hold:

(4.4)

\displaystyle\int_{Q\setminus\cup_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}P}|T(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq C_{1}\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q)}\lVert g\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{2}}(\lambda Q)}

and

(4.5)
