\displaystyle\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}\int_{P}|T((\Lambda_{Q}-\Lambda_{P})f)(x)g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq C_{2}\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q)}\lVert g\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{2}}(\lambda Q)}

for any
g\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu)
supported on
\lambda Q
.

Then, for any
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
supported on
\lambda Q
, there exists a family
{\mathcal{G}}
of pairwise disjoint dyadic sets in
{D}(Q)
with
\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}\mu(P)\leq d\varepsilon\mu(Q)
such that

(4.6)

\int_{Q}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq Cd^{3/2}|\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\cdot\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{2}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}|+\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}\int_{P}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{P}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

for any
g\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
supported on
\lambda Q
, with
C=C_{1}+C_{2}
.

Our proof of
Proposition

4.3
is a refinement of the proof of
[
Hyt24
, Proposition 4.6]
. The following result from
[
Hyt24
]
will play a central role.

Lemma D

(
[
Hyt24
, Lemma 4.1]
)
.

Let
X,Y
be complex normed spaces, and take
x\in\nolinebreak X^{d}
and
y\in\nolinebreak Y^{d}
. Assume that
0\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{Int}\,(\llangle x\rrangle_{X})
. Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the John ellipsoid of
\llangle x\rrangle_{X}
, and consider an invertible matrix
A\in\nolinebreak M_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
such that
A{\mathcal{E}}=\overline{\mathbf{B}}
. Also, let
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
. If we set

x_{i}:=v_{i}\bullet(A\bullet x)\quad\text{and}\quad y_{i}:=v_{i}\bullet((A^{\ast})^{-1}\bullet y)\qquad\text{for\penalty\ }i=1,\ldots,d,

then it holds that

\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lVert x_{i}\rVert_{X}\cdot\lVert y_{i}\rVert_{Y}\leq d^{3/2}|\llangle x\rrangle_{X}\cdot\llangle y\rrangle_{Y}|.

We omit the proof of
Lemma

D
and instead refer the reader to
[
Hyt24
, Lemma 4.1]
. We note that the proof given there concerns the case of normed spaces over the real numbers, but it extends to normed spaces over the complex numbers with only minor modifications.

Proof of
Proposition

4.3
.

Take functions
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
and
g\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
both supported on
\lambda Q
. Consider the convex body
K:=\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q)}
. The case when
f=0

\mu
-a.e. on
\lambda Q
is trivial. Assume now that
f
is not
\mu
-a.e. equal to
0
on
\lambda Q
. Then, we distinguish two cases.

0\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{Int}(K)
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the John ellipsoid of
K
, and let
R:{\mathbb{C}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
be an invertible linear map such that
R{\mathcal{E}}=\overline{\mathbf{B}}
. Pick an orthonormal basis
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}\}
of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
. Then, we can write

Rf=\sum_{i=1}^{d}f_{i}v_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad(R^{*})^{-1}g=\sum_{i=1}^{d}g_{i}v_{i}

for some
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu)
and
g_{1},\ldots,g_{d}\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu)
. For each
i=1,\ldots,d
, let
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
be the subfamily of
{D}(Q)
which is given by the assumptions for the function
f_{i}\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu)
. Consider the dyadic family

{\mathcal{G}}:=\left\{\text{maximal dyadic sets in }\bigcup_{i=1}^{d}{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\right\},

where by “maximal” we mean that we select those sets from
\bigcup_{i=1}^{d}{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
which are not properly contained in any other set of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{d}{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
. Then, observe that
{\mathcal{G}}
consists of disjoint dyadic sets (thanks to dyadic structure) and that it covers
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
for all
i=1,\ldots,d
.

Now, we estimate

\int_{Q}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int_{Q\setminus\cup_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}P}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}\int_{P}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Applying the subadditivity of
\mathbf{T}
, we obtain

\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)\cdot g(x)=\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f-\Lambda_{P}f+\Lambda_{P}f)(x)\cdot g(x)\subseteq\mathbf{T}((\Lambda_{Q}-\Lambda_{P})f)(x)\cdot g(x)+\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{P}f)(x)\cdot g(x)

for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak M
and for every
P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}
. It follows that

\begin{split}\int_{Q}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)&\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\int_{Q\setminus\cup_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}P}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\\
&+\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}\int_{P}|\mathbf{T}((\Lambda_{Q}-\Lambda_{P})f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}\int_{P}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{P}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).\end{split}

This means that it only remains to show that

\begin{split}\int_{Q\setminus\cup_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}P}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}\int_{P}|\mathbf{T}(&(\Lambda_{Q}-\Lambda_{P})f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\\
&\leq Cd^{3/2}|\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\cdot\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{2}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}|.\end{split}

We start with the first summand.
In order to lighten the formulae, let us set
F:=Q\setminus\bigcup_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}P
.
The sublinearity of
\mathbf{T}
and (
4.1
) give us that

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)\cdot g(x)

\displaystyle\subseteq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}T(\Lambda_{Q}f_{i})(x)\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}R^{-1}v_{i}\right)\cdot g(x)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}T(\Lambda_{Q}f_{i})(x)\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}v_{i}\right)\cdot((R^{*})^{-1}g(x))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(T(\Lambda_{Q}f_{i})(x)\overline{g_{i}(x)})\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}

for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak M
. It follows that

\displaystyle\int_{F}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{F}|(T(\Lambda_{Q}f_{i})(x)\overline{g_{i}(x)})\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{F}|T(\Lambda_{Q}f_{i})(x)\overline{g_{i}(x)}|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

For each
i=1,\ldots,d
, assumption (
4.4
) for the functions
f_{i}
and
\overline{g_{i}}
implies that

\int_{F}|T(\Lambda_{Q}f_{i})(x)\overline{g_{i}(x)}|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq C_{1}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q)}\lVert g_{i}\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{2}}(\lambda Q)}.

Thus, using
Lemma

D
we deduce

\displaystyle\int_{Q\setminus\bigcup_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}P}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q)}\lVert g_{i}\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{2}}(\lambda Q)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}d^{3/2}|\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\cdot\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{2}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}|.

Similarly, we obtain

\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}\int_{P}|\mathbf{T}((\Lambda_{Q}-\Lambda_{P})f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq C_{2}d^{3/2}|\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\cdot\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{2}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}|

concluding the proof in this case.

\mathrm{Int}(K)=\emptyset
.

Let
H
be the affine subspace of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
generated by
K
, and observe that
H
is a vector subspace of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
. Let
P:{\mathbb{C}}^{d}\to H
be the orthogonal projection of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
on
H
. Recall that
P
is a Hermitian linear map. Moreover, one can easily see that
f=Pf
; see the second part of the proof of
[
Hyt24
, Proposition 4.2]
.

Set
m:=\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}H>0
(
m
is positive, because
f
is not the zero function), and let
S:H\to{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
be an invertible linear map with
S(v)\cdot S(w)=v\cdot w
for all
v,w\in\nolinebreak H
. Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the John ellipsoid of the convex body
S(K)
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
and let
R:{\mathbb{C}}^{m}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
be an invertible linear map such that
R{\mathcal{E}}=\overline{\mathbf{B}}_{m}
, where
\overline{\mathbf{B}}_{m}
is the Euclidean closed unit ball of
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
. Pick an orthonormal basis
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\}
of
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
. Let us write

RSf=RSPf=\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}v_{i},\quad(R^{\ast})^{-1}SPg=\sum_{i=1}^{m}g_{i}v_{i},

for some
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu)
and
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu)
. For each
i=1,\ldots,m
, let
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
be the subfamily of
{D}(Q)
offered by the assumptions for the function
f_{i}\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu)
. Let

{\mathcal{G}}:=\left\{\text{maximal dyadic sets in }\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\right\}.

Observe that
{\mathcal{G}}
covers
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
, for all
i=1,\ldots,m
. Then, similarly to Case 1, we only have to show that

(4.7)

\begin{split}\int_{Q\setminus\cup_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}P}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}\int_{P}|\mathbf{T}(&(\Lambda_{Q}-\Lambda_{P})f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\\
&\leq Cd^{3/2}|\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{2}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}|.\end{split}

First of all, set
F:=Q\setminus\bigcup_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}}}P
. Then, by the sublinearity of
\mathbf{T}
and (
4.1
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)\cdot g(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\subseteq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}T(\Lambda_{Q}f_{i})(x)\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}S^{-1}R^{-1}v_{i}\right)\cdot g(x)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}T(\Lambda_{Q}f_{i})(x)\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}PS^{-1}R^{-1}v_{i}\right)\cdot g(x)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}T(\Lambda_{Q}f_{i})(x)\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}v_{i}\right)\cdot(R^{*})^{-1}SPg(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(T(\Lambda_{Q}f_{i})(x)\overline{g_{i}(x)})\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}.

The estimates proceed then as in case 1 with
d
replaced by
m
,
f
by
Sf
and
g
by
SPg
, so that at the end we obtain

\begin{split}\int_{Q\setminus\bigcup_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}P}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q}f)(x)\cdot g(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)|+\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}\int_{P}|\mathbf{T}(&(\Lambda_{Q}-\Lambda_{P})f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\\
&\leq Cm^{3/2}|\llangle Sf\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\cdot\llangle SPg\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{2}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}|.\end{split}

Finally, we observe that

\displaystyle\llangle Sf\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\cdot\llangle SPg\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{2}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}

\displaystyle=(S\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})})\cdot(SP\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{2}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})})

\displaystyle=(P\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})})\cdot\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{2}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{1}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\cdot\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{r_{2}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})},

concluding the proof.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Examining the previous proof, we see that in the assumptions of
Proposition

4.3
we can restrict the covering collections
{\mathcal{G}}
to the ones which additionally satisfy
\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}}\mu(P)\leq d\varepsilon\mu(Q)
.

4.3.
Applying the bootstrapping principle

Here we accomplish the extension of
[
BFP16
, Theorem 5.7]
to vector-valued functions, with the help of
Proposition

4.3
.

The set up is as follows. Recall that
1\leq p_{0}<2<q_{0}\leq\infty
, and fix
p\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
. Let
\mathbf{T}:L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})\to L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(M,\mu)
be a sublinear operator compatible with a bounded sublinear operator
T:L^{p}(M,\mu)\to L^{p}(M,\mu)
satisfying the assumptions in
Section

2
(see Definitions
4.1
and
4.2
).

Theorem

1.2
.

Let
p\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
. Let
{D}
be a dyadic system in
M
as in
Section

2.2
. Let also
0<\varepsilon<1
. Then, there exists a constant
C=C(T,\mu,p_{0},q_{0},\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
and
g\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
, whose coordinate functions are all supported on
\lambda Q_{0}
for some cube
Q_{0}\in\nolinebreak{D}
and sufficiently large (depending on
{D}
)
\lambda\geq 2
, there exists an
\varepsilon
-sparse collection
{S}\subseteq{D}
(depending on
T,p_{0},q_{0},d,f
and
g
) with

\int_{Q_{0}}|\mathbf{T}f\cdot g|\,\mathrm{d}\mu\leq Cd^{3/2}\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\textmu(P)\Big|\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda P;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\cdot\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(\lambda P;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\Big|.

The proof of this theorem follows very closely the original proof for scalar-valued functions up to the point where one needs to check that the assumptions of
Proposition

4.3
hold. Here, we present the important definitions and briefly mention all necessary properties, but the reader would have to confer
[
BFP16
]
for more details. In addition, as we explained in
Section

2.2
, the parameter
\lambda
(
5
in the original proof of
[
BFP16
, Theorem 5.7]
) is made here more precise.

Sketch of proof.

For
p\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
and
\lambda\geq 2
(to be fixed later), consider functions
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu)
and
g\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu)
supported on
\lambda Q_{0}
, where
Q_{0}\in\nolinebreak{D}
.

Now, let
\eta>0
be some large enough constant (also to be fixed later) and define the following set:

E:=E_{\eta,\lambda}:=\left\{x\in\nolinebreak Q_{0}\,:\,\max\{{\mathcal{M}}^{*}_{Q_{0},p_{0}}f(x),T^{\#}_{Q_{0}}f(x)\}>\eta\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{0})}\right\}.

(Remember the definitions (
2.7
) and (
2.12
).)

For large enough
\eta>0
, the set
E
is properly contained inside
Q_{0}
, and
\mu(E)\leq\frac{K}{\eta}\mu(Q_{0})
(for some
K>0
). In fact,
E
is a union of dyadic cubes, therefore
E
is open (as dyadic sets are open themselves). Hence, one can find a “maximal” covering of
E
by dyadic sets
\{B_{j}\}_{j}\subseteq{D}(Q_{0})
, which are disjoint up a to a set of
\mu
-measure zero; readily this means that

•

E=\bigsqcup_{j}B_{j}
(up to a null set), and

•

\hat{B}_{j}\cap E^{c}\not=\emptyset
for all
j
. (Recall
\hat{B}_{j}
is the parent of
B_{j}
.)

In particular,
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mu(B_{j})=\mu(E)\leq\frac{K}{\eta}\mu(Q_{0})
.

Next, for any
P\in\nolinebreak{D}(Q_{0})
we define the linear operators
\Lambda_{P}
:

\displaystyle\Lambda_{P}f:=\begin{cases}\int^{\ell(P)^{2}}_{0}{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda P})\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}&\text{when}\penalty\ P\not=Q_{0},\\
f&\text{when}\penalty\ P=Q_{0}.\end{cases}

In Step 1 of their proof, Bernicot et al. show that

(4.8)

\Biggl|\int_{Q_{0}}Tf\cdot g\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Biggr|\leq C\eta\mu(Q_{0})\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{0})}\lVert g\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(\lambda Q_{0})}+\Biggl|\sum_{j}\int_{B_{j}}T_{B_{j}}f\cdot g\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Biggr|.

This is attained by noticing that each
\hat{B}_{j}
is contained in
Q_{0}
, contains points which lie inside
Q_{0}
but outside
E
, and for
\mu
-almost every such
x\in\nolinebreak Q_{0}\setminus E
the quantities
{\mathcal{M}}^{*}_{Q_{0},p_{0}}f(x)
and
T^{\#}_{Q_{0}}f(x)
are sufficiently small. The rest comes down to a series of several precise decompositions and approximations.

Here, (
4.8
) is similar to
eq.

4.6
for
Q=Q_{0}
.
Our aim is to show that the conditions of the assumption in
Proposition

4.3
hold. Then, the sum on the left-hand side will enter an iterative process and go to zero.

Towards this goal, let
\{P_{i}\}_{i}\subseteq{D}(Q_{0})
be a dyadic collection, which is comprised of disjoint dyadic sets, and covers
\{B_{j}\}_{j}
, that is, the
P_{i}
’s are not strictly contained in any of the
B_{j}
’s, and
\bigcup_{j}B_{j}\subseteq\bigcup_{i}P_{i}
.
In fact, we can additionally assume that
\sum_{i}\mu(P_{i})\leq\frac{dK}{\eta}\mu(Q_{0})
; however, this is will not be necessary.
Moreover, the
P_{i}
’s satisfy that
E\subseteq\bigsqcup_{i}P_{i}
(up to a
\mu
-null set) and
\hat{P}_{i}\cap E^{c}\not=\emptyset
, “almost like” the collection of the
B_{j}
’s.

Naturally, our proof is very close to the one for Theorem 5.7 in
[
BFP16
]
, but this is unavoidable. For this reason and to keep things contained, we omit the parts which apply verbatim in our case.

For starters, there might exist only one such
P_{i}
, namely
Q_{0}
itself. In this case, both (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) hold trivially as the left-hand side would be zero, and thus (
4.6
) also holds.

From now on, we assume that all
P_{i}
’s are properly contained in
Q_{0}
. Thanks to the dyadic structure, this means that
\hat{P}_{i}\subseteq Q_{0}
. And since
\hat{P}_{i}\cap E^{c}\not=\emptyset
, the definitions of
E
,
M_{Q_{0},p_{0}}^{*}
and
T_{Q_{0}}^{\#}
imply that

(4.9)

\displaystyle\inf_{y\in\nolinebreak\hat{P}_{i}}{\mathcal{M}}_{p_{0}}f(y)\leq\eta\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{0})}

and

(4.10)

\displaystyle\Biggl(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\hat{P}_{i}}\Bigg|T\int_{\ell(\hat{P}_{i})^{2}}^{\infty}{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\Bigg|^{q_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Biggr)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}\leq\eta\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{0})}.

For the first condition of
Proposition

4.3
, we can apply Hölder’s inequality which, combined with the fact that
|Tf(x)|\leq T^{\#}_{Q_{0}}f(x)\leq\eta\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{0})}
for almost every
x\in\nolinebreak Q_{0}\setminus E
, gives us

\int_{Q_{0}\setminus\cup_{i}P_{i}}|Tf\cdot g|\,\mathrm{d}\mu\leq\eta\mu(Q_{0})\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{0})}\lVert g\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(Q_{0})}\lesssim\eta\mu(Q_{0})\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{0})}\lVert g\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(\lambda Q_{0})}.

This is exactly
eq.

4.4
.

The verification of the second condition is much trickier. Like above, Hölder’s inequality gives us that

(4.11)

\sum_{i}\int_{P_{i}}|T(f-\Lambda_{P_{i}}f)\cdot g|\,\mathrm{d}\mu\leq\sum_{i}\mu(P_{i})\Bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{P_{i}}|T(f-\Lambda_{P_{i}}f)|^{q_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}\Bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{P_{i}}|g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}^{\prime}}}.

Further, one needs to bound the
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.70834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.85004pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.3542pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.14171pt}}\!L^{q_{0}}(P_{i})
norm of
T(f-\Lambda_{P_{i}}f)
. We do not present all the details in our paper, as this was already done in
[
BFP16
]
. Nonetheless, we explain what we think is important and necessary for the proof to work for the larger (than
B_{j}
) dyadic sets
P_{i}
.

Start by fixing an index
i\geq 1
, and recall the definition
(T1)
of sublinearity for
T
.
Then, we have

(4.12)

\displaystyle|T(f-\Lambda_{P_{i}}f)|

\displaystyle=\Big|T\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}-\int_{0}^{\ell(P_{i})^{2}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda P_{i}})\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|T\left(\int_{0}^{\ell(P_{i})^{2}}\!{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda P_{i}}+f\mathbbm{1}_{(\lambda P_{i})^{c}})\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}+\int_{\ell(P_{i})^{2}}^{\infty}\!{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}-\int_{0}^{\ell(P_{i})^{2}}\!{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda P_{i}})\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)\!\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|T\int_{0}^{\ell(P_{i})^{2}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f\mathbbm{1}_{(\lambda P_{i})^{c}})\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\Big|+\Big|T\int_{\ell(P_{i})^{2}}^{\infty}{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\Big|=:|{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(P_{i};\lambda)|+|{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(P_{i})|.

Here, we used Calderón reproducing formula (
Proposition

2.7
), and the linearity of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}
.

For the
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.70834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.85004pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.3542pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.14171pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(P_{i})
norms of
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(P_{i};\lambda)
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(P_{i})
, fix
k_{0}\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and take
\lambda
to be at least
2^{k_{0}}
so that for any
Q\in\nolinebreak{D}

(\lambda Q)^{c}\subseteq\bigcup_{k\geq k_{0}}S_{k}(Q).

After a series of geometrical observations and computations, Bernicot et al. show that for
k_{0}=2
and any
j=1,2,\dots

(4.13)

\displaystyle\lVert{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(B_{j};\lambda)\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}}(B_{j})}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{j}}\Big|T\int_{0}^{\ell(B_{j})^{2}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f\mathbbm{1}_{(\lambda B_{j})^{c}})\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\Big|^{q_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\ell(B_{j})^{2}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{j}}|T{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f\mathbbm{1}_{(\lambda B_{j})^{c}})|^{q_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle\lesssim\dots\lesssim\sum_{k\geq k_{0}}2^{-k}\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(S_{k}(B_{j}))}\lesssim\sup_{k\geq k_{0}}\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(2^{k+1}B_{j})},

where the implicit constants depend only on
p_{0}
,
q_{0}
, the doubling measure
\mu
, and the parameters
c_{0}
,
C_{0}
, and
\delta
of the dyadic system. (The first inequality comes from the sublinearity (
2.11
) of the operator
T
followed by Minkowski’s inequality.) In fact, their proof holds true for any
k_{0}\geq 0
and for any
Q\in\nolinebreak{D}
in place of the
B_{j}
’s; in particular

\lVert{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(P_{i};\lambda)\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}}(P_{i})}\lesssim\sup_{k\geq k_{0}}\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(2^{k+1}P_{i})}.

Notice, now, that if
\hat{P}_{i}\subseteq 2^{k+1}P_{i}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
, then

\sup_{k\geq k_{0}}\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(2^{k+1}P_{i})}\lesssim\inf_{y\in\nolinebreak\hat{P}_{i}}{\mathcal{M}}_{p_{0}}f(y)

thanks to
Lemma

2.3
. The smallest
k_{0}
for which this can be true (recall the definition (
2.4
)) is

k_{0}=\lceil\log_{2}\big(1+\frac{2}{\delta}\big)-1\rceil.

Consequently, we can take
\lambda\geq 1+\frac{2}{\delta}
. (We note that in the case of the “usual” dyadic cubes in
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
, where
\delta=\frac{1}{2}
, one can simply take
\lambda=5
.)

Using (
4.9
), we eventually get

(4.14)

\lVert{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(P_{i};\lambda)\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}}(P_{i})}\lesssim\eta\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{0})}.

For
{\mathcal{T}}_{2}
, the sublinearity of
T
gives us that

|{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(P_{i})|\leq\Big|T\int_{\ell(\hat{P}_{i})^{2}}^{\infty}{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\Big|+\Big|T\int_{\ell(P_{i})^{2}}^{\ell(\hat{P}_{i})^{2}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\Big|.

Taking the
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.70834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.85004pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.3542pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.14171pt}}\!L^{q_{0}}
-norm over
P_{i}
, we get

\displaystyle\lVert{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(P_{i})\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}}(P_{i})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\hat{P}_{i}}\Big|T\int_{\ell(\hat{P}_{i})^{2}}^{\infty}{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\Big|^{q_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}+\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\hat{P}_{i}}\Big|T\int_{\ell(P_{i})^{2}}^{\ell(\hat{P}_{i})^{2}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\Big|^{q_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq\eta\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{0})}+\int_{\ell(P_{i})^{2}}^{\ell(\hat{P}_{i})^{2}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\hat{P}_{i}}|T{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f)|^{q_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}.

The first part of the second inequality comes from (
4.10
); the second from Minkowski’s inequality and the sublinearity of
T
.

At this point, the same argument used in (
4.13
) works verbatim for our second summand above, since

\int_{\ell(P_{i})^{2}}^{\ell(\hat{P}_{i})^{2}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\hat{P}_{i}}|T{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f)|^{q_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\leq\int_{0}^{\ell(\hat{P}_{i})^{2}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\hat{P}_{i}}|T{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f)|^{q_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}.

The difference is that the absence of the
\mathbbm{1}_{(\lambda\hat{P}_{i})^{c}}
term forces the sum, and in turn the supremum, to start from
k_{0}=0
:

\int_{\ell(P_{i})^{2}}^{\ell(\hat{P}_{i})^{2}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\hat{P}_{i}}|T{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}^{(N)}(f)|^{q_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\lesssim\sup_{k\geq 0}\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(2^{k+1}\hat{P}_{i})}.

But now,
\hat{P}_{i}\subseteq 2^{k+1}\hat{P}_{i}
is true for all
k\geq 0
, and therefore through
Lemma

2.3
again one gets

\sup_{k\geq 0}\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(2^{k+1}\hat{P}_{i})}\lesssim\inf_{y\in\nolinebreak\hat{P}_{i}}{\mathcal{M}}_{p_{0}}f(y).

Hence, (
4.9
) along with the above inequalities give us that

(4.15)

\lVert{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(P_{i})\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}}(P_{i})}\lesssim\eta\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{0})}.

Combining (
4.14
) and (
4.15
) with (
4.12
), we finally have that

\lVert(T-T\Lambda_{P_{i}})f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}}(P_{i})}\lesssim\eta\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{0})}.

And substituting this back to (
4.11
), we get

(
4.11
’)

\sum_{i}\int_{P_{i}}|T(f-\Lambda_{P_{i}}f)\cdot g|\,\mathrm{d}\mu\lesssim\eta\lVert f\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{0})}\sum_{i}\mu(P_{i})\Big(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{P_{i}}|g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Big)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}^{\prime}}}.

For
q_{0}^{\prime}=1
(or
q_{0}=\infty
),

\sum_{i}\mu(P_{i})\Big(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{P_{i}}|g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Big)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}^{\prime}}}=\sum_{i}\int_{P_{i}}|g|\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{\cup_{i}P_{i}}|g|\,\mathrm{d}\mu\lesssim\mu(Q_{0})\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\lambda Q_{0}}|g|\,\mathrm{d}\mu,

since the
P_{i}
’s are disjoint and
{\cup_{i}P_{i}}\subseteq Q_{0}
. Inputting this back to (
’
‣
4.3
), we get exactly (
4.5
).

For
q_{0}^{\prime}\in\nolinebreak(1,2)
(or
q_{0}\in\nolinebreak(2,\infty)
), first observe that
\big(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\big)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}^{\prime}}}\leq\inf_{y\in\nolinebreak Q}{\mathcal{M}}_{q_{0}^{\prime}}g(y)
for any
Q\in\nolinebreak{D}
. Then, since the
P_{i}
’s are disjoint, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\mu(P_{i})\Big(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{P_{i}}|g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Big)^{\frac{1}{q_{0}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i}\mu(P_{i})\inf_{y\in\nolinebreak P_{i}}{\mathcal{M}}_{q_{0}^{\prime}}g(y)\leq\sum_{i}\int_{P_{i}}{\mathcal{M}}_{q_{0}^{\prime}}g(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\cup_{i}P_{i}}{\mathcal{M}}_{q_{0}^{\prime}}g(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\lesssim\mu(\cup_{i}P_{i})^{1-\frac{1}{q_{0}^{\prime}}}\lVert g^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\rVert_{L^{1}(\cup_{i}P_{i})}^{\frac{1}{q_{0}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq\mu(Q_{0})\lVert g\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(Q_{0})}\lesssim\mu(Q_{0})\lVert g\rVert_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(\lambda Q_{0})}.

For the inequality in the second line, we have used Kolmogorov’s inequality and the boundedness of
{\mathcal{M}}_{q_{0}^{\prime}}
; in the third line, we used the fact that
\sum_{i}\mu(P_{i})\leq\mu(Q_{0})
and
Lemma

2.3
. This combined with (
’
‣
4.3
) again gives us exactly (
4.5
).

Therefore, since both
eqs.

4.4
and
4.5
are true, the assumptions of
Proposition

4.3
are fulfilled. Consequently, there exists a dyadic family
\{Q_{1}^{j}\}_{j}
of pairwise disjoint sets in
{D}(Q_{0})
with
\sum_{j}\mu(Q_{1}^{j})\leq\frac{dK}{\eta}\mu(E)
and such that

\int_{Q_{0}}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q_{0}}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq Cd^{3/2}|\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{0};{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\cdot\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(\lambda Q_{0};{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}|+\sum_{j}\int_{Q_{1}^{j}}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q_{1}^{j}}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

for all
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
and
g\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu,{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
whose coordinates are supported on
\lambda Q_{0}
.

Now, we need to construct our sparse family. This can be done by following Step 2 in the proof of Bernicot et al. almost without any changes; the fact that here we are dealing with vector-valued functions does not affect their argument. The conclusion is similar to
[
Hyt24
, Corollary 5.4]
.

Here, we explain the iterative procedure with which one constructs the sparse family. Initially, set
{S}:=\{Q_{0}\}
. Then, we can rewrite the above inequality in the following form

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{0}}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q_{0}}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq Cd^{3/2}|\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{0};{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\cdot\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(\lambda Q_{0};{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}|

\displaystyle+\sum_{j}\int_{Q_{1}^{j}}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q_{1}^{j}}f^{j})(x)\cdot g^{j}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

where (for all
j
)
f^{j}=\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda Q^{j}_{1}}f
, and
g^{j}=\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda Q^{j}_{1}}g
;
f^{j}
and
g^{j}
are supported on
\lambda Q_{1}^{j}
(by the definition of
\Lambda_{P}
and since the integration is over
Q_{1}^{j}
).

Next, we can add the collection of all such
Q_{1}^{j}
into
{S}
by redefining
{S}:={S}\cup\bigcup_{j}\{Q_{1}^{j}\}
, and then iterate on each
Q_{1}^{j}
to get

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{1}^{j}}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q_{1}^{j}}f^{j})(x)\cdot g^{j}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq Cd^{3/2}|\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q_{1}^{j};{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\cdot\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(\lambda Q_{1}^{j};{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}|

\displaystyle+\sum_{k}\int_{Q_{2}^{j,k}}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{Q_{2}^{j,k}}f^{j,k})(x)\cdot g^{j,k}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

and so on.

Eventually, as we show below, the remainder terms go to
0
and we conclude that

\int_{Q_{0}}|\mathbf{T}f\cdot g|\,\mathrm{d}\mu\leq Cd^{3/2}\eta\sum_{Q\in\nolinebreak{S}}\textmu(Q)\Big|\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\cdot\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(\lambda Q;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\Big|.

Following the argument in
[
BFP16
]
one needs to pick an
\eta
large enough so that the series
\sum_{\alpha\geq 1}\big(d\frac{K}{\eta}\big)^{\alpha}\leq\frac{dK}{\eta-dK}
converges. For such large
\eta
, the dyadic collection
{S}\subseteq{D}(Q_{0})
is
\varepsilon
-sparse with
\varepsilon=1-\frac{dK}{\eta-dK}
.

All that is left to do is to check that the remainder term indeed goes to
0
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
be the family of all cubes added at the initial sparse collection
\{Q_{0}\}
at the
n
-th step of our construction. The remainder term at the
n
-th step is clearly

\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\int_{P}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{P}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

By construction,
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mu(F_{n})=0
, where
F_{n}:=\bigcup_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}P
.

Write now
f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})
and
g=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{d})
. Then, similarly to the proof of
Proposition

4.3
we have

\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\int_{P}|\mathbf{T}(\Lambda_{P}f)(x)\cdot g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\int_{P}|T(\Lambda_{P}f_{i})(x)g_{i}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Fix
i\in\nolinebreak\{1,\ldots,d\}
. We show that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\int_{P}|T(\Lambda_{P}f_{i})(x)g_{i}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0.

Although this follows by a standard argument, we include the details for the reader’s convenience.

Apply first Hölder’s inequality:

\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\int_{P}|T(\Lambda_{P}f_{i})(x)g_{i}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\left(\int_{P}|T(\Lambda_{P}f_{i})(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{1/p}\left(\int_{P}|g_{i}(x)|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}.

By
Remark

2.6
(iii) and
Equation

2.9
, we deduce that the operators
\Lambda_{P}
,
P\in\nolinebreak{D}(Q_{0})
are
L^{p}(M,\mu)
-bounded,
uniformly in the cube
P
. Thus, since
T
is also by assumption
L^{p}(M,\mu)
bounded we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\left(\int_{P}|T(\Lambda_{P}f_{i})(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{1/p}\left(\int_{P}|g_{i}(x)|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert T\rVert_{L^{p}(M,\mu)}\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\left(\int_{P}|f_{i}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{1/p}\left(\int_{P}|g_{i}(x)|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}.

Applying Hölder’s inequality (for sums) and taking into account the disjointness of the sets
P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\left(\int_{P}|f_{i}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{1/p}\left(\int_{P}|g_{i}(x)|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\int_{P}|f_{i}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{1/p}\left(\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\int_{P}|g_{i}(x)|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{F_{n}}|f_{i}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{1/p}\left(\int_{F_{n}}|g_{i}|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}.

Since the functions
|f_{i}|^{p}
and
|g_{i}|^{p^{\prime}}
are
\mu
-integrable and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mu(F_{n})=0
, we deduce the desired result.
∎

5.
Matrix weighted bounds for bilinear convex body sparse forms

In this section we obtain matrix weighted bounds for bilinear convex body sparse forms of the type appearing in
Theorem

1.2
. Our main result is the following.

Theorem

1.3
.

Let
1\leq p_{0}<q_{0}\leq\infty
and
p\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
. Let
{D}
be a dyadic system in
M
as in Subsection
2.2
and let
\lambda\geq 2
. Let
0<\varepsilon<1
and let
{S}\subseteq{D}
be an
\varepsilon
-sparse collection. Set

t:=\frac{p}{p_{0}},\quad s:=\frac{q_{0}}{p},\quad a:=\frac{t^{\prime}p}{t},\quad b:=s^{\prime}p.

Let
W
be a
d\times d
matrix weight on
M
such that
[W^{s^{\prime}}]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}}<\infty
. Then, there exists a constant
C=(\mu,d,p_{0},q_{0},p)
such that for all
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)
and all
g\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{\prime}}(M,\mu)
the following estimate holds:

\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)|\llangle f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda P)}\cdot\llangle g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(\lambda P)}|\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon}([W^{s^{\prime}}]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}})^{\alpha}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{W}(M,\mu)}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{\prime}}(M,\mu)},

where
W^{\prime}:=W^{-1/(p-1)}
and

\alpha:=\frac{1}{s^{\prime}(p-p_{0})}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}.

After reviewing some notation and facts on the so-called
reducing operators
in Subsection
5.1
, which furnish a major tool for manipulating matrix weights in estimates, we introduce the so-called
Muckenhoupt characteristics
for matrix weights in Subsection
5.2
. Finally, we prove
Theorem

1.3
in Subsection
5.3
.

Further in the paper, we implement the following elementary facts without special mention:

(i)

For all
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\geq 0
, for all
a>0
, and for all positive integers
k
, we have

(5.1)

(x_{1}+\ldots+x_{k})^{a}\simeq_{a,k}x_{1}^{a}+\ldots+x_{k}^{a}.

In the rest of the text, we mostly apply this for
k=1,2,3,4
or
d
non-negative numbers. Moreover, the place of
a
will be taken by the exponent or exponents at hand.

(ii)

For a matrix
A\in\nolinebreak M_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
, we denote by
|A|
the
spectral norm
of
A
, that is, the largest singular value of
A
(i.e. the square root of the largest eigenvalue of the matrix
A^{*}A
). Then, it is true that

(5.2)

|A|\simeq_{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}|Ae_{i}|,

where
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{d})
is the standard orthonormal basis of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
.

5.1.
Reducing operators

Integral averages of scalar weights play a major role in the investigation of scalar weighted estimates. In the context of matrix weighted estimates, it is often the case that plain integral averages of positive definite matrix valued functions are poorly suited for the problems at hand. The concept of the so-called
reducing operators
offers in many cases a possible remedy.

Let
(X,m)
be a measure space and let
E
be a measurable subset of
X
with
0<m(E)<\infty
. Recall that an
m
-measurable function
f:E\to M_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
(for some positive integer
d
) is said to be
m
-integrable over
E
if

\int_{E}|f(x)|\,\mathrm{d}m(x)<\infty,

and that in this case we denote

\langle f\rangle_{E}:=\frac{1}{m(E)}\int_{E}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}m(x)=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}m(x).

Definition 5.1
.

(1)

Let
1\leq p<\infty
, and
W:E\to\mathrm{M}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
be an
m
-integrable function such that
W(x)
is a positive definite matrix for
m
-a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak E
. Then, one can consider the norm

e\mapsto\rho_{W,E,p}(e):=\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E}|W(x)^{1/p}e|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}m(x)\right)^{1/p}

acting on vectors
e\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
. Let
K\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
be the closed unit ball of
\rho_{W,E,p}
. In the terminology of
Section

3.3
,
K
is a convex body in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
with
0\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{Int}(K)
. Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the John ellipsoid of
K
. Subsequently, as explained in
Section

3.3
, there exists a positive definite matrix
A\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{M}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
with
A\overline{\mathbf{B}}={\mathcal{E}}
, where
\overline{\mathbf{B}}
is the standard (Euclidean) closed unit ball in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
. Following
[
Gol03
]
, we call the matrix
A^{-1}

the reducing operator of
W
over
E
with respect to the exponent
p
and denote
{\mathcal{W}}_{E,p}:=A^{-1}
.

(We omit the measure
m
from the notation of the reducing operator.) Since
{\mathcal{E}}\subseteq K\subseteq d^{1/2}{\mathcal{E}}
(see
[
Gol03
,
Naz+17
]
), one easily deduces that

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E}|W(x)^{1/p}e|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}m(x)\right)^{1/p}\leq|{\mathcal{W}}_{E,p}e|\leq d^{1/2}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E}|W(x)^{1/p}e|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}m(x)\right)^{1/p}\quad\forall e\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}.

Notice that if
p=2
or
d=1
, we simply have
{\mathcal{W}}_{E,p}=\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E}W(x)\,\mathrm{d}m(x)\right)^{1/p}
.

(2)

If
1<p<\infty
and
W:E\to\mathrm{M}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
is a
m
-measurable function with
W(x)\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{P}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
for
m
-a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak E
such that the function
V:=W^{-1/(p-1)}
is
m
-integrable over
E
, then we denote
{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}_{E,p}:={\mathcal{V}}_{E,p^{\prime}}
.

Notice that, if
1<s<\infty
, and
W:E\to\mathrm{M}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
is a
m
-integrable function with
W(x)\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{P}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
for
m
-a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak E
, then, by Jensen’s inequality, we have

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E}|W(x)^{1/s}|\,\mathrm{d}m(x)=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E}|W(x)|^{1/s}\,\mathrm{d}m(x)\leq\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E}|W(x)|\,\mathrm{d}m(x)\right)^{1/s}<\infty.

Therefore, the function
W^{1/s}
is also
m
-integrable over
E
. However, this might not be the case for
W^{s}
.

The following standard lemma, whose proof we omit, helps to avoid ambiguous terminology below.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
(X,m)
be a measure space and let
E
be a measurable subset of
X
with
0<m(E)<\infty
.
Let
1\leq a,b<\infty
, and
W,V:E\to\mathrm{M}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
be
m
-measurable functions with
W(x),V(x)\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{P}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
for
m
-a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak E
. Then, we have

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E}|W(x)^{1/b}V(y)^{1/a}|^{a}\,\mathrm{d}m(y)\right)^{b/a}\,\mathrm{d}m(x)<\infty\iff W,\penalty\ V\text{ are $m$-integrable over }E.

Moreover, in this case, the following holds:

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E}|W(x)^{1/b}V(y)^{1/a}|^{a}\,\mathrm{d}m(y)\right)^{b/a}\,\mathrm{d}m(x)\simeq_{d,a,b}|{\mathcal{W}}_{E,b}{\mathcal{V}}_{E,a}|^{b}.

5.2.
Matrix Muckenhoupt weights

The weight
W
in
Theorem

1.3
was assumed to satisfy the condition
[W^{s^{\prime}}]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}}<\infty
, which is a so-called
matrix Muckenhoupt condition
. We explain the terminology in this subsection.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
1<p\leq q<\infty
, and
W:M\to\mathrm{M}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
be a measurable function with values
\mu
-a.e. in the set of positive-definite
d\times d
complex-valued matrices. We define

(5.3)

[W]_{A_{p,q}}:=\sup_{B}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B}|W(x)^{\frac{1}{q}}W(y)^{-\frac{1}{q}}|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\right)^{\frac{q}{p^{\prime}}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

where the supremum ranges over all open balls
B\subseteq M
. If
[W]_{A_{p,q}}<\infty
, then we say that
W
is a
d\times d
matrix
A_{p,q}
weight
, and we write
W\in\nolinebreak A_{p,q}
. If
p=q
, then we denote
A_{p,p}:=A_{p}
.

Variants of the matrix
A_{p,q}
weights were already introduced and studied in
[
NT96
,
LT07
]
. The particular form of condition (
5.3
) considered here was first introduced and studied in
[
IM19
]
and further investigated in
[
CI22
]
.

Below, we present several properties of the matrix
A_{p,q}
weights that are important for our purposes. These were all established in
[
IM19
]
in the case when the ambient space
M
is simply
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
equipped with the Euclidean metric and the
n
-dimensional Lebesgue measure, but they still hold true in arbitrary doubling measure metric spaces simply by replacing cubes with balls. See
[
IM19
, Section 3]
for the proofs.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
1<p\leq q<\infty
, and let
W
be a
d\times d
matrix
A_{p,q}
weight on
M
. Set
r:=1+\frac{q}{p^{\prime}}
. Then, the following are true:

\displaystyle[W^{-p^{\prime}/q}]_{A_{q^{\prime},p^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\simeq_{d,p,q}[W]_{A_{p,q}}^{1/(r-1)};

\displaystyle\sup_{e\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}}[|W^{1/q}e|^{q}]_{A_{r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p,q}[W]_{A_{p,q}};

\displaystyle\sup_{e\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}}[|W^{-1/q}e|^{p^{\prime}}]_{A_{r^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p,q}[W^{-p^{\prime}/q}]_{A_{q^{\prime},p^{\prime}}}.

We also need the so-called “weak” reverse Hölder property of scalar Muckenhoupt weights, in general, doubling measure metric spaces, as obtained from
[
HPR12
, Theorem 1.1]
coupled with (a trivial generalization of)
[
Naz+17
, Lemma 4.1]
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
w
be a scalar weight on
M
with
[w]_{A_{p}}<\infty
for some
1<p<\infty
. Then, there exist constants
a_{1},a_{2}>0
depending only on the doubling measure
\mu
and
p
such that for all
0<\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{1+a_{1}[w]_{A_{p}}}
we have

\langle w^{1+\varepsilon}\rangle_{B}^{1/(1+\varepsilon)}\leq a_{2}\langle w\rangle_{2B},

for all open balls
B\subseteq M
, where
2B
denotes the ball co-centric with
B
and twice its radius.

In particular,
Lemma

5.5
can be used to show that scalar
A_{p}
weights for
1<p<\infty
satisfy an openness property, see, for instance,
[
HPR12
]
for a precise statement. However, counterexamples due to
[
Bow01
]
show that matrix
A_{p}
weights on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
fail to satisfy an analogous openness property for any
1<p<\infty
.

5.3.
Proving the matrix-weighted bound

We devote this subsection to a proof of
Theorem

1.3
.

First, we need some preparation. Fix
1\leq p_{0}<p<q_{0}\leq\infty
. Next, set

t:=\frac{p}{p_{0}},\quad s:=\frac{q_{0}}{p},\quad\tilde{s}:=\frac{p^{\prime}}{q_{0}^{\prime}},

and

a:=\frac{t^{\prime}p}{t},\quad b:=s^{\prime}p.

Observe that
t,s,\tilde{s},a,b>1
, and
t,\tilde{s},a,b<\infty
. Direct computation gives

a=\frac{p}{t-1}=t^{\prime}p_{0},\quad b=\frac{p^{\prime}}{\tilde{s}-1}=\tilde{s}^{\prime}q_{0}^{\prime},

and

\frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{b}=\frac{1}{p_{0}}-\frac{1}{q_{0}}\leq 1.

In particular, we have
b\geq a^{\prime}
. Finally, define

r:=s^{\prime}(t-1)+1.

Observe that
r\in\nolinebreak(1,\infty)
and

\frac{b}{a}=r-1=\frac{s^{\prime}t}{t^{\prime}}.

Let
W
be a
d\times d
matrix weight on
M
. Set
U:=W^{-1/(t-1)}
and
V:=W^{s^{\prime}}
. Notice that

U=V^{-1/(r-1)}\quad\text{and}\quad V^{1/b}=W^{1/p}=U^{-1/a}.

Assume now that

[V]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}}<\infty.

Then, by
Lemma

5.2
we deduce the following:

(5.4)

|{\mathcal{U}}_{B,a}{\mathcal{V}}_{B,b}|\lesssim_{d,a,b}[V]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}}^{1/b},

for all open balls
B\subseteq M
. Also,
Lemma

5.4
for
p=a^{\prime}
and
q=b
gives us that

(5.5)

\displaystyle[|V^{1/b}e|^{b}]_{A_{r}}\lesssim_{d,a,b}[V]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}}\quad\forall e\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}\setminus\{0\},

(5.6)

\displaystyle[|U^{1/a}e|^{a}]_{A_{r^{\prime}}}\lesssim_{d,a,b}[U]_{A_{b^{\prime},a}}\quad\forall e\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}\setminus\{0\},

and

(5.7)

\displaystyle[U]_{A_{b^{\prime},a}}\simeq_{d,a,b}[V]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}}^{1/(r-1)}.

Now, we are finally ready to prove
Theorem

1.3

Proof (of
Theorem

1.3
).

We adapt the arguments from
[
Naz+17
, Section 5]
. We keep the setup and notation from the previous subsection. Throughout the proof, we ignore all implied constants depending only on
\mu,d,p_{0},q_{0}
and
p
. First of all, it suffices to prove that

\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)|\llangle W^{-1/p}f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda P)}\cdot\llangle W^{1/p}g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(\lambda P)}|\lesssim\frac{1}{\varepsilon}([W^{s^{\prime}}]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}})^{\alpha}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})},

for all
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
and all
g\in\nolinebreak L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})
. In what follows, we fix such
f
and
g
. From
Lemma

2.3
we have that for all
P\in\nolinebreak{S}
, there exists an open ball
B(P)\subseteq M
such that
\lambda P\subseteq B(P)
and
\mu(B(P))\lesssim\mu(\lambda P)
. Thus, we begin by estimating

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle:=\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)|\llangle W^{-1/p}f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda P)}\cdot\llangle W^{1/p}g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(\lambda P)}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)|(\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}^{-1}\llangle W^{-1/p}f\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{p_{0}}(\lambda P)})\cdot(\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}\llangle W^{1/p}g\rrangle_{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-3.0005pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.13586pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.35042pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-1.06459pt}}\!L^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(\lambda P)})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)\left\langle|\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}^{-1}W^{-1/p}f|^{p_{0}}\right\rangle_{\lambda P}^{1/p_{0}}\left\langle|\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}W^{1/p}g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{\lambda P}^{1/q_{0}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)\left\langle|\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}^{-1}W^{-1/p}f|^{p_{0}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}^{1/p_{0}}\left\langle|\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}W^{1/p}g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}^{1/q_{0}^{\prime}}.

We apply Hölder’s inequality for the exponents
p
and
p^{\prime}
:

A\leq\left(\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)\left\langle|\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}^{-1}W^{-1/p}f|^{p_{0}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}^{p/p_{0}}\right)^{1/p}\left(\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)\left\langle|\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}W^{1/p}g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}^{p^{\prime}/q_{0}^{\prime}}\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}=:A_{1}\cdot A_{2}.

Now we estimate
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
from above separately. We start with
A_{1}
:

\displaystyle A_{1}^{p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)\left\langle|\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}^{-1}W^{-1/p}f|^{p_{0}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}^{p/p_{0}}=\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)\left\langle|\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}^{-1}U^{1/a}f|^{a/t^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}^{t}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)\left\langle|\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}^{-1}U^{1/a}|^{a/t^{\prime}}\cdot|f|^{a/t^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}^{t}.

Set
\theta:=t^{\prime}(1+\delta)
, for some
\delta>0
to be chosen later. Then, applying Hölder’s inequality for the exponents
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
, we get

\displaystyle A_{1}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)\left\langle|\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}^{-1}U^{1/a}|^{a\theta/t^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}^{t/\theta}\cdot{\left\langle|f|^{a\theta^{\prime}/t^{\prime}}\right\rangle}_{B(P)}^{t/\theta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)\left\langle|\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}^{-1}U^{1/a}|^{a(1+\delta)}\right\rangle_{B(P)}^{(t-1)/(1+\delta)}\cdot{\left\langle|f|^{a\theta^{\prime}/t^{\prime}}\right\rangle}_{B(P)}^{t/\theta^{\prime}}.

For every
P\in\nolinebreak{S}
, consider the scalar weight

\omega_{P}:=|\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}^{-1}U^{1/a}|^{a}=|U^{1/a}\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}^{-1}|^{a}

on
M
. Then, by (
5.6
) coupled with (a tiny modification of)
[
Dom+24
, Lemma 3.4]
, we deduce

\sup_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}[\omega_{P}]_{A_{r^{\prime}}}\lesssim[U]_{A_{b^{\prime},a}}.

Thus, by the weak Reverse Hölder property for scalar Muckenhoupt weights,
Lemma

5.5
, there exists a constant
a_{1}>0
depending only on
\mu,d,p_{0},q_{0}
and
p
such that for the choice

\delta:=\frac{1}{1+a_{1}[U]_{A_{b^{\prime},a}}}

we have, for every
P\in\nolinebreak{S}
,

\left\langle\omega_{P}^{1+\delta}\right\rangle_{B}^{1/(1+\delta)}\lesssim\left\langle\omega_{P}\right\rangle_{2B},

for every open ball
B\subseteq M
. Thus, for every
P\in\nolinebreak{S}
, we obtain

\left\langle\omega_{P}^{1+\delta}\right\rangle_{B(P)}^{1/(1+\delta)}\lesssim\left\langle\omega_{P}\right\rangle_{2B(P)}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{2B(P)}|U(x)^{1/a}\mathcal{U}_{2B(P),a}^{-1}|^{a}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\simeq|{\mathcal{U}}_{2B(P),a}{\mathcal{U}}_{2B(P),a}^{-1}|^{a}=1.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle A_{1}^{p}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)\left\langle|f|^{a\theta^{\prime}/t^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}^{t/\theta^{\prime}}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(F_{P})\left\langle|f|^{a\theta^{\prime}/t^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}^{t/\theta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\int_{F_{P}}(\mathcal{M}(|f|^{a\theta^{\prime}/t^{\prime}})(x))^{t/\theta^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{M}(\mathcal{M}(|f|^{a\theta^{\prime}/t^{\prime}})(x))^{t/\theta^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

since the sets
F_{P}
,
P\in\nolinebreak{S}
are pairwise disjoint. Observe that
\theta>t^{\prime}
, therefore
\theta^{\prime}<t
, that is
q:=t/\theta^{\prime}>1
. Thus, applying (
2.6
) we obtain

A_{1}^{p}\lesssim\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(q^{\prime})^{q}\int_{M}(|f(x)|^{a\theta^{\prime}/t^{\prime}})^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=(q^{\prime})^{q}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{M}|f(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Lemma

5.6
below shows that
(q^{\prime})^{q}\lesssim\delta^{-1}
, therefore we finally obtain

A_{1}\lesssim\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{1/p}}[U]_{A_{b^{\prime},a}}^{1/p}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}.

To estimate
A_{2}
, we first observe that for all
P\in\nolinebreak{S}
, by (
5.4
) we get

\displaystyle\left\langle|{\mathcal{U}}_{2B(P),a}W^{1/p}g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle|{\mathcal{U}}_{2B(P),a}{\mathcal{V}}_{2B(P),b}{\mathcal{V}}_{2B(P),b}^{-1}W^{1/p}g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}

\displaystyle\leq|{\mathcal{U}}_{2B(P),a}{\mathcal{V}}_{2B(P),b}|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\cdot\left\langle|{\mathcal{V}}_{2B(P),b}^{-1}W^{1/p}g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}

\displaystyle\lesssim[V]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}}^{q_{0}^{\prime}/b}\cdot\left\langle|{\mathcal{V}}_{2B(P),b}^{-1}W^{1/p}g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}.

Similarly to the above and using (
5.5
) instead of (
5.6
), we obtain

\left(\sum_{P\in\nolinebreak{S}}\mu(P)\left\langle|{\mathcal{V}}_{2B(P),b}^{-1}W^{1/p}g|^{q_{0}^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{B(P)}^{p^{\prime}/q_{0}^{\prime}}\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{1/p^{\prime}}}[V]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}}^{1/p^{\prime}}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}.

Putting all estimates together and using (
5.7
), we finally deduce

A\lesssim\frac{1}{\varepsilon}([V]_{A_{a^{\prime},b}})^{\alpha}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}(M,\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{d})},

where

\alpha:=\frac{1}{p(r-1)}+\frac{1}{b}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{s^{\prime}p(t-1)}+\frac{1}{s^{\prime}p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=\frac{t}{s^{\prime}p(t-1)}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{s^{\prime}(p-p_{0})}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}},

concluding the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
0<\delta<1
. Set
\theta:=t^{\prime}(1+\delta)
and
q:=\frac{t}{\theta^{\prime}}
. Then, we have
(q^{\prime})^{q}\lesssim\delta^{-1}
.

Proof.

Set
y:=q-1>0
, then

y=\frac{t}{\theta^{\prime}}-1=\frac{t}{\frac{\theta}{\theta-1}}-1=\frac{t(\theta-1)-\theta}{\theta}.

Since
\theta=t^{\prime}(1+\delta)
, we rewrite

y=\frac{t(t^{\prime}(1+\delta)-1)-t^{\prime}(1+\delta)}{t^{\prime}(1+\delta)},

which gives

(5.8)

y=\frac{t\left(\frac{t}{t-1}(1+\delta)-1\right)-\frac{t}{t-1}(1+\delta)}{\frac{t}{t-1}(1+\delta)}=\frac{(t(1+\delta)-t+1)-(1+\delta)}{(1+\delta)}=\frac{\delta}{1+\delta}(t-1).

Now, we observe that

(q^{\prime})^{q}=((y+1)^{\prime})^{y+1}=\left(1+\frac{1}{y}\right)^{y+1}\leq e\left(1+\frac{1}{y}\right).

Thus, using (
5.8
), we get

(q^{\prime})^{q}\leq e\left(1+\frac{1+\delta}{\delta(t-1)}\right)=e\left(\frac{\delta t+1}{\delta(t-1)}\right)<e\frac{t+1}{\delta(t-1)}\lesssim\delta^{-1}

concluding the proof.
∎

5.4.
An open question on sharpness of estimates

Although we expect our estimates to be both qualitatively and quantitatively sharp, especially in the view of
[
Dom+24a
]
, we have not been able to prove that as of the moment of writing.

6.
A hierarchy of two-exponent matrix Muckenhoupt conditions

In this section we want to understand the relationship of the various two-exponent matrix Muckenhoupt characteristics
[W]_{A_{p,q}}
for
1<p\leq q<\infty
with
\frac{q}{p^{\prime}}+1=r
for some
fixed

r
. In the scalar case, all these characteristics coincide. However, in the matrix case, they turn out to be quite different from each other, and only one-sided inclusions remain in general true. We provide explicit, fully constructive counterexamples demonstrating that. As a result, we deduce that the matrix weighted bound of
Theorem

1.3
improves the one obtained earlier in
[
Lau23
]
. Our methods are very flexible and allow us to consider even several endpoint cases that play an important role in the limited range extrapolation in
Section

7
later.

Throughout this section, for the sake of clarity and simplicity we focus only on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
equipped with the Euclidean metric and the
n
-dimensional Lebesgue measure. In particular, we simply write
\mathrm{d}x
for integration under the Lebesgue measure, and
|E|
for the
n
-dimensional Lebesgue measure of any Lebesgue measurable subset
E
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

6.1.
The members of the hierarchy

Let us first recall the two-exponent matrix Muckenhoupt conditions from

Definition

5.3
. In the case of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, one usually takes the supremum in
Definition

5.3
to range over cubes
Q\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
(with faces parallel to the coordinate hyperplanes) instead of open balls
B\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then, the resulting Muckenhoupt characteristics are equivalent to their “ball” versions up to constants depending only on
n
and the exponents in question. The resulting equivalence classes of (scalar and matrix) weights coincide with their “ball” versions.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
1<p\leq q<\infty
, and
W:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to\mathrm{M}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
be a measurable function with values a.e. in the set of positive-definite
d\times d
complex-valued matrices. We define

[W]_{A_{p,q}}:=\sup_{Q}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{\frac{1}{q}}W(y)^{-\frac{1}{q}}|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{\frac{q}{p^{\prime}}}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty,

where the supremum ranges over all cubes
Q\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
(with faces parallel to the coordinate hyperplanes). If
[W]_{A_{p,q}}<\infty
, then we say that
W
is a
d\times d
matrix
A_{p,q}
weight
, and we write
W\in\nolinebreak A_{p,q}
. If
p=q
, then we denote
A_{p,p}:=A_{p}
.

Furthermore, we are interested in the following endpoint cases.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
W:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to\mathrm{M}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
be a measurable function with values a.e. in the set of positive-definite
d\times d
complex-valued matrices, and
1\leq t<\infty
. We define

[W]_{A_{t,\infty}}:=\sup_{B}\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak B}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B}|W(x)^{1/t}W(y)^{-1/t}|^{t}\,\mathrm{d}y,

where the supremum ranges over all cubes
Q\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
(with faces parallel to the coordinate hyperplanes). If
[W]_{A_{t,\infty}}<\infty
, then we say that
W
is a
d\times d
matrix
A_{t,\infty}
weight
, and we write
W\in\nolinebreak A_{t,\infty}
. Moreover, we denote
A_{1,\infty}:=A_{\infty}
.

Our definition of
A_{\infty}
follows
[
BC22
]
. However, our definitions of
A_{t,\infty}
and
A_{\infty}
deviate from the ones appearing in other places in the literature, even in the scalar case. See, for example,
[
NT96
,
Vol97
,
HPR12
,
Naz+17
,
CIM18
,
ID24
]
. We also refer to
[
DMO16
]
for a detailed discussion of the relationship between the various definitions in the scalar case.

It is worth noting that if
W:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to\mathrm{P}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
is a measurable function with
W\in\nolinebreak A_{t,\infty}
for some
1\leq t<\infty
, then the function
|W|
is locally bounded and the function
W^{-1}
is locally integrable.

We turn our attention to reverse Hölder classes.

Definition 6.3
.

If
w
is a scalar weight on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
1<s<\infty
, then we define

[w]_{RH_{s}}:=\sup_{Q}\frac{\langle w^{s}\rangle_{Q}^{1/s}}{\langle w\rangle_{Q}},

where the supremum ranges over all cubes
Q
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
(with faces parallel to the coordinate hyperplanes). We say that
w
belongs to the
Reverse Hölder class of order
s
, whenever
[w]_{RH_{s}}<\infty
, and we write
w\in\nolinebreak RH_{s}
.

Moreover, following
[
AM07
]
, for a scalar weight
w
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
we define

[w]_{RH_{\infty}}:=\sup_{Q}\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak Q}\frac{w(x)}{\langle w\rangle_{Q}},

where the supremum ranges over all cubes
Q
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
