(with faces parallel to the coordinate hyperplanes). We say that
w
belongs to the
Reverse Hölder class of order
\infty
whenever
[w]_{RH_{\infty}}<\infty
, and we write
w\in\nolinebreak RH_{\infty}
.

Definition 6.4
.

If
W
is a
d\times d
matrix weight on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
1<t<\infty
, and
1<s\leq\infty
, then following
[
Lau23
]
we define

[W]_{RH_{t,s}}:=\sup_{e\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}[|W^{1/t}e|^{t}]_{RH_{s}}<\infty.

We say that
W
belongs to the
Reverse Hölder class of exponent
t
and order
s
and write
W\in\nolinebreak RH_{t,s}
when
[W]_{RH_{t,s}}<\infty
.

6.2.
Situation in the scalar case

The various condition introduced in
Section

6.1
are related to each other in the case of scalar weights in a very simple way. We briefly review how below.

Let
w
be a scalar weight on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then, for all
1\leq p\leq q<\infty
, we clearly have

(6.1)

[w]_{A_{p,q}}=[w]_{A_{r}},\quad\text{where }r:=1+\frac{q}{p^{\prime}}.

It is also obvious that

(6.2)

[w]_{A_{t,\infty}}=[w]_{A_{\infty}},

for all
1\leq t<\infty
.

Finally, let
1<s,t<\infty
and set
r:=s(t-1)+1
. It is a well-known fact that

(6.3)

w\in\nolinebreak A_{t}\cap RH_{s}\iff w^{s}\in\nolinebreak A_{r},

see, for example,
[
JN91
,
AM07
,
BFP16
]
.

6.3.
Commonalities with the scalar case

In this subsection we show that at least parts of (
6.1
) and (
6.2
), as well as an appropriate form of (
6.3
) survive in the matrix case.

We begin with the correct analog of (
6.3
).

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
W
be a
d\times d
matrix weight on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, and
1<t,s<\infty
. Then, the following hold:

(1)

\max([W]_{A_{t}},[W]_{RH_{t,s}})^{s}\lesssim_{d,t,s}[W^{s}]_{A_{t,st}}\lesssim_{d,t,s}([W]_{A_{t}}[W]_{RH_{t,s}})^{s}.

In particular,
W\in\nolinebreak A_{t}\cap RH_{t,s}
if and only if
W^{s}\in\nolinebreak A_{t,st}
.

(2)

\max([W]_{A_{t}},[W]_{RH_{t,\infty}})^{t^{\prime}/t}\lesssim_{d,t}[W^{t^{\prime}/t}]_{A_{t^{\prime},\infty}}\lesssim_{d,t}([W]_{A_{t}}[W]_{RH_{t,\infty}})^{t^{\prime}/t}.

In particular,
W\in\nolinebreak A_{t}\cap RH_{t,\infty}
if and only if
W^{t^{\prime}/t}\in\nolinebreak A_{t^{\prime},\infty}
.

Proof.

For (1), set
V:=W^{s}
. Then,
V^{1/(st)}=W^{1/t}
.

First, assume that
[W^{s}]_{A_{t,st}}<\infty
. For all cubes
Q\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, since
s\geq 1
, Jensen’s inequality yields

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/t}W(y)^{-1/t}|^{t^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{t/t^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/t}W(y)^{-1/t}|^{t^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{st/t^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/s},

which directly implies that
[W]_{A_{t}}\leq[V]_{A_{t,st}}^{1/s}
.

Let us now show that
[W]_{RH_{t,s}}\lesssim_{d,t,s}[V]_{A_{t,s^{\prime}t}}^{1/s}
. Let
Q\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be any cube and let
e\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. Since
[W]_{A_{t}}<\infty
,
W^{-1/(t-1)}
is integrable over
Q
. Therefore, repeatedly applying (
5.2
) and (
5.1
), we can estimate

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/t}{\mathcal{W}}_{Q,t}^{\prime}|^{st}\,\mathrm{d}x\simeq_{d,t,s}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/t}W(y)^{-1/t}|^{t^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{st/t^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}x\leq[V]_{A_{t,st}}.

It follows that

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/t}e|^{st}\,\mathrm{d}x=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/t}{\mathcal{W}}_{Q,t}^{\prime}({\mathcal{W}}_{Q,t}^{\prime})^{-1}e|^{st}\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim_{d,t,s}[V]_{A_{t,st}}|({\mathcal{W}}_{Q,t}^{\prime})^{-1}e|^{st}.

It is well-known (see e.g.
[
NT96
,
LT07
,
Dom+24
]
) that
|({\mathcal{W}}_{Q,t}^{\prime})^{-1}e|\leq|{\mathcal{W}}_{Q,t}e|
.
From this, we get that

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/t}e|^{st}\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim_{d,t,s}[V]_{A_{t,st}}|{\mathcal{W}}_{Q,t}e|^{st}\simeq_{d,t,s}[V]_{A_{t,st}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/t}e|^{t}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{s}.

Consequently,
[W]_{RH_{t,s}}\lesssim_{d,t,s}[V]_{A_{t,s^{\prime}t}}^{1/s}
.

Conversely, assume that
W\in\nolinebreak A_{t}\cap RH_{t,st}
. Then, repeatedly applying (
5.2
) and (
5.1
), we can estimate

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/t}W(y)^{-1/t}|^{t^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{st/t^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\simeq_{d,t,s}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/t}{\mathcal{W}}_{Q,t}^{\prime}|^{st}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,t,s}[W]_{RH_{t,s}}^{s}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/t}{\mathcal{W}}_{Q,t}^{\prime}|^{t}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{s}

\displaystyle\simeq_{d,t,s}[W]_{RH_{t,s}}^{s}|{\mathcal{W}}_{Q,t}{\mathcal{W}}_{Q,t}^{\prime}|^{ts}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,t,s}[W]_{RH_{t,s}}^{s}[W]_{A_{t}}^{s},

for all cubes
Q\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. It follows that
[V]_{A_{t,st}}\lesssim_{d,t,s}[W]_{RH_{t,s}}^{s}[W]_{A_{t}}^{s}
.

We omit the proof for (2) as it is very similar to the proof for part (1).
∎

Next, we treat the parts of (
6.1
), (
6.2
) that survive in the scalar case.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
W
be a
d\times d
matrix weight on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

(1)

Let
1<p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2}<\infty
with
p_{i}\leq q_{i}
(for
i=1,2
),
p_{1}\leq p_{2}
and
\frac{q_{2}}{p_{2}^{\prime}}=\frac{q_{1}}{p_{1}^{\prime}}
. Then,

[W]_{A_{p_{1},q_{1}}}\leq[W]_{A_{p_{2},q_{2}}}.

(2)

Let
1\leq s\leq t<\infty
. Then,

[W]_{A_{t,\infty}}\leq[W]_{A_{s,\infty}}.

In the proof, we apply the following inequality due to Cordes
[
Cor87
]
.

Lemma 6.7

(Cordes Inequality)
.

Let
A,B\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{P}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
and
0\leq a\leq 1
. Then, it holds that

|A^{a}B^{a}|\leq|AB|^{a}.

Proof (of
Lemma

6.6
).

For (1), first observe that

0<\frac{q_{2}}{q_{1}}=\frac{p_{2}^{\prime}}{p_{1}^{\prime}}\leq 1.

Therefore, by Cordes inequality, we estimate

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{q_{1}/p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|(W(x)^{1/q_{2}})^{q_{2}/q_{1}}(W(y)^{-1/q_{2}})^{q_{2}/q_{1}}|^{q_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{q_{1}/p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/q_{2}}W(y)^{-1/q_{2}}|^{q_{2}p_{1}^{\prime}/q_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{q_{1}/p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q}|W(x)^{1/q_{2}}W(y)^{1/q_{2}}|^{p_{2}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{q_{2}/p_{2}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x,

for all cubes
Q\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. The desired result follows.

The proof for (2) is analogous to the proof of part (1).
∎

6.4.
Differences from the scalar case

Despite the positive results in Subsection
6.3
, (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) can actually fail in the matrix case. The following two propositions demonstrate that. Let

{\mathcal{D}}:=\{[m2^{k},(m+1)2^{k}):\penalty\ m,k\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}\}

be the family of dyadic intervals in
{\mathbb{R}}
. For
1<p\leq q<\infty
, we denote

[W]_{A_{p,q},{\mathcal{D}}}:=\sup_{I\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{D}}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I}|W(x)^{1/q}W(y)^{-1/q}|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{q/p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Similarly to
Definition

6.2
(with the cubes replaced by the dyadic intervals of
{\mathbb{R}}
), we define
[W]_{A_{t,\infty},{\mathcal{D}}}
for
1\leq t<\infty
.

Proposition 6.8
.

(1)

Let
1<p_{1}\leq q_{1}<\infty
. Then, there exists a
2\times 2
matrix weight
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}
with
[W]_{A_{p_{1},q_{1}}}<\infty
such that for all
1<p_{2}\leq q_{2}<\infty
with
p_{1}<p_{2}
and
\frac{q_{2}}{p_{2}^{\prime}}=\frac{q_{1}}{p_{1}^{\prime}}
we have
[W]_{A_{p_{2},q_{2}},{\mathcal{D}}}=\infty
.

(2)

Let
1<t<\infty
. Then, there exists a
2\times 2
matrix weight
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}
with
[W]_{A_{t,\infty}}<\infty
such that
[W]_{A_{s,\infty},{\mathcal{D}}}=\infty
for all
1\leq s<t
.

Proposition 6.9
.

(1)

Let
1<p_{2}\leq q_{2}<\infty
. Then, there exists a
2\times 2
matrix weight
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}
with
[W]_{A_{p_{2},q_{2}},{\mathcal{D}}}=\infty
such that
[W]_{A_{p_{1},q_{1}}}<\infty
, for all
1<p_{1}\leq q_{1}<\infty
with
\frac{q_{1}^{\prime}}{p_{1}}=\frac{q_{2}^{\prime}}{p_{2}}
and
p_{1}<p_{2}
.

(2)

Let
1\leq s<\infty
. Then, there exists a
2\times 2
matrix weight
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}
with
[W]_{A_{s,\infty},{\mathcal{D}}}=\infty
such that
[W]_{A_{t,\infty}}<\infty
, for all
s<t<\infty
.

Before proving
Proposition

6.8
and
Proposition

6.9
, let us briefly examine their implications for the relationship of the matrix weighted bounds of
Theorem

1.3
to those obtained earlier by
[
Lau23
]
. We first recall the notation of
Theorem

1.3
: we consider
1\leq p_{0}<q_{0}\leq\infty
,
p\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
and let

t:=\frac{p}{p_{0}},\quad s:=\frac{q_{0}}{p},\quad a:=\frac{p}{t-1},\quad b:=s^{\prime}p.

In this notation, the matrix-weighted bounds in
[
Lau23
]
are valid for matrix weights
W\in\nolinebreak A_{t}\cap RH_{t,s^{\prime}}
. By
Lemma

6.5
, this condition is equivalent to
W^{s^{\prime}}\in\nolinebreak A_{t,s^{\prime}t}
. Observe that
t=\frac{p}{p_{0}}\leq p
. Therefore

a=\frac{p}{t-1}\geq\frac{t}{t-1}=t^{\prime},

and consequently
a^{\prime}\leq t
. Since
\frac{b}{a}=\frac{s^{\prime}t}{t^{\prime}}
, by
Lemma

6.6
we deduce that the condition
W^{s^{\prime}}\in\nolinebreak A_{a^{\prime},b}
featuring in
Theorem

1.3
is weaker than the condition
W\in\nolinebreak A_{t}\cap RH_{t,s^{\prime}}
. Observe also that
a^{\prime}=t
if and only if
p_{0}=1
. Therefore, if
p_{0}>1
and
d\geq 2
, then using
Proposition

6.8
one can find a
d\times d
matrix weight
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with
W^{s^{\prime}}\in\nolinebreak A_{a^{\prime},b}
but
W\notin A_{t}\cap RH_{t,s^{\prime}}
.

Let us now prove
Proposition

6.8
and
Proposition

6.9
. The core of both proofs boils down to constructing appropriate families of counterexamples to the Cordes inequality. One such family is given in the lemma below.

Lemma 6.10
.

Set

C_{n}:=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&n\end{bmatrix},\quad D_{n}:=\begin{bmatrix}1&\frac{1}{2n^{1/2}}\\
\frac{1}{2n^{1/2}}&\frac{1}{n}\end{bmatrix},\quad n=1,2,\ldots.

Then,
C_{n},D_{n}\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{P}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
with

\tr(C_{n}D_{n}),\penalty\ \tr(C_{n}^{-1}D_{n}^{-1})\simeq 1,

for all
n=1,2,\ldots
. Moreover, for all
a>1
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\tr(C_{n}^{a}D_{n}^{a})=\infty.

We apply
Lemma

6.10
to prove
Lemma

6.11
. For completeness, we give a proof of
Lemma

6.10
in
Section

8
, together with a second family of counterexamples to the converse of the Cordes inequality. The latter family allows one to prove in a similar way
Proposition

6.9
. Thus, we concentrate on proving
Proposition

6.8
. We only prove part (1) of
Proposition

6.8
; it is clear that part (2) can be proved similarly.

Going forward, by “interval” we always mean a left-closed, right-open, non-trivial bounded interval in
{\mathbb{R}}
. Before proving part (1) of
Proposition

6.8
, we construct an analogous counterexample on the unit interval
I_{0}:=[0,1)
. A
weight

W
on
I_{0}
is an integrable function
W:I_{0}\to\mathrm{M}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
with
W(x)\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{P}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak I_{0}
. Moreover, for
1<p\leq q<\infty
we denote

[W]_{A_{p,q}(I_{0})}:=\sup_{I\subseteq I_{0}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I}|W(x)^{1/q}W(y)^{-1/q}|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{q/p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x,

and we define
[W]_{A_{p,q},{\mathcal{D}}(I_{0})}
analogously.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
1<p_{1}\leq q_{1}<\infty
. Then, there exists a
2\times 2
matrix weight
W
on
I_{0}
with
[W]_{A_{p_{1},q_{1}}(I_{0})}<\infty
such that for all
1<p_{2}\leq q_{2}<\infty
with
p_{1}<p_{2}
and
\frac{q_{2}}{p_{2}^{\prime}}=\frac{q_{1}}{p_{1}^{\prime}}
, we have
[W]_{A_{p_{2},q_{2}},{\mathcal{D}}(I_{0})}=\infty
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we suppress from the notation dependence of implied constants on
p_{1}
and
q_{1}
.

For all positive integers
n
, consider the matrices
C_{n},D_{n}
from
Lemma

6.10
and set

A_{n}:=C_{n}^{-q_{1}/2},\quad B_{n}:=D_{n}^{q_{1}/2}.

Notice that
for all
1<p_{2}\leq q_{2}<\infty
with
p_{1}<p_{2}
and
\frac{q_{2}}{p_{2}^{\prime}}=\frac{q_{1}}{p_{1}^{\prime}}
we have

|A_{n}^{-1/q_{2}}B_{n}^{1/q_{2}}|=|C_{n}^{q_{1}/(2q_{2})}D_{n}^{q_{1}/(2q_{2})}|\simeq\tr(C_{n}^{q_{1}/q_{2}}D_{n}^{q_{1}/q_{2}})^{1/2},

for all
n=1,2,\ldots
, therefore since
\frac{q_{1}}{q_{2}}=\frac{p_{1}^{\prime}}{p_{2}^{\prime}}>1
,
Lemma

6.10
implies
\lim_{n\to\infty}|A_{n}^{-1/q_{2}}B_{n}^{1/q_{2}}|=\infty
.

Set now

W:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\mathbbm{1}_{(J_{n})_{-}}A_{n+1}+\mathbbm{1}_{(J_{n})_{+}}B_{n+1}),

where

J_{n}:=\left[\frac{1}{2^{n+1}},\frac{1}{2^{n}}\right),\quad n=0,1,2,\ldots,

and for all intervals
I=[a,b)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
we denote

I_{-}:=\left[a,\frac{a+b}{2}\right),\quad I_{+}:=\left[\frac{a+b}{2},b\right).

Notice that for all
1<p_{2}\leq q_{2}<\infty
with
p_{1}<p_{2}
and
\frac{q_{2}}{p_{2}^{\prime}}=\frac{q_{1}}{p_{1}^{\prime}}
we have

\displaystyle[W]_{A_{p_{2},q_{2}}(I_{0})}

\displaystyle\geq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{J_{n}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{J_{n}}|W(x)^{1/q_{2}}W(y)^{-1/q_{2}}|^{p_{2}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{q_{2}/p_{2}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\gtrsim_{q_{2},p_{2}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{(J_{n})_{+}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{(J_{n})_{-}}|W(x)^{1/q_{2}}W(y)^{-1/q_{2}}|^{p_{2}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{q_{2}/p_{2}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=|B_{n+1}^{1/q_{2}}A_{n+1}^{-1/q_{2}}|^{q_{2}}=|A_{n+1}^{-1/q_{2}}B_{n+1}^{1/q_{2}}|^{q_{2}},

for all
n=0,1,2,\ldots
, therefore
[W]_{A_{p_{2},q_{2}},{\mathcal{D}}(I_{0})}=\infty
.

It remains now to show that
[W]_{A_{p_{1},q_{1}}(I_{0})}<\infty
. We show a slightly stronger property, that is:

\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1,

for all subintervals
I
of
I_{0}
.

Let us first show that for all
k=0,1,2,\ldots
we have

\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I_{k}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I_{k}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1,

where

I_{k}:=\left[0,\frac{1}{2^{k}}\right),\quad k=0,1,2\ldots.

Let
k
be any nonnegative integer. Observe that
I_{k}=\bigcup_{i=k}^{\infty}J_{k}
. Thus, it suffices to prove that

\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak J_{i}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I_{k}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1,\quad\forall i=k,k+1,\ldots.

We show the following more general estimate, which is later useful for the proof of
Proposition

6.8
:

\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak J_{i}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I_{k}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\max\left(1,\frac{k+1}{i+1}\right)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2},\quad\forall i=0,1,2,\ldots.

Let
i
be any nonnegative integer. For all
x\in\nolinebreak(J_{i})_{-}
, we compute

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I_{k}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}2^{k-j}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{J_{j}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\simeq\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}2^{k-j}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{(J_{j})_{-}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\\
&\qquad\qquad+\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}2^{k-j}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{(J_{j})_{+}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\end{split}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}2^{k-j}|A_{i+1}^{1/q_{1}}A_{j+1}^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}+\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}2^{k-j}|A_{i+1}^{1/q_{1}}B_{j+1}^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}2^{k-j}\tr(C_{i+1}^{-1}C_{j+1})^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}+\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}2^{k-j}\tr(C_{i+1}^{-1}D_{j+1}^{-1})^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}.

A similar computation yields

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I_{k}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\simeq\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}2^{k-j}\tr(D_{i+1}C_{j+1})^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}+\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}2^{k-j}\tr(D_{i+1}D_{j+1}^{-1})^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2},

for all
x\in\nolinebreak(J_{i})_{+}
. Direct computation gives

\displaystyle\tr(C_{m}^{-1}C_{n})\simeq\max\left(1,\frac{n}{m}\right),\quad\tr(D_{m}C_{n})\simeq\max\left(1,\frac{n}{m}\right),

\displaystyle\tr(C_{m}^{-1}D_{n}^{-1})\simeq\max\left(1,\frac{n}{m}\right),\quad\tr(D_{m}D_{n}^{-1})\simeq\max\left(1,\frac{n}{m}\right)

for all
m,n=1,2,\ldots
. We distinguish two cases:

Case 1.

i\geq k
. Then, for all
x\in\nolinebreak J_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I_{k}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{j=k}^{i}2^{k-j}+\sum_{j=i+1}^{\infty}2^{k-j}\left(\frac{j+1}{i+1}\right)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}2^{k-j}\left(\frac{j+1}{k+1}\right)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}=1+\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}2^{k-j}\left(\frac{j-k}{k+1}+1\right)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}2^{k-j}((j-k)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}+1)\lesssim 1.

Case 2.

i<k
. Then, for all
x\in\nolinebreak J_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I_{k}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}2^{k-j}\left(\frac{j+1}{i+1}\right)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{(i+1)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}}\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}2^{k-j}((j-k)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}+(k+1)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2})

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{k+1}{i+1}\right)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}.

Let now
I
by any subinterval of
I_{0}
. Let
k
be the largest nonnegative integer with
I\subseteq I_{k}
. If
|I|\geq\frac{|I_{k}|}{512}
, then clearly

\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I_{k}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I_{k}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1.

Assume now that
|I|<\frac{|I_{k}|}{512}
. Then, at least one of the two must hold:

•

I\subseteq J_{k}=(J_{k})_{+}\cup(J_{k})_{-}

•

I\subseteq(J_{k+1})_{+}\cup(J_{k})_{-}
.

And since

\tr(C_{i}D_{j}),\penalty\ \tr(C_{i}^{-1}D_{j}^{-1}),\penalty\ \tr(C_{i}C_{j}^{-1}),\penalty\ \tr(D_{i}D_{j}^{-1})\simeq 1

for all
i,j\in\nolinebreak\{k+1,k+2\}
, it easily follows that

\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1,

concluding the proof.
∎

To extend the counterexample of
Lemma

6.11
to the whole real line, we use a standard trick that was also used in
[
Kak19
, Lemma 2.2]
.

Proof (of part (1) of
Proposition

6.8
).

Throughout the proof, we suppress from the notation dependence of implied constants on
p_{1}
and
q_{1}
.

Let
W
be the
2\times 2
matrix weight on
I_{0}
from the proof of
Lemma

6.11
. Consider the function
\widetilde{W}:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathrm{M}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
given by

\widetilde{W}(x):=w(x-k),\quad x\in\nolinebreak(k,k+1),\quad\text{if }k\text{ is even},

\widetilde{W}(x):=w(k+1-x),\quad x\in\nolinebreak(k,k+1),\quad\text{if }k\text{ is odd},

\widetilde{W}(k):=I\in\nolinebreak M_{2}({\mathbb{R}}),\quad k\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}.

It is obvious that
[\widetilde{W}]_{A_{p_{2},q_{2}},{\mathcal{D}}}=\infty
, for all
1<p_{2}\leq q_{2}<\infty
with
p_{1}<p_{2}
and
\frac{q_{2}}{p_{2}^{\prime}}=\frac{q_{1}}{p_{1}^{\prime}}
. It remains to show that
[\widetilde{W}]_{A_{p_{1},q_{1}}}<\infty
. We prove a slightly stronger fact, namely:

\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1,

for all intervals
I\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
.

First of all, let
m,n\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}
with
m<n
be arbitrary. We check that

\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak[m,n)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{[m,n)}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1.

Since
[m,n)=\bigcup_{k=m}^{n-1}[k,k+1)
, we may compute

\displaystyle\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak[m,n)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{[m,n)}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I_{0}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{[m,n)}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I_{0}}\sum_{k=m}^{n-1}\frac{1}{n-m}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{[k,k+1)}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I_{0}}\sum_{k=m}^{n-1}\frac{1}{n-m}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I_{0}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I_{0}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I_{0}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1.

Moreover, let
k,\ell
be arbitrary non-negative integers. We check that

\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak\left[-\frac{1}{2^{k}},\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\right)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\left[-\frac{1}{2^{k}},\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\right)}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1.

First of all, we have

\displaystyle\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak\left[-\frac{1}{2^{k}},0\right)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\left[-\frac{1}{2^{k}},\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\right)}

\displaystyle|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\leq\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak\left[-\frac{1}{2^{k}},0\right)}\frac{2^{-k}}{2^{-k}+2^{-\ell}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\left[-\frac{1}{2^{k}},0\right)}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\\
&\qquad\qquad+\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak\left[-\frac{1}{2^{k}},0\right)}\frac{2^{-\ell}}{2^{-k}+2^{-\ell}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\left[0,\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\right)}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I_{k}}\frac{2^{\ell}}{2^{k}+2^{\ell}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I_{k}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\\
&\qquad\qquad+\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I_{k}}\frac{2^{k}}{2^{k}+2^{\ell}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I_{\ell}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y.\end{split}

Since
I_{k}=\bigcup_{i=k}^{\infty}J_{i}
, by the proof of
Lemma

6.11
we deduce

\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I_{k}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I_{k}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1

and

\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I_{k}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I_{\ell}}|W(x)^{1/q_{1}}W(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\max\left(1,\frac{\ell+1}{k+1}\right)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak\left[-\frac{1}{2^{k}},0\right)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\left[-\frac{1}{2^{k}},\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\right)}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1+\frac{2^{k}}{2^{k}+2^{\ell}}\max\left(1,\frac{\ell+1}{k+1}\right)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}.

Analogously, we obtain

\displaystyle\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak\left[0,\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\right)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\left[-\frac{1}{2^{k}},\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\right)}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1+\frac{2^{\ell}}{2^{k}+2^{\ell}}\max\left(1,\frac{k+1}{\ell+1}\right)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}.

If
k<\ell
, then

\displaystyle\frac{2^{k}}{2^{k}+2^{\ell}}\left(\frac{\ell+1}{k+1}\right)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}\leq 2^{k-\ell}\left(\frac{\ell-k}{k+1}+1\right)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}\lesssim 2^{k-\ell}(\ell-k)^{p_{1}^{\prime}/2}\lesssim 1.

The case
\ell<k
is symmetric, thus we deduce the desired estimate.

Let now
I
be any interval in
{\mathbb{R}}
. We distinguish the following cases:

Case 1.

There exists
m\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}
with
I\subseteq[m,m+1)
. It is then immediate by
Lemma

6.11
that

\displaystyle\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1.

Case 2.

There does not exist
m\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}
with
I\subseteq[m,m+1)
, and
|I|\geq\frac{1}{2}
. Then, there exist
m,n\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}
with
m+1<n
such that
I\subseteq[m,n)
and
I\nsubseteq[m+1,n)
,
I\nsubseteq[m,n-1)
. It is clear that
|I|\simeq|[m,n)|
, thus

\displaystyle\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak[m,n)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{[m,n)}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1.

Case 3.

There does not exist
m\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}
with
I\subseteq[m,m+1)
, and
|I|<\frac{1}{2}
. Then, there exists
m\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{Z}}
with
I\subseteq[m,m+2)
and
m+1\in\nolinebreak\mathrm{Int}(I)
.

If
m+1
is odd, that is
m
is even, then it is clear that

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}(x)\in\nolinebreak\{A_{1},B_{1}\},\quad\text{for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak I,

therefore we trivially deduce

\displaystyle\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1.

If
m+1
is even, that is
m
is odd, then it is easy to see that there exist positive integers
k,\ell
with

I\subseteq J:=\left[m+1-\frac{1}{2^{k}},m+1+\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\right)

and
|I|\geq\frac{1}{2}|J|
, therefore

\displaystyle\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak I}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{I}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak J}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{J}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak\left[-\frac{1}{2^{k}},\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\right)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\left[-\frac{1}{2^{k}},\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\right)}|\widetilde{W}(x)^{1/q_{1}}\widetilde{W}(y)^{-1/q_{1}}|^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim 1,

concluding the proof.

∎

7.
Limited range extrapolation for matrix weights

In this section, we establish a limited-range extrapolation theorem for matrix weights. Although the proof is a straightforward adaptation of
[
BC22
, Theorem 9.1]
guided by
[
AM07
, Theorem 4.9]
, we carefully track all details. We restrict ourselves to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
equipped with the Euclidean metric and
n
-dimensional Lebesgue measure as underlying space. In particular, we simply write
\mathrm{d}x
for integration under the Lebesgue measure, and
|E|
for the
n
-dimensional Lebesgue measure of any Lebesgue-measurable subset
E
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

7.1.
Preliminaries

Here we briefly recall some important definitions and facts from
[
BC22
]
, where we refer for details and proofs.

7.1.1.
Integration of convex set valued maps

We consider a complete
\sigma
-finite measure space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{A}},\mu)
.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
be an
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable map. We say that this map is
\mu
-integrably bounded
, if there exists a
\mu
-integrable function
k:\Omega\to[0,\infty)
with
F(\omega)\subseteq k(\omega)\overline{\mathbf{B}}
, for all
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
.

Observe that an
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable map
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
is
\mu
-integrably bounded if and only if the function
|F|:\Omega\to[0,\infty)
is
\mu
-integrable.

Definition 7.2
.

Let
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
be a
\mu
-integrably bounded map. Following
[
BC22
, Definition 3.11]
, we define the
Aumann
\mu
-integral of
F
over
\Omega
,
\int_{\Omega}F(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)
, as the following subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
:

\int_{\Omega}F(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega):=\left\{\int_{\Omega}f(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)\,:\,f:\Omega\to{\mathbb{C}}^{d}\;\mu\text{-integrable selection function for }F\right\}.

Let
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
be a
\mu
-integrably bounded map. In
[
BC22
, Section 3]
it is proved that the Aumann integral
\int_{X}F(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)
is a convex body in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
. For all
A\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{A}}
, we denote

\int_{A}F(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega):=\int_{X}\mathbbm{1}_{A}(\omega)F(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)=\int_{A}F(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\mu|_{A}(\omega).

Remark 7.3
.

Let
F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
be a
\mu
-integrably bounded map. It is proved in
[
BC22
, Lemma 3.19]
that
\int_{X}F(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)=\{0\}
if and only if
F(\omega)=\{0\}
for
\mu
-a.e.
\omega\in\nolinebreak\Omega
.

7.1.2.
Convex body valued maximal function

Definition 7.4
.

(1)

For any measurable function
F:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
and for any
d\times d
matrix weight
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, we denote

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}:=\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/p}F(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/p},\quad 0<p<\infty,

and we also denote

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}:=\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)F(x)|.

Then, for any
0<p\leq\infty
, we denote by
L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
the family of all (Lebesgue-a.e. equivalence classes of) measurable functions
F:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
with
\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}<\infty
. Observe that if
F\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
for some
0<p\leq\infty
, then the subset
F(x)
of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
is bounded, for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

(2)

If
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
and
W,V
are
d\times d
matrix weights on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, then a map
T
is said to be
L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
-
L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(V)
bounded, if it is a map
T:L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)\to L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(V)
and there exists
0<C<\infty
such that

\lVert Tf\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(V)}\leq C\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)},

for all
f\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
; in this case, we denote by
\|T\|_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)\to L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(V)}
the best such constant. Moreover, we denote
\|T\|_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}:=\|T\|_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)\to L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}
.

(3)

A measurable map
F:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
is said to be
locally integrable
if the function
|F|:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to[0,\infty]
is locally integrable.

Remark 7.5
.

If
F\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
for some
1<p<\infty
and some
d\times d
matrix weight
W
such that
W^{1/(p-1)}
is also a
d\times d
matrix weight, then it is easy to see that
F
is locally integrable on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Definition 7.6
.

(1)

A map
T
acting from a space of
{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
-valued functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that is closed under the (pointwise) Minkowski sum and (pointwise) multiplication with complex numbers into a space of
{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
-valued functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is said to be
sublinear
, if for all
F,G
in the domain of definition of
T
and for all
a\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{C}}
we have

T(F+G)(x)\subseteq TF(x)+TG(x)

and

T(aF)(x)=aTF(x)=|a|TF(x),

for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

(2)

A map
T
acting from a space of
{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
-valued functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
into a space of
{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
-valued functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is said to be
monotone
if
TF(x)\subseteq TG(x)
for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, whenever
F,G
are in the domain of definition of
T
such that
F(x)\subseteq G(x)
for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Definition 7.7
.

If
F:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
is a locally integrable function, then following
[
BC22
]
we define its
convex-set-valued maximal function
by

{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}F(x):=\mathrm{clos}\bigg(\mathrm{conv}\bigg(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\text{ cube in }{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\\
x\in\nolinebreak Q\end{subarray}}\langle F\rangle_{Q}\bigg)\bigg)

where
\langle F\rangle_{Q}:=\frac{1}{|Q|}\int_{Q}F(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
. Observe that
{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}F(x)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{cs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. It is proved in
[
BC22
, Section 5]
that this gives rise to an operator
{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}
that is sublinear and monotone moreover satisfies
F(x)\subseteq{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}F(x)
, for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Remark 7.8
.

Let
F:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
be a locally integrable function that is not a.e. equal to
\{0\}
. Then, for all
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, there exists a cube
Q\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with
x\in\nolinebreak Q
such that
F
is not a.e. equal to
\{0\}
on
Q
, therefore by
Remark

7.3
we deduce
\langle F\rangle_{Q}\neq\{0\}
. It follows that
{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}F(x)\neq\{0\}
, for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

We need the following weighted bound for the maximal function
{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}
proved in
[
BC22
]
.

Theorem E

(
[
BC22
, Theorem 6.9]
)
.

Let
1<p<\infty
and let
W
be a
d\times d
matrix weight on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then,
{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}
acts boundedly on
L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
,
{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}:L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)\to L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
with

\lVert{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\lesssim_{d,n,p}[W]_{A_{p}}^{1/(p-1)}.

7.1.3.
Rubio de Francia algorithm for convex body valued functions

Definition 7.9
.

Let
W:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to\mathrm{M}_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
be a measurable function with values a.e. in the set of positive-definite
d\times d
complex-valued matrices. Following Bownik and Cruz-Uribe
[
BC22
]
, we define

[W]_{A_{1}}:=\sup_{Q}\esssup_{x\in\nolinebreak Q}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int|W^{-1}(x)W(y)|\,\mathrm{d}y,

where the supremum ranges over all cubes
Q\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. If
[W]_{A_{1}}<\infty
, then we say that
W
is a
d\times d
matrix
A_{1}
weight, in symbols
W\in\nolinebreak A_{1}
. Observe that
[W]_{A_{1}}=[W^{-1}]_{A_{\infty}}
.

Definition 7.10
.

Following
[
BC22
]
, we say that a locally integrable function
F:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
belongs to the
A_{1}^{{\mathcal{K}}}
class, in symbols
F\in\nolinebreak A^{{\mathcal{K}}}_{1}
, if there exists
0<C<\infty
such that

{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}F(x)\subseteq F(x),

for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
; in this case, we denote the infimum of all such constants
C
by
[F]_{A_{1}^{{\mathcal{K}}}}
.

In what follows, we denote by
\overline{\mathbf{B}}
the closed unit ball in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
. Bownik and Cruz-Uribe
[
BC22
, Corollary 7.4]
describe precisely the relation between the
A_{1}^{{\mathcal{K}}}
class and matrix
A_{1}
weights.

Lemma F

(
[
BC22
, Corollary 7.4]
)
.

If
W
is a
d\times d
matrix weight on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, then
W\overline{\mathbf{B}}\in\nolinebreak A_{1}^{{\mathcal{K}}}
if and only if
W\in\nolinebreak A_{1}
.

Bownik and Cruz-Uribe
[
BC22
, Theorem 7.6]
establish a Rubio de Francia-type algorithm to prove their extrapolation algorithm. This algorithm is of fundamental importance also for us, so we recall it below. We refer to
[
BC22
, Theorem 7.6]
for a detailed proof.

Theorem G

(
[
BC22
, Theorem 7.6]
)
.

Let
1\leq p\leq\infty
and let
W
be a
d\times d
matrix weight on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Let
T:L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)\to L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
be a bounded sublinear and monotone map. Given
G\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
, define
SG
by

SG:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{k}\lVert T\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{W}}}(K)}^{k}}T^{k}G,

where
T^{0}G:=G
and
T^{k}
denotes the
k
-fold composition of
T
with itself. Then, this series converges in
L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
. Moreover, it has the following properties:

(1)

G(x)\subseteq SG(x)
, for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

(2)

\lVert SG\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\leq 2\lVert G\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}

(3)

T(SG)(x)\subseteq 2\lVert T\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}SG(x)
, for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Definition 7.11
.

If
F:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textnormal{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
is a measurable function and
W:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to M_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
is a measurable function, then following
[
BC22
]
we way that
F
is
ellipsoid-valued with respect to
W
is there exists a function
r:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with
F(x)=r(x)W(x)
, for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Cruz-Uribe and Bownik
[
BC22
, Lemma 8.5]
show that their Rubio de Francia algorithm in
Theorem

G
behaves well for input functions that are ellipsoid-valued with respect to a given matrix function.

Lemma H

(
[
BC22
, Lemma 8.5]
)
.

Let
V:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to M_{d}({\mathbb{C}})
be a measurable function and
T
be an operator as in
Theorem

G
such that whenever
G\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
is ellipsoid-valued with respect to
V
, then
T(G)
is also ellipsoid-valued with respect to
V
. Then,
SG
is also ellipsoid-valued with respect to
V
, for all
G\in\nolinebreak L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
that are ellipsoid-valued with respect to
V
.

Finally, we need the following reverse Jones factorization type lemma for matrix weights
[
BC22
, Corollary 8.9]
, which is itself a special case of a much more general result
[
BC22
, Proposition 8.8]
.

Theorem I

(
[
BC22
, Corollary 8.9]
)
.

Let
1<p<\infty
, and let
W
be a
d\times d
matrix weight on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

(1)

Let
s:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to[0,\infty)
be a measurable function such that
W_{1}:=sW^{1/p}\in\nolinebreak A_{\infty}
. Then, for all
p_{0}\in\nolinebreak(p,\infty)
, we have

\overline{W}:=s^{p_{0}-p}W^{p_{0}/p}\in\nolinebreak A_{p_{0}},

with

[\overline{W}]_{A_{p_{0}}}\lesssim_{d,n,p,p_{0}}[W]_{A_{p}}[W_{1}]_{A_{\infty}}^{p_{0}-p}.

(2)

Let
r:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to[0,\infty)
be a measurable function such that
W_{0}:=rW^{1/p}\in\nolinebreak A_{1}
. Then, for all
p_{0}\in\nolinebreak(1,p)
, we have

\overline{W}:=r^{p_{0}-p^{\prime}(p_{0}-1)}W^{p_{0}/p}\in\nolinebreak A_{p_{0}},

with

[\overline{W}]_{A_{p_{0}}}\lesssim_{d,n,p,p_{0}}[W_{0}]_{A_{1}}^{p_{0}-p^{\prime}(p_{0}-1)}[W]_{A_{p}}^{(p_{0}-1)/(p-1)}.

7.2.
Statement of main result

In the rest of this section,
{\mathcal{F}}
denotes a non-empty family of pairs
(F,G)
of measurable functions
F,G:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{\textrm{bcs}}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})
such that neither
F
nor
G
is a.e. equal to
\{0\}
. Whenever we write an inequality of the form

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\leq C\lVert G\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)},\quad(F,G)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}}

we understand that the inequality holds for all pairs
(F,G)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}}
with
\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}<\infty
. The constant
C
depends on some concrete Muckenhoupt characteristic of some concrete power of the weight
W
, but not on the particular weight
W
itself.

We need some notation. Let
0<p_{0}<q_{0}\leq\infty
. For all
p\in\nolinebreak[p_{0},q_{0})
, we set

t(p):=\frac{p}{p_{0}}\geq 1,\quad s(p):=\frac{q_{0}}{p}\geq 1,\quad r(p):=s(p)^{\prime}(t(p)-1)+1\geq 1,

where the usual convention
\infty^{\prime}=1
is used; notice that
1<s(p)\leq\infty
, so
1\leq(s(p))^{\prime}<\infty
. Observe that for all
p,q\in\nolinebreak[p_{0},q_{0})
with
p<q
, we have
r(p)<r(q)
.

Theorem

1.6
.

Let
0<p_{0}<q_{0}\leq\infty
. Assume that for some fixed
p\in\nolinebreak[p_{0},q_{0}]
, there exists an increasing function
K_{p}
such that one of the following holds:

(1)

If
p<q_{0}
, then

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\leq K_{p}([W^{s(p)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(p)}})\lVert G\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)},\quad(F,G)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}},

for all
d\times d
matrix weights
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with
[W^{s(p)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(p)}}<\infty
.

(2)

If
p=q_{0}<\infty
, then

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\leq K_{p}([W^{t(p)^{\prime}/t(p)}]_{A_{\infty}})\lVert G\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)},\quad(F,G)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}},

for all
d\times d
matrix weights
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with
[W^{t(p)^{\prime}/t(p)}]_{A_{\infty}}<\infty
.

(3)

If
p=q_{0}=\infty
, then

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\leq K_{p}([W^{1/p_{0}}]_{A_{\infty}})\lVert G\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)},\quad(F,G)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}},

for all
d\times d
matrix weights
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with
[W^{1/p_{0}}]_{A_{\infty}}<\infty
.

Then, for all
q\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
,

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\leq K_{q}([W^{s(q)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(q)}})\lVert G\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)},\quad(F,G)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}}

holds, for all
d\times d
matrix weights
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with
[W^{s(q)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(q)}}<\infty
, where

K_{q}([W^{s(q)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(q)}})=c_{1}K_{p}(c_{2}[W^{s(q)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(q)}}^{\alpha(p,q)}),

for some constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
depending only on
n,d,p_{0},q_{0},p
and
q
, and the exponent
\alpha(p,q)
is given by

(7.1)

\alpha(p,q):=\begin{cases}\frac{r(p)-1}{r(q)-1},\text{ if }q<p<q_{0}\\
\\
\frac{1}{r(q)-1},\text{ if }q<p=q_{0}\\
\\
1,\text{ if }p\leq q\end{cases}.

Remark 7.12
.

Given the comparison results proved in
Section

6
, the conditions on the weights considered in the above theorem are reasonable. In particular, in the case
d=1
we recover Theorem
Theorem

C
. Moreover, in the case
p_{0}=1
and
q_{0}=\infty
we recover
[
BC22
, Theorem 9.1]
.

7.3.
Proof of main result

Here we prove
Theorem

1.6
in detail. We first need the following technical lemma.

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
p,q\in\nolinebreak[p_{0},q_{0})
. The following two identities are true:

(7.2)

s(p)^{\prime}s(q)^{\prime}(p-q)=ps(p)^{\prime}-qs(q)^{\prime}

and

(7.3)

\frac{r(p)}{r(q)}=\frac{ps(p)^{\prime}}{qs(q)^{\prime}}.

Proof.

If
q_{0}=\infty
, then
s(p)^{\prime}=s(q)^{\prime}=1
, so the first identity holds trivially, and the second says
\frac{t(p)}{t(q)}=\frac{p}{q}
, which also holds trivially because
t(p)=\frac{p}{p_{0}}
and
t(q)=\frac{q}{p_{0}}
.

Now we prove the identities under the assumption that
q_{0}<\infty
. For the first identity we have

s(p)^{\prime}=(q_{0}/p)^{\prime}=\frac{q_{0}}{q_{0}-p},\quad s(q)^{\prime}=\frac{q_{0}}{q_{0}-q},

so what we want to see becomes

\frac{q_{0}^{2}(p-q)}{(q_{0}-p)(q_{0}-q)}=\frac{pq_{0}}{q_{0}-p}-\frac{qq_{0}}{q_{0}-q}.

Computing

pq_{0}(q_{0}-q)-qq_{0}(q_{0}-p)=q_{0}^{2}p-pqq_{0}-qq_{0}^{2}+pqq_{0}=q_{0}^{2}(p-q),

we obtain the first identity.

The second identity can be equivalently rewritten as

\displaystyle qs(q)^{\prime}r(p)=ps(p)^{\prime}r(q),

that is

qs(q)^{\prime}[s(p)^{\prime}(t(p)-1)+1]=ps(p)^{\prime}[s(q)^{\prime}(t(q)-1)+1],

that is after expanding and rearranging

s(p)^{\prime}s(q)^{\prime}(qt(p)-q-pt(q)+p)=ps(p)^{\prime}-qs(q)^{\prime}.

We compute

qt(p)-q-pt(q)+p=\frac{qp}{p_{0}}-q-\frac{pq}{p_{0}}+p=p-q.

Thus, the second identity is equivalent to the first one, which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof (of
Theorem

1.6
).

We combine and adapt the proofs of
[
AM07
, Theorem 4.9]
and
[
BC22
, Theorem 9.1]
.

Let
q\in\nolinebreak(p_{0},q_{0})
,
W
be a
d\times d
matrix weight with
[W^{s(q)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(q)}}<\infty
, and
(F,G)\in\nolinebreak{\mathcal{F}}
with
0<\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}<\infty
. We may assume that
q\neq p
. We may also assume that
0<\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}<\infty
, otherwise the inequality to show is trivial. Throughout the proof, we suppress the notation dependence of implied constants on
n,d,p_{0},q_{0},p
, and
q
. Moreover,
c,c_{1},c_{2}
are positive finite constants depending only on these parameters which might change from line to line.

We now distinguish several cases.

q<p<q_{0}
. We first need to define an associated Rubio de Francia operator. Thus, consider the following auxiliary operator
N_{W}
acting on functions
H\in\nolinebreak L^{r(q)}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(V)
:

N_{W}H(x):=|V(x)^{1/r(q)}H(x)|W(x)^{-1/r(q)}\overline{\mathbf{B}},\quad\text{for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

where
V:=W^{s(q)^{\prime}}
. For this operator we may compute

\displaystyle\lVert N_{W}H\rVert_{L^{r(q)}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}=\lVert H\rVert_{L^{r(q)}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(V)}.

It is easy to see that
N_{W}
is sublinear and monotone.

Furthermore, we claim that

H(x)\subseteq\Sigma(x)N_{W}H(x),\quad\text{for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

where
\Sigma:=W^{1/r(q)}\cdot V^{-1/r(q)}=W^{(1-s(q)^{\prime})/r(q)}
. Indeed, this inclusion is equivalent to

H(x)\subseteq|V(x)^{1/r(q)}H(x)|V(x)^{-1/r(q)}\overline{\mathbf{B}},\quad\text{for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

that is

V(x)^{1/r(q)}H(x)\subseteq|V(x)^{1/r(q)}H(x)|\overline{\mathbf{B}},\quad\text{for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

which is obvious.

Now we define the operator
P_{W}
acting on functions
H\in\nolinebreak L^{r(q)}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
by

P_{W}H(x):=N_{W}({\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}(\Sigma H))(x)=|V(x)^{1/r(q)}{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}(\Sigma H)(x)|W(x)^{-1/r(q)}\overline{\mathbf{B}},\quad\text{for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

Then, using
Theorem

E
we can compute

\displaystyle\lVert P_{W}H\rVert_{L^{r(q)}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}=\lVert{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}(\Sigma H)\rVert_{L^{r(q)}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(V)}\lesssim[V]_{A_{r(q)}}^{1/(r(q)-1)}\lVert\Sigma H\rVert_{L^{r(q)}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(V)}=[V]_{A_{r(q)}}^{1/(r(q)-1)}\lVert H\rVert_{L^{r(q)}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}.

Since
{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}
is itself sublinear and monotone, it is easy to see that
P_{W}
is sublinear and monotone. (Although we do not need it, we note that it is easy to see that
H(x)\subseteq P_{W}H(x)
, for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.) Note also that if
H
is not a.e. equal to
\{0\}
, then
P_{W}H(x)\neq\{0\}
, for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

All assumptions of
Theorem

G
are fulfilled, thus we can define the following Rubio de Francia type operator acting on functions
H\in\nolinebreak L^{r(q)}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
:

{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{k}\lVert P_{W}\rVert_{L^{r(q)}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}^{k}}(P_{W})^{k}H.

We record the following properties of
{\mathcal{R}}_{W}
that we obtain from
Theorem

G
:

H(x)\subseteq{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H(x),

for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,

\lVert{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H\rVert_{L^{r(q)}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\leq 2\lVert H\rVert_{L^{r(q)}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}

and

P_{W}({\mathcal{R}}_{W}H)(x)\subseteq\lVert P_{W}\rVert_{L^{r(q)}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H(x)\subseteq c[V]_{A_{r(q)}}^{1/(r(q)-1)}{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H(x),

for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. In particular, we have

{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}(\Sigma{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H)(x)\subseteq\Sigma(x)P_{W}({\mathcal{R}}_{W}H)(x)\subseteq c[V]_{A_{r(q)}}^{1/(r(q)-1)}\Sigma(x){\mathcal{R}}_{W}H(x),

for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. This means that if
H
is not a.e. equal to
\{0\}
, then the convex body valued function
\Sigma{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H
belongs to the
A^{1}_{{\mathcal{K}}}
class with
[\Sigma{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H]_{A^{1}_{{\mathcal{K}}}}\leq c[V]_{A_{r(q)}}^{1/(r(q)-1)}
.

Finally, notice that
P_{W}H
is always ellipsoid-valued with respect to the matrix valued function
W^{-1/r(q)}
, therefore, by
Lemma

H
, we deduce that if
H
happens to be ellipsoid-valued with respect to
W^{-1/r(q)}
, then
{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H
is also ellipsoid-valued with respect to
W^{-1/r(q)}
.

We now define the scalar-valued function

\bar{h}(x):=\left(\frac{|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|}{\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}}+\frac{|W(x)^{1/q}G(x)|}{\lVert G\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}}\right)^{q/r(q)},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

It is obvious that

\lVert\bar{h}\rVert_{L^{r(q)}}\lesssim 1.

We consider the convex body valued function

\bar{H}(x):=\bar{h}(x)W(x)^{-1/r(q)}\overline{\mathbf{B}},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

It is clear that
\lVert H\rVert_{L^{r(q)}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\leq 2
. Since
\bar{H}
is ellipsoid-valued with respect to
W^{-1/r(q)}
, we deduce that
{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{H}
is also ellipsoid-valued with respect to
W^{-1/r(q)}
, therefore we can write
{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{H}(x)={\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h}(x)W(x)^{-1/r(q)}\overline{\mathbf{B}}
for some nonnegative scalar-valued function
{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h}
. Then, we have

\bar{h}(x)W(x)^{-1/r(q)}\overline{\mathbf{B}}\subseteq{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h}(x)W(x)^{-1/r(q)}\overline{\mathbf{B}},

which implies immediately that
\bar{h}(x)\leq{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h}(x)
, for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We now define the nonnegative scalar-valued function

h:=({\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h})^{r(q)/q}.

Since
\bar{H}
is not a.e. equal to
\{0\}
, we deduce that
h(x)\neq 0
, for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We compute

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/q}=\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|h(x)^{-1/(p/q)^{\prime}}W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\cdot h(x)^{q/(p/q)^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|h(x)^{-1/(p/q)^{\prime}}W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/p}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}h(x)^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/(q(p/q)^{\prime})}.

It is of course the case that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}h(x)^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}({\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h}(x))^{r(q)}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/r(q)}\bar{H}(x)|^{r(q)}\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim 1.

Now let us deal with the first integral. Set

T(x):=h(x)^{-p/(p/q)^{\prime}}W(x)^{p/q},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

What we want to check is that

T^{s(p)^{\prime}}\in\nolinebreak A_{r(p)}.

By above we have

\displaystyle\Sigma{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{H}\in\nolinebreak A^{1}_{{\mathcal{K}}},

that means concretely

({\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h})V^{-1/r(q)}\overline{\mathbf{B}}\in\nolinebreak A^{1}_{{\mathcal{K}}}.

Thus, by
Lemma

F
we have
{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h}V^{-1/r(q)}\in\nolinebreak A_{1}
, therefore,

({\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h})^{-1}V^{1/r(q)}\in\nolinebreak A_{\infty},

that is

h^{-q/r(q)}W^{s(q)^{\prime}/r(q)}\in\nolinebreak A_{\infty}.

Set

m(x):=h(x)^{-q/r(q)},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

Then

mW^{s(q)^{\prime}/r(q)}\in\nolinebreak A_{\infty}\quad\text{and}\quad W^{s(q)^{\prime}}\in\nolinebreak A_{r(q)}.

The fact that
p>q
easily implies
r(p)>r(q)
. Thus, by
Theorem

I
(1), we deduce

m^{r(p)-r(q)}(W^{s(q)^{\prime}})^{r(p)/r(q)}\in\nolinebreak A_{r(p)}.

Our goal now is to check that

m^{r(p)-r(q)}(W^{s(q)^{\prime}})^{r(p)/r(q)}=T^{s(p)^{\prime}},

that is

h^{-q(r(p)-r(q))/r(q)}W^{s(q)^{\prime}r(p)/r(q)}=h^{-s(p)^{\prime}p/(p/q)^{\prime}}W^{s(p)^{\prime}p/q}.

We first check that the exponents of
h
agree:

\displaystyle-\frac{q(r(p)-r(q))}{r(q)}=-\frac{qr(p)}{r(q)}+q\overset{\eqref{second identity}}{=}-\frac{qps(p)^{\prime}}{qs(q)^{\prime}}+q=-\frac{ps(p)^{\prime}}{s(q)^{\prime}}+q=-\frac{ps(p)^{\prime}-qs(q)^{\prime}}{s(q)^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{first identity}}{=}-s(p)^{\prime}(p-q)=-\frac{s(p)^{\prime}p}{\frac{p}{p-q}}=-\frac{s(p)^{\prime}p}{(p/q)^{\prime}}.

We also check that the exponents of
W
agree:

\displaystyle\frac{s(q)^{\prime}r(p)}{r(q)}\overset{\eqref{second identity}}{=}\frac{ps(p)^{\prime}}{q}=\frac{s(p)^{\prime}p}{q}.

Thus, we can estimate

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/q}\leq c\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|T(x)^{1/p}F(x)|^{p}\right)^{1/p}.

We now observe that

h(x)=({\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h}(x))^{r/q}\geq\bar{h}(x)^{r/q}\geq\frac{|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|}{\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

thus

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|T(x)^{1/p}f(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}h(x)^{-p/(p/q)^{\prime}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}h(x)^{q-p}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}^{q-p}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x=\|F\|_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}^{p}<\infty.

Therefore, we are entitled to apply the extrapolation hypothesis, obtaining

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|T(x)^{1/p}F(x)|^{p}\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}K_{p}([T^{s(p)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(p)}})\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|T(x)^{1/p}G(x)|^{p}\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}K_{p}(c_{2}[V]_{A_{r(q)}}^{\alpha(p,q)})\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|T(x)^{1/p}G(x)|^{p}\right)^{1/p},

since

[T^{s(p)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(p)}}\leq c[W^{s(q)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(q)}}[s(x)W^{s(q)^{\prime}/r(q)}]_{A_{\infty}}^{r(p)-r(q)}\leq c[V]_{A_{r(q)}}[V]_{A_{r(q)}}^{(r(p)-r(q))/(r(q)-1)}=c[V]_{A_{r(q)}}^{\alpha(p,q)}.

Similarly to above, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|T(x)^{1/p}G(x)|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\leq\lVert G\rVert_{L_{{\mathcal{K}}}^{q}(W)}.

Thus

\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/q}\leq c_{1}K_{p}(c_{2}[V]_{A_{r(q)}}^{\alpha(p,q)})\lVert G\rVert_{L_{{\mathcal{K}}}^{q}(W)},

concluding the proof in this case.

q<p=q_{0}<\infty
. Similarly to Case 1, we construct a nonnegative scalar-valued function
h
with the following properties:

h(x)\geq\frac{|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|}{\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}}+\frac{|W(x)^{1/q}G(x)|}{\lVert G\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

h(x)>0,\quad\text{for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}h(x)^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim 1,

and

h^{q/r(q)}W^{-s(q)^{\prime}/r(q)}\in\nolinebreak A_{1}.

As in case 1 we have

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\lesssim\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(T)},\quad\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(T)}\leq\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\quad\text{ and }\quad\lVert G\rVert_{L^{p}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(T)}\leq\lVert G\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)},

where

T(x):=h(x)^{-p/(p/q)^{\prime}}W(x)^{p/q},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

Notice that

B:=h^{-q/r(q)}W^{s(q)^{\prime}/r(q)}\in\nolinebreak A_{\infty}.

We check now that

T=B^{s(p_{0})-1},

that is

h^{-p/(p/q)^{\prime}}W^{p/q}=h^{-q(s(p_{0})-1)/r(q)}W^{s(q)^{\prime}(s(p_{0})-1)/r(q)}.

We first check that the exponents of
h
agree. We need to show that

-q_{0}/(q_{0}/q)^{\prime}=-\frac{q}{r(q)}\left(\frac{q_{0}}{p_{0}}-1\right),

that is that

q_{0}-q=\frac{q(q_{0}-p_{0})}{p_{0}r(q)}.

We compute

\displaystyle p_{0}r(q)=s(q)^{\prime}(q-p_{0})+p_{0}=\frac{q_{0}(q-p_{0})}{q_{0}-q}+p_{0}=\frac{q_{0}(q-p_{0})+p_{0}(q_{0}-q)}{q_{0}-q}=\frac{q(q_{0}-p_{0})}{q_{0}-q},

yielding the desired equality. Moreover, we check that the exponents of
W
agree. We have

\displaystyle\frac{s(q)^{\prime}(s(p_{0})-1)}{r(q)}=\frac{q_{0}(q_{0}-p_{0})}{p_{0}r(q)}=\frac{q_{0}}{q}=\frac{p}{q}.

Thus

T^{t(q_{0})^{\prime}/t(q_{0})}=T^{s(p_{0})^{\prime}-1}=B\in\nolinebreak A_{\infty}.

The rest of the proof proceeds exactly as in case 1.

q<p=q_{0}=\infty
. Similarly to Case 1, we construct a nonnegative scalar-valued function
h
with the following properties:

h(x)\geq\frac{|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|}{\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}}+\frac{|W(x)^{1/q}G(x)|}{\lVert G\rVert_{L^{q}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

h(x)>0,\quad\text{for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}h(x)^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim 1,

and

h^{q/r(q)}W^{-s(q)^{\prime}/r(q)}\in\nolinebreak A_{1}.

We compute

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/q}=\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|h(x)^{-1/(p/q)^{\prime}}W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\cdot h(x)^{q/(p/q)^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert F\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(T)}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}h(x)^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/(q(p/q)^{\prime})}\lesssim\lVert F\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(T)},

where

T(x):=h(x)^{-1/(p/q)^{\prime}}W(x)^{1/q},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

Notice that

B:=h^{-q/r(q)}W^{s(q)^{\prime}/r(q)}\in\nolinebreak A_{\infty}.

So, we only have to check that
T^{1/p_{0}}=B
. We check that the exponents of
h
agree:

-q/(p_{0}r(q))=-q/(p_{0}t(q))=-q/q=-1,\quad-1/(p/q)^{\prime}=-1/1=-1.

We also check that the exponents of
W
agree:

s(q)^{\prime}/r(q)=1/t(q)=p/q_{0}.

Thus
T^{1/p_{0}}=B\in\nolinebreak A_{\infty}
. The rest of the proof proceeds exactly as in case 1.

p_{0}\leq p<q
. We first need to define an associated Rubio de Francia operator. So consider first the following auxiliary operator acting on convex-body valued functions
H\in\nolinebreak L^{r(q)^{\prime}}(U)
:

N_{W}H(x):=|U(x)^{1/r(q)^{\prime}}H(x)|W(x)^{-1/r(q)^{\prime}}\overline{\mathbf{B}},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

where
U:=W^{-1/(t(q)-1)}
. For this operator, we may compute

\displaystyle\lVert N_{W}H\rVert_{L^{r(q)^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}=\lVert H\rVert_{L^{r(q)^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(U)}.

It is easy to see that this map is sublinear and monotone and

H(x)\subseteq\Sigma(x)N_{W}H(x),\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

where
\Sigma:=W^{1/r(q)^{\prime}}\cdot U^{-1/r(q)^{\prime}}=W^{t(q)^{\prime}/r(q)^{\prime}}
.

Now we define the operator acting on functions
H\in\nolinebreak L^{r(q)^{\prime}}(W)

P_{W}H(x):=N_{W}({\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}(\Sigma H))(x)=|U(x)^{1/r(q)^{\prime}}{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}(\Sigma H)(x)|W(x)^{-1/r(q)^{\prime}}\overline{\mathbf{B}},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

Since
{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}
is sublinear and monotone, one can easily observe that
P_{W}
is sublinear and monotone. Moreover, using
Theorem

E
we can compute

\displaystyle\lVert P_{W}H\rVert_{L^{r(q)^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}=\lVert{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}(\Sigma H)\rVert_{L^{r(q)^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(U)}\lesssim[U]_{A_{r(q)^{\prime}}}^{1/(r(q)^{\prime}-1)}\lVert\Sigma H\rVert_{L^{r(q)^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(U)}=[U]_{A_{r(q)^{\prime}}}^{1/(r(q)^{\prime}-1)}\lVert H\rVert_{L^{r(q)^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}.

(Although we do not need it, we note that it is easy to see that
H(x)\subseteq P_{W}H(x)
, for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.) Note also that if
H
is not a.e. equal to
\{0\}
, then
P_{W}H(x)\neq\{0\}
, for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Thus, all assumptions of
Theorem

G
are fulfilled, therefore we can define the following Rubio de Francia type operator acting on functions
H\in\nolinebreak L^{r(q)^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)
:

{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{k}\lVert P_{W}\rVert_{L^{r(q)^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}^{k}}(P_{W})^{k}H.

We record the following properties of
{\mathcal{R}}_{W}
that we obtain from
Theorem

G
:

H(x)\subseteq{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H(x),\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

\lVert{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H\rVert_{L^{r(q)^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}\leq 2\lVert H\rVert_{L^{r(q)^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}

and

P_{W}({\mathcal{R}}_{W}H)(x)\subseteq\lVert P_{W}\rVert_{L^{r(q)^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H(x)\subseteq c[U]_{A_{r(q)^{\prime}}}^{1/(r(q)^{\prime}-1)}{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H(x),\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

In particular, we have

{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{K}}}(\Sigma{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H)(x)\subseteq\Sigma(x)P_{W}({\mathcal{R}}_{W}H)(x)\subseteq c[U]_{A_{r^{\prime}}}^{1/(r^{\prime}-1)}\Sigma(x){\mathcal{R}}_{W}H(x),\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

This means that if
H
is not a.e. equal to
\{0\}
, then the convex body valued function
\Sigma{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H
belongs to the
A^{1}_{{\mathcal{K}}}
class with
[\Sigma{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H]_{A^{1}_{{\mathcal{K}}}}\leq c[U]_{A_{r(q)^{\prime}}}^{1/(r(q)^{\prime}-1)}
.

Finally, notice that
P_{W}H
is always ellipsoid-valued with respect to
W^{-1/r(q)^{\prime}}
, therefore by
Lemma

H
we deduce that if
H
happens to be ellipsoid-valued with respect to
W^{-1/r(q)^{\prime}}
, then
{\mathcal{R}}_{W}H
is also ellipsoid-valued with respect to
W^{-1/r(q)^{\prime}}
.

We now observe that the Riesz representation theorem yields that there exists a nonnegative scalar-valued function
h_{1}\in\nolinebreak L^{(q/p)^{\prime}}
with
\lVert h_{1}\rVert_{L^{(q/p)^{\prime}}}=1
such that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{p})^{q/p}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{p}h_{1}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Now we define the nonnegative scalar-valued function

\bar{h}(x):=h_{1}(x)^{(q/p)^{\prime}/r(q)^{\prime}},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

and the convex body valued function

\bar{H}(x):=\bar{h}(x)W(x)^{-1/r(q)^{\prime}}\overline{\mathbf{B}},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

It is clear that
\lVert H\rVert_{L^{r(q)^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(W)}=1
. Since
\bar{H}
is ellipsoid-valued with respect to
W^{-1/r(q)^{\prime}}
, we deduce that
{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{H}
is also ellipsoid-valued with respect to
W^{-1/r(q)^{\prime}}
, therefore we can write
{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{H}(x)={\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h}(x)W(x)^{-1/r(q)^{\prime}}\overline{\mathbf{B}}
for some nonnegative scalar-valued function
{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h}
. Then, we have

\bar{h}(x)W(x)^{-1/r(q)^{\prime}}\overline{\mathbf{B}}\subseteq{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h}(x)W(x)^{-1/r(q)^{\prime}}\overline{\mathbf{B}},

which implies immediately that
\bar{h}(x)\leq{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h}(x)
, for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We now define the nonnegative scalar-valued function

h:=({\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h})^{r(q)^{\prime}/(q/p)^{\prime}}.

Since
\bar{H}
is not a.e. equal to
\{0\}
, we deduce that
h(x)\neq 0
, for a.e.
x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Observe that
h\geq h_{1}
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{p}h(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|h(x)^{1/p}W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Set

T(x):=h(x)W(x)^{p/q},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

Our goal is to show that

T^{s(p)^{\prime}}\in\nolinebreak A_{r(p)}.

By above, we have

\displaystyle\Sigma{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{H}\in\nolinebreak A^{1}_{{\mathcal{K}}},

that means concretely

({\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h})U^{-1/r(q)^{\prime}}\overline{\mathbf{B}}\in\nolinebreak A^{1}_{{\mathcal{K}}},

that is

({\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h})W^{s(q)^{\prime}/r(q)}\overline{\mathbf{B}}\in\nolinebreak A^{1}_{{\mathcal{K}}}.

Thus, by
Lemma

F
we have
({\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h})V^{1/r(q)}\in\nolinebreak A_{1}
, that is

h^{(q/p)^{\prime}/r(q)^{\prime}}W^{s(q)^{\prime}/r(q)}\in\nolinebreak A_{1}.

Set

m(x):=h(x)^{(q/p)^{\prime}/r(q)^{\prime}},\quad\text{ for a.e. }x\in\nolinebreak{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

Then

mW^{s(q)^{\prime}/r(q)}\in\nolinebreak A_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad W^{s(q)^{\prime}}\in\nolinebreak A_{r(q)}.

The fact that
p<q
easily implies
r(p)<r(q)
. Thus, by
Theorem

I
(2), we deduce

m^{r(p)-r(q)^{\prime}(r(p)-1)}(W^{s(q)^{\prime}})^{r(p)/r(q)}\in\nolinebreak A_{r(p)},

both for
p>p_{0}
and
p=p_{0}
.

Our goal now is to check that

m^{r(p)-r(q)^{\prime}(r(p)-1)}(W^{s(q)^{\prime}})^{r(p)/r(q)}=T^{s(p)^{\prime}},

that is

h^{(q/p)^{\prime}[r(p)-r(q)^{\prime}(r(p)-1)]/r(q)^{\prime}}W^{s(q)^{\prime}r(p)/r(q)}=h^{s(p)^{\prime}}W^{s(p)^{\prime}p/q}.

We first check that the exponents of
h
agree:

\displaystyle(q/p)^{\prime}\frac{r(p)-r(q)^{\prime}(r(p)-1)}{r(q)^{\prime}}=(q/p)^{\prime}\left[\frac{r(p)}{r(q)^{\prime}}-r(p)+1\right]=(q/p)^{\prime}\left[r(p)\left(\frac{1}{r(q)^{\prime}}-1\right)+1\right]

\displaystyle=(q/p)^{\prime}\left(-\frac{r(p)}{r(q)}-1\right)\overset{\eqref{second identity}}{=}(q/p)^{\prime}\left(-\frac{ps(p)^{\prime}}{qs(q)^{\prime}}-1\right)=(q/p)^{\prime}\frac{qs(q)^{\prime}-ps(p)^{\prime}}{qs(q)^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{second identity}}{=}(q/p)^{\prime}\frac{s(p)^{\prime}s(q)^{\prime}(q-p)}{qs(q)^{\prime}}=\frac{q}{q-p}\cdot\frac{s(p)^{\prime}q-p}{q}=s(p)^{\prime}.

Moreover, we check that the exponents of
W
agree:

\displaystyle s(p)^{\prime}\frac{r(p)}{r(q)}\overset{\eqref{second identity}}{=}s(p)^{\prime}\frac{p}{q}.

Therefore, we have

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|T(x)^{1/p}F(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x.

We also observe that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|T(x)^{1/p}F(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{p}h(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{p/q}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}h(x)^{(q/p)^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/(q/p)^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{p/q}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h}(x)^{r(q)^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/(q/p)^{\prime}}.

Notice that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{h}(x)^{r(q)^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/r(q)^{\prime}}{\mathcal{R}}_{W}\bar{H}(x)|^{r(q)^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq c.

It follows that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|T(x)^{1/p}F(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq c\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{p/q}<\infty.

Thus, we are entitled to apply the extrapolation hypothesis, obtaining

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}F(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/q}\leq c\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|T(x)^{1/p}F(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/p}\leq cK_{p}([T^{s(p)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(p)}})

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}K_{p}(c_{2}[V]_{A_{r(p)}}^{\alpha(p,q)})\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|T(x)^{1/p}G(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/p},

because

\displaystyle[T^{s(p)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(p)}}\leq c[W^{s(q)^{\prime}}]_{A_{r(q)}}^{(r(p)-1)/(r(q)-1)}[r(x)W(x)^{s(q)^{\prime}/r(q)}]_{A_{1}}^{r(p)-r(q)^{\prime}(r(p)-1)}

\displaystyle\leq c[V]_{A_{r(q)}}^{(r(p)-1)/(r(q)-1)}[U]_{A_{r(q)^{\prime}}}^{[r(p)-r(q)^{\prime}(r(p)-1)]/(r(q)^{\prime}-1)}

\displaystyle\simeq c[V]_{A_{r(q)}}^{(r(p)-1)/(r(q)-1)}[V]_{A_{r(q)}}^{r(p)-r(q)^{\prime}(r(p)-1)}\leq c_{2}[V]_{A_{r(q)}}^{\alpha(p,q)}.

Similarly to the above, we obtain

\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|T(x)^{1/p}G(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{q}\leq c\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|W(x)^{1/q}G(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/q}.
